# Supplement Evidence Audit — Full Compound Dossiers

*Compiled 2026-08-04. 121 compounds across 12 outcome categories. Every grade re-derived from primary trials; 50 grades adversarially re-checked. See `README.md` for the ranked map and the four decision lists, and `data.json` for the machine-readable dataset.*

Each dossier reports what the rubric demands: trial count and total n, the largest independent trial, effect size **with confidence interval**, whether it clears the MCID, **trial dose vs typical retail active dose**, the studied population and its transfer to a healthy adult, funding-source split, outlier leverage, regulatory advisories, an explicit **confidence in the score**, and — where relevant — the adversarial re-check, the movement from the prior pass, and the non-supplement intervention that actually works.

> **Reading the score:** it grades the *quality and independence of the human-outcome evidence*, not how much a vendor markets it. A 2 is the default resting state of this market: a real mechanism and a plausible meta-analysis, with an effect that doesn't clear the MCID, hinges on industry funding or one outlier trial, or vanishes outside a deficient/diseased population.


---

## Mind / memory

### Creatine monohydrate — **4/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** The best-evidenced item in this category, but the prior grade of 5 overstates it for the marketed use. Worth buying if you are vegetarian, older, or acutely sleep-deprived; the general healthy-rested-adult memory benefit is small. Cheap, safe, well-characterized.

- **Evidence base:** 16 RCTs (2024 meta) plus acute MRS trial — total n ≈ ~492 across 16 RCTs (Prokopidis Frontiers Nutr 2024 meta); acute sleep-dep trial n=15
- **Largest independent trial:** No single large independent healthy-adult cognition RCT; evidence rests on the pooled 16-trial meta (Prokopidis, Frontiers in Nutrition 2024) and the n=15 MRS sleep-dep trial.
- **Effect size (CI):** Memory SMD ~0.3 (GRADE moderate) in 2024 meta; processing-speed/attention low certainty. Acute trial: improved PVT and processing speed during 21 h sleep deprivation, effect from ~3.5 h, peak ~4 h.
- **Clears MCID?** Borderline. Meaningful under metabolic stress (sleep loss, vegetarian repletion); marginal in rested omnivores.
- **Dose — trials:** Chronic cognition trials 5-20 g/day; acute sleep-deprivation protocol 0.35 g/kg single dose · **retail (active):** 3-5 g/day creatine monohydrate (fully active, no elemental discount)
- **Population studied:** Mixed: healthy young adults, vegetarians, older adults, and sleep-deprived young men. Largest cognitive effects in metabolically stressed or creatine-depleted subgroups. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Partial. Benefit largest when muscle/brain creatine is low; rested well-fed omnivore gains little.
- **Funding split:** Not cleanly reported in the 2024 meta; effect not obviously funding-dependent. · **Outlier leverage:** Small total n is the main fragility; not driven by one extreme study.
- **Regulatory / safety:** EFSA 2024 evaluated a creatine + cognitive-function health claim (Article 13(5)); not established/authorized. No safety advisory, strong safety record.
- **Moved from prior:** Section-3 5 → 4 **(≥1-point move — interesting case)** — Dropped 5 to 4. The '16-RCT meta, GRADE moderate' is real but the pooled base is only ~492 subjects, memory SMD is modest, and the largest effects concentrate in creatine-DEPLETED states (vegetarians, older adults, sleep deprivation). The 2024 sleep-deprivation result (Gordji-Nejad, Sci Rep 2024) used a single 0.35 g/kg dose (~25 g for a 70 kg adult, n=15), not the 3-5 g/day retail regimen. As a memory supplement for a healthy, rested, omnivorous adult it is a 4, not a 'would bet on it' 5.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 5; decisive check: Check 3 (effect size vs MCID) — the only real point of attack — held; the confirming pillars were Check 4 (dose match) and Check 5 (population transfer)). I attacked this as the trap-prone checks (2,3,4,5,7) but none broke it. The strongest available attack is Check 3: the pooled effect is genuinely modest (Branch 2003 ES ~0.24; some of the +1.1 kg "lean mass" is intracellular water rather than contractile tissue). But this does not sink the grade — Delpino 2022 LBM MD +1.10 kg (95% CI 0.56-1.65, verified) excludes zero, strength gains are real, and the effect is distributed across >100 RCTs, so there is no outlier leverage (Check 2 fails to break it). Unlike the rubric's cautionary examples, every disqualifying condition is absent: retail 5 g/day matches trial maintenance dose exactly (Check 4, ISSN-confirmed), so retail is not below trial; the benefit was demonstrated directly in healthy young and older adults doing resistance training, not inferred from a deficiency/MCI/disease population (Check 5); creatine is generic and off-patent with a heavily university-funded base, so industry funding is not load-bearing (Check 1, not compromised); dose-response is not adverse — up to 30 g/day for 5 years is well tolerated (Check 6); and there is no TGA/FDA/EMA/MHRA advisory, with EFSA in fact granting a positive muscle-function claim (Check 7). The grade survives at 5; a defensible 4.5 floor exists purely on the modest per-person effect size, but the evidence quality is the best of any sports supplement.
- **Conditional on:** Clearest benefit conditional on creatine depletion: vegetarian/vegan diet, age, or acute sleep deprivation. Acute cognitive rescue needs ~0.35 g/kg, far above the 3-5 g/day maintenance dose.
- **Product:** Thorne Thorne Creatine — 5 g creatine monohydrate per scoop (fully active; no elemental discount) active/serving; NSF Certified for Sport. Best-in-class pick: uses Creapure (the branded form used in most cognition/creatine trials) and is NSF Certified for Sport. 5 g/scoop meets the standard chronic cognition dose. Unflavored powder. ASIN varies by listing; search URL is reliable.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Thorne+Creatine+NSF+Certified+for+Sport
- **Product:** Thorne Thorne Creatine (Creapure) — 5 g creatine monohydrate per scoop active/serving; NSF Certified for Sport. Best-in-class pick: branded Creapure creatine monohydrate AND NSF Certified for Sport (strongest anti-adulteration mark). 5 g/scoop fully covers the 3-5 g maintenance/muscle-energy dose and the low end of cognition dosing; sits at the LOW end of the 5-20 g cognition range and below the ~14-18 g single-dose sleep-deprivation protocol (would need 3-4 scoops). Alternative also NSF: Klean Athlete Klean Creatine (also Creapure).  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Thorne+Creatine+Creapure+NSF+Certified+for+Sport
- **Primary sources:** Prokopidis K, et al. Frontiers in Nutrition 2024;11:1424972 (16-RCT meta, PMC11275561); Gordji-Nejad A, et al. Scientific Reports 2024;14:4937, doi:10.1038/s41598-024-54249-9; Xu C, et al. Nutrients 2026;18:1192 (single-dose creatine, sleep deprivation); EFSA Journal 2024;22:e9100 (creatine + cognition health-claim evaluation)

### B-vitamins (B12/folate/B6, homocysteine-lowering) - added, in-category — **3/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Conditional buy only. In healthy replete adults, large trials are null; benefit appears in a specific subgroup, elevated homocysteine AND adequate omega-3 status. This is a lab-value-gated intervention, not a general memory pill.

- **Evidence base:** 1 pivotal targeted RCT (VITACOG) + several null unselected RCTs — total n ≈ VITACOG n=271; multiple larger unselected RCTs null
- **Largest independent trial:** VITACOG (Smith AD, et al. PLoS ONE 2010;5:e12244; de Jager, Int J Geriatr Psychiatry 2012).
- **Effect size (CI):** VITACOG: reduced rate of brain atrophy (~30% overall; up to ~53% in the high-homocysteine subgroup); cognitive benefit concentrated where homocysteine elevated and omega-3 adequate.
- **Clears MCID?** In the targeted subgroup, plausibly; in unselected adults, no.
- **Dose — trials:** Folic acid 0.8 mg + B12 0.5 mg + B6 20 mg/day · **retail (active):** Comparable B-complex doses widely available (dose is not the limiting factor; patient selection is)
- **Population studied:** Older adults with MCI and elevated homocysteine (VITACOG); benefit modified by omega-3 status. Null in unselected/replete populations. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No, replete, normal-homocysteine adults show no benefit.
- **Funding split:** VITACOG academically funded; not the driver of concern. · **Outlier leverage:** Effect depends on a pre-specified subgroup; whole-sample effect weaker.
- **Regulatory / safety:** High-dose folic acid / B6 cautions exist (B6 neuropathy at chronic high doses); no memory claim authorized.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** The primary fix for the target group is correcting B12/folate deficiency and lowering homocysteine; in replete adults the effective levers are cardiovascular risk control, exercise, and sleep, not B-vitamin pills.
- **Conditional on:** Benefit gated on ELEVATED homocysteine and adequate omega-3 index; check labs before use.
- **Product:** Jarrow Formulas Jarrow Formulas B-Right — Per capsule: B6 (as P5P) ~25 mg; Folate ~400 mcg (as 5-MTHF); B12 ~100 mcg (as methylcobalamin) active/serving; none — ⚠ per-serving dose BELOW trial dose. Well-formulated methylated B-complex. Flagged dose_adequate=false on a strict per-serving basis (folate 400<800 mcg; B12 100<500 mcg), correctable with 2 caps/day. For an exact homocysteine-protocol match, 'Thorne Homocysteine Factors' or 'NOW Homocysteine Regulators' pair ~0.8-1 mg folate + B12 + B6 with betaine/TMG.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Jarrow+Formulas+B-Right
- **Primary sources:** Smith AD, et al. PLoS ONE 2010;5:e12244 (VITACOG, brain atrophy); de Jager CA, et al. Int J Geriatr Psychiatry 2012;27:592-600 (cognitive outcomes); Jerneren F, et al. Am J Clin Nutr 2015;102:215-221 (omega-3 interaction)

### Bacopa monnieri — **3/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Conditional buy at best. Real but modest signal, mainly on speed of attention / reaction time rather than memory, and it takes ~12 weeks. Use a standardized extract (55% bacosides / Bacognize) at 300 mg. GI side effects common. Prior of 3 holds.

- **Evidence base:** 9 RCTs (2014 meta); several 12-wk 300 mg trials since — total n ≈ 437 across 9 RCTs (Kongkeaw et al. 2014 meta)
- **Largest independent trial:** No single large independent trial; Stough 2001/2008 (CDRI-08, ~n=46-107) and Calabrese 2008 (n=54, 300 mg, 12 wk) are the anchors.
- **Effect size (CI):** Meta: significant improvement in Trail-Making/choice-RT and speed of attention; decreased reaction time. Memory (free recall) improvements less consistent.
- **Clears MCID?** Modest; attention/RT changes small-to-moderate, no formal MCID.
- **Dose — trials:** 300-450 mg/day standardized extract (~55% bacosides, or Bacognize/CDRI-08) · **retail (active):** 300-320 mg standardized extract (matches trials), but many non-standardized 'Bacopa 500 mg' whole-herb products deliver far less bacoside
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults, medical students, and older adults with age-associated memory impairment; 300 mg standardized extract, 12 wk. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Partial, some healthy-adult and student trials positive, but onset requires chronic (>=8-12 wk) dosing.
- **Funding split:** No clean funding-split meta; several trials use proprietary extracts (CDRI-08, Bacognize) with sponsor involvement. · **Outlier leverage:** Small trials; heterogeneous extracts limit pooling confidence.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No major regulator efficacy authorization; commonly reported GI adverse effects (nausea, cramping, diarrhea, increased stool frequency).
- **Conditional on:** Benefit conditional on a standardized bacoside extract and >=12 weeks of use; whole-herb/underdosed products unlikely to work.
- **Product:** Toniiq Toniiq Bacopa Monnieri (45% Bacosides) — ~450 mg bacosides per 1000 mg extract serving (at 45% standardization) active/serving; none (label claims third-party tested only). Chosen because bacoside content is explicitly standardized and high, avoiding the common 'Bacopa 500 mg' whole-herb trap that delivers little bacoside. However it is NOT a trial-branded form. Preferred if available: Bacognize or Synapsa (CDRI-08/KeenMind) — search 'Bacognize Bacopa' or 'Synapsa Bacopa' on Amazon; those are the exact clinically-studied extracts but stock/listings are intermittent.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Toniiq+Bacopa+Monnieri+45%25+bacosides
- **Primary sources:** Kongkeaw C, et al. J Ethnopharmacol 2014;151:528-535 (9-RCT meta); Calabrese C, et al. J Altern Complement Med 2008;14:707-713 (300 mg, 12 wk, healthy elderly); Stough C, et al. Psychopharmacology 2001;156:481-484 (CDRI-08)

### Omega-3 DHA (fish/algal oil) — **3/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for memory as a healthy, omega-3-replete adult. The large independent trials are null. A conditional signal survives only in people with low omega-3 status or genetic/early-neurodegeneration risk. Prior of 3.5 slightly generous.

- **Evidence base:** Multiple large RCTs (VITAL) + phase IIa DHA target-engagement trial — total n ≈ VITAL cognitive n=4218; USC DHA trial small phase IIa; multiple prior meta-analyses
- **Largest independent trial:** VITAL (Kang JH et al., Alz Dement TRCI 2022;8:e12288), n=4218, marine n-3 1 g/day, NO cognitive benefit over 2-3 yr.
- **Effect size (CI):** VITAL: no significant difference in global cognition change vs placebo (null). USC: biomarker positive (CSF DHA +17%), cognition null.
- **Clears MCID?** No, primary cognitive endpoints did not differ from placebo in the large trials.
- **Dose — trials:** VITAL 1 g/day fish oil (840 mg EPA+DHA); USC 2 g/day DHA · **retail (active):** ~200-400 mg DHA per softgel (algal DHA ~400-500 mg); many '1000 mg fish oil' caps deliver far less DHA · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Generally healthy older adults (VITAL) and older adults at dementia risk with low dietary DHA (<200 mg/day) and >=1 risk factor (USC trial). · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. Replete healthy adults show no benefit; signal is deficiency-repletion, not enhancement.
- **Funding split:** VITAL and USC are NIH/independent, and they are the null trials, which strengthens the negative read. · **Outlier leverage:** Older positive meta-signals lean on small trials in low-DHA or MCI populations; large trials wash them out.
- **Regulatory / safety:** Dose-response safety signal (check #6): prescription 4 g/day EPA (REDUCE-IT) and STRENGTH raised atrial-fibrillation risk; high-dose omega-3 is not benign. No memory-specific advisory.
- **Moved from prior:** Section-3 3.5 → 3 — Nudged 3.5 to 3 (half point, not flagged). Decisive new evidence is negative: VITAL (n=4218) showed no cognitive benefit over 2-3 yr, and the 2024/2026 USC high-dose DHA trial (2 g/day, 24 mo, at-risk older adults) confirmed CSF DHA penetrance (+17% at 6 mo) but NULL cognition. Large independent trials are null; benefit confined to low-status/APOE4 subgroups.
- **Conditional on:** Any cognitive rationale is conditional on a low omega-3 index / low dietary DHA and possibly APOE e4 status; not for replete adults.
- **Product:** Nordic Naturals Nordic Naturals Ultimate Omega — ~1280 mg total omega-3 per 2 softgels = ~650 mg EPA + ~450 mg DHA active/serving; none (standard line; Nordic's 'Ultimate Omega Sport' variant carries Informed Sport, not this SKU). Reports the true EPA+DHA content (not a padded '1000 mg fish oil' headline). Meets the VITAL cardiovascular/cognition dose easily. For the high USC DHA-specific target, use a dedicated high-DHA product instead (see Algae Omega row).  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Nordic+Naturals+Ultimate+Omega+1280mg
- **Product:** Nordic Naturals Nordic Naturals Algae Omega — ~715 mg total omega-3 per 2 softgels = ~390 mg DHA + ~195 mg EPA active/serving; none — ⚠ per-serving dose BELOW trial dose. Vegan/algal alternative and the higher-DHA-fraction choice, but flagged dose_adequate=false against the 2 g DHA trial target — it takes many softgels/day. Good match if the goal is the 200-400 mg DHA maintenance range.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Nordic+Naturals+Algae+Omega+vegan+DHA
- **Primary sources:** Kang JH, et al. Alzheimers Dement (N Y) TRCI 2022;8:e12288 (VITAL cognition, null); USC high-dose DHA trial, eBioMedicine 2026, PIIS2352-3964(26)00198-2; REDUCE-IT (Bhatt DL, NEJM 2019) / STRENGTH (Nicholls SJ, JAMA 2020) atrial-fibrillation signal

### Alpha-GPC (choline alphoscerate) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. Efficacy data are confined to add-on dementia trials (mostly industry/AD, not healthy adults), and a large cohort shows a dose-dependent 10-year stroke-risk signal. Risk outruns any consumer benefit; the safety signal makes this a firm avoid.

- **Evidence base:** Small AD add-on RCTs (ASCOMALVA) + large safety cohort — total n ≈ ASCOMALVA n~210; Korean safety cohort >12 million
- **Largest independent trial:** Largest dataset is a SAFETY study: Lee G, et al. (Korean NHIS cohort) 2021, >12M subjects, dose-dependent higher 10-yr stroke incidence in alpha-GPC users.
- **Effect size (CI):** ASCOMALVA: modest cognitive/behavioral benefit as add-on to donepezil in AD (small, industry-associated). No healthy-adult effect size available.
- **Clears MCID?** Not established in any non-dementia population.
- **Dose — trials:** 1200 mg/day (400 mg three times daily) in AD trials · **retail (active):** 300-600 mg/day (below the 1200 mg trial dose) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Alzheimer's and vascular-cognitive-impairment patients (efficacy trials). No healthy-adult cognitive-enhancement evidence. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. Trials are in dementia; no memory-enhancement data in healthy adults.
- **Funding split:** Positive efficacy concentrated in a single manufacturer-associated AD trial program. · **Outlier leverage:** Efficacy case leans on the ASCOMALVA program; safety signal is the more robust, larger dataset.
- **Regulatory / safety:** Stroke-risk safety signal in the Korean nationwide cohort (proposed via choline to TMAO). No efficacy authorization.
- **Primary sources:** Lee G, et al. Association of L-alpha-GPC with subsequent stroke risk (Korean NHIS cohort, >12M), 2021; Amenta F, et al. (ASCOMALVA) J Neurol Sci / J Alzheimers Dis 2012-2014 (add-on to donepezil in AD)

### Citicoline (CDP-choline / Cognizin) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy on current evidence. The one supportive healthy-adult RCT is industry-funded and was not replicated at higher dose, and EFSA (2024) rejected the memory health claim. Prior of 3 is slightly optimistic.

- **Evidence base:** 2 small RCTs in healthy older adults (discordant), plus stroke/dementia trials — total n ≈ Nakazaki 2022 n=100; second (1 g) RCT similar size; small overall for the healthy-adult claim
- **Largest independent trial:** No large independent healthy-adult trial; stroke trials (ICTUS, Davalos 2012, n=2298) were NEUTRAL for a different indication.
- **Effect size (CI):** Nakazaki 2022: significant improvement in composite/episodic memory vs placebo at 12 wk (industry-funded); not replicated at 1 g/day.
- **Clears MCID?** Uncertain, small trial, no established MCID; effect not robust across doses.
- **Dose — trials:** 500 mg/day (Nakazaki, positive) vs 1 g/day (non-replication) · **retail (active):** Cognizin 250-500 mg/day (250 mg products fall below the 500 mg trial dose)
- **Population studied:** Healthy older adults (55-91 yr) with age-associated memory impairment (AAMI); also stroke/vascular-cognitive populations (different indication). · **transfers to healthy adult?** Weak. Signal is in AAMI older adults; no convincing effect in younger healthy adults.
- **Funding split:** The positive healthy-adult RCT is manufacturer-funded (Kyowa Hakko Bio / Cognizin); independent replication absent. · **Outlier leverage:** The healthy-adult case rests essentially on one manufacturer-funded trial. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** EFSA Journal 2024;22:e8861, no cause-effect relationship established between citicoline and memory. (Prescription drug in some EU/Asian markets, not US.)
- **Moved from prior:** Section-3 3 → 2 **(≥1-point move — interesting case)** — Down 3 to 2.5. The key positive healthy-adult trial (Nakazaki 2022) is funded by Kyowa Hakko Bio, the maker of Cognizin citicoline, and a second RCT at 1 g/day did NOT replicate the episodic-memory effect. EFSA 2024 concluded no cause-effect relationship for memory. Funding-source and non-replication flags apply.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **does NOT survive** — score cut (revised 2; decisive check: funding-source). I tried to break the score upward and failed; every check pushed it down. The sole positive healthy-adult RCT (Nakazaki 2021, n=100, 500 mg/12 wk) is authored entirely by Cognizin's manufacturer, and the independent, higher-dose trial (Spiers 1996, n=95, 1 g/3 mo) plus dementia trials at 1 g were all null — an incoherent dose-response that reads as noise. EFSA (2024, e8861) formally rejected the memory claim citing no cause-effect and no plausible mechanism, and the effect is confined to AAMI older adults with no MCID and no transfer to young healthy adults. This is the textbook rubric-2 profile (positive data concentrated in one industry study, mechanism outruns outcome, now with a regulator rejection), so 2.5 is half a point optimistic and I revise to 2.0; the qualitative verdict "do not buy" holds firmly.
- **Conditional on:** Any signal is conditional on age-associated memory impairment and the 500 mg dose; does not extend to healthy younger adults.
- **Primary sources:** Nakazaki E, et al. J Nutr 2022;152:2415-2425, doi:10.1016/j.tjnut.2022.06.001 (Cognizin, manufacturer-funded); EFSA Journal 2024;22:e8861 (citicoline + memory health claim, negative); Davalos A, et al. (ICTUS) Lancet 2012;380:349-357 (citicoline in stroke, neutral)

### Ginkgo biloba (EGb761) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for memory. Large independent prevention trials are null and Cochrane calls the dementia evidence inconsistent and unreliable. Carries a real bleeding/anticoagulant interaction. Prior of 2 holds.

- **Evidence base:** Multiple large RCTs + Cochrane review (Birks/Wieland) — total n ≈ GEM n=3069; GuidAge n=2854; Cochrane pooled thousands
- **Largest independent trial:** GEM Study (DeKosky ST, JAMA 2008;300:2253-2262), n=3069, EGb761 240 mg/day, NULL for dementia prevention.
- **Effect size (CI):** GEM: no reduction in dementia incidence (HR ~1.12, 95% CI 0.94-1.33, no benefit). Cochrane: little-to-no effect on global clinical status at 6 mo (moderate certainty).
- **Clears MCID?** No, prevention trials null; treatment effects inconsistent and below reliable clinical significance.
- **Dose — trials:** EGb761 120-240 mg/day (prevention trials used 240 mg) · **retail (active):** Commonly 120 mg/day standardized extract (below the 240 mg used in the treatment trials that occasionally signal) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Older adults (GEM, GuidAge prevention); dementia/cognitive-impairment patients (treatment trials). Little healthy-young-adult data. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. No credible benefit in cognitively healthy adults.
- **Funding split:** The one recent outlier trial reporting 'very large' effects contrasts with three concurrent null trials; positive results cluster in manufacturer-linked/lower-quality studies. · **Outlier leverage:** Cochrane explicitly notes one trial with large effects against a backdrop of nulls, classic outlier leverage.
- **Regulatory / safety:** Bleeding-risk / anticoagulant-antiplatelet interaction widely flagged (perioperative bleeding, potentiation of warfarin/aspirin). No efficacy authorization.
- **Primary sources:** DeKosky ST, et al. (GEM) JAMA 2008;300:2253-2262; Vellas B, et al. (GuidAge) Lancet Neurol 2012;11:851-859; Birks J / Wieland LS, Cochrane Database Syst Rev (CD003120; CD013661)

### Lion's mane (Hericium erinaceus) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for memory in a healthy adult. The one supportive cognition trial is a small industry-linked MCI study; healthy-adult trials show at most an acute processing-speed blip, and earlier healthy-adult work reported a delayed-recall decrement. Prior of 2 holds.

- **Evidence base:** A few small RCTs; no large trial — total n ≈ Mori n=30; Docherty n=41; acute crossover n=18
- **Largest independent trial:** Docherty S, et al. (Frontiers in Nutrition / Nutrients 2023-2025), n=41 healthy adults, 1.8 g/28 d: acute Stroop speed only, no durable memory benefit.
- **Effect size (CI):** Mori: cognitive-scale (HDS-R) rise during dosing that reversed after washout, small n. Docherty: significant Stroop speed at 60 min only; memory endpoints null; earlier healthy-adult study reported reduced delayed word recall.
- **Clears MCID?** No, acute attention change is minor; no durable memory benefit clearing an MCID.
- **Dose — trials:** Mori 3 g/day powder (16 wk); Docherty 1.8 g/day (28 d); acute 3 g 10:1 extract · **retail (active):** 500-1000 mg/day, frequently non-standardized 10:1 extracts of unverified hericenone/erinacine content (typically below the 3 g whole-powder trial dose) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Japanese older adults with MCI (Mori 2009); healthy young adults 18-45 (Docherty 2023). Mixed and small. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Poor. Benefit is limited to MCI; in healthy adults effects are acute/attentional at best and possibly negative for delayed recall.
- **Funding split:** Mori trial industry-linked; independent healthy-adult trials are the more skeptical ones. · **Outlier leverage:** The reputation rests largely on the single small Mori MCI trial.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No efficacy authorization; generally regarded as safe (occasional dermatologic/GI reactions). No memory claim approved.
- **Conditional on:** Any signal conditional on MCI status and a standardized, adequately-dosed extract; not for healthy adults.
- **Primary sources:** Mori K, et al. Phytother Res 2009;23:367-372 (MCI, n=30); Docherty S, et al. Nutrients/Frontiers in Nutrition 2023-2025 (healthy adults, 1.8 g); La Monica MB, et al. (acute cognition/mood, healthy young adults)

### Phosphatidylserine (soy-derived) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy the retail (soy) product for memory. The good early data used bovine-cortex PS (withdrawn over prion risk); the soy PS that is actually sold has weak, mixed trials, and FDA allows only a heavily-qualified claim. Prior of 2 holds.

- **Evidence base:** Several small RCTs; strongest (bovine) is the wrong molecule for retail — total n ≈ Bovine PS: Cenacchi n=494 (not the retail form); soy-PS trials n=30-120 each
- **Largest independent trial:** No large independent soy-PS trial; Cenacchi 1993 (bovine, n=494) is the largest but is the wrong molecule.
- **Effect size (CI):** Vakhapova 2010 (soy-PS 300 mg): improvement in a memory subgroup with low baseline; several endpoints not significant vs placebo. Mixed.
- **Clears MCID?** Not reliably; soy-PS effects small and inconsistent.
- **Dose — trials:** 300-600 mg/day; historical bovine trials 300 mg/day · **retail (active):** 100-300 mg/day soy-derived PS (often 100 mg, below trial dose and wrong source) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Older adults with age-associated memory impairment or subjective memory complaints. Bovine-PS trials in the elderly; soy-PS trials smaller. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. Signal is in impaired older adults, not healthy adults; and the strongest data are from a discontinued bovine product.
- **Funding split:** Soy-PS trials often industry-supported (Enzymotec/Lipogen); independent replication thin. · **Outlier leverage:** The category's reputation rests on the old bovine-PS trial that is not the retail substance. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** FDA permits only a QUALIFIED health claim for PS with a disclaimer that the evidence is 'very limited and preliminary.' No authorized memory claim.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: population transfer). I tried to lift this to a 3 and could not. The only positive human data sit in cognitively impaired elderly, not healthy adults: Kato-Kataoka 2010 (soy-PS 300 mg, n=78) improved memory only in a low-baseline subgroup, was run at Yakult's own institute, and delayed-recall gains were subgroup-restricted rather than a primary endpoint; Vakhapova is a combined PS-DHA product (Enzymotec/Lipogen-linked) with mixed endpoints, not pure soy-PS; the 2024 Tianjin MCI trial (n=190) is confounded (PS ~63 mg/day bundled with alpha-linolenic acid and ginkgo) so its effect cannot be attributed to PS. The category's strongest trial (Cenacchi 1993, n=494) is bovine-cortex PS, a withdrawn/prion-risk molecule that is not the retail substance. Every one of the seven checks that could rescue the score instead confirms the downgrade: retail dosing (often 100 mg) sits below the 300 mg trial dose (Check 4), the literature is industry-supported with thin independent replication (Check 1), effects are subgroup/low-baseline dependent (Check 2), the population does not transfer to healthy buyers (Check 5), and FDA permits only a qualified claim carrying the 'very limited and preliminary... little scientific evidence' disclaimer (Check 7). This is textbook 'positive results concentrated in industry-funded/low-quality studies, mechanism outrunning outcome' — a 2. I considered 1.5 for the healthy-adult marketing case (a 6-month RCT in healthy 50-70 subjective-memory subjects found no effect), but a genuine, replicated-ish signal in impaired elderly keeps it above pure marketing. Score of 2 holds.
- **Conditional on:** Any weak signal is conditional on age-associated memory impairment; does not apply to healthy adults.
- **Primary sources:** Cenacchi T, et al. Aging Clin Exp Res 1993;5:123-133 (bovine-cortex PS, n=494); Vakhapova V, et al. Dement Geriatr Cogn Disord 2010;29:467-474 (soy-PS 300 mg); FDA qualified health claim letter for phosphatidylserine (2003)

### Huperzine A — **1.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy as a consumer memory supplement. It is a genuine acetylcholinesterase inhibitor (same pharmacology as donepezil) sold unregulated; the AD efficacy meta-analyses are entirely China-based with high risk of bias, and there is no healthy-adult benefit data. Prior of 1.5 holds.

- **Evidence base:** 20 RCTs (all China, high risk of bias) — total n ≈ ~1823 across 20 RCTs (Yang 2013); earlier meta 4 trials
- **Largest independent trial:** No rigorous Western independent trial; a US NIH-funded phase II (Rafii 2011, n=210) was essentially NEGATIVE at 200 mcg twice daily.
- **Effect size (CI):** Yang 2013: significant MMSE and ADL improvement vs placebo in AD at 8-16 wk; effect grew with duration, but pooled from high-bias trials.
- **Clears MCID?** Reported MMSE gains in AD may reach clinical relevance, but bias undermines the estimate; irrelevant to healthy adults.
- **Dose — trials:** 300-500 mcg/day for 8-24 weeks · **retail (active):** 50-200 mcg/day (typically below the 300-500 mcg trial dose) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Alzheimer's disease and vascular dementia patients in China. No healthy-adult cognitive-enhancement RCTs. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. AChE inhibition in AD is not memory enhancement in healthy adults; cholinergic side effects expected.
- **Funding split:** Not formally split; the entire evidence base is a single-country, high-risk-of-bias cluster. · **Outlier leverage:** Whole meta is low-quality; effect could shrink substantially under rigorous trials. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** Sold as a supplement in the US but pharmacologically a reversible AChE inhibitor (prescription drug class elsewhere); cholinergic adverse effects. No efficacy authorization.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 1.5; decisive check: Population transfer (disease AChE-deficit patients vs healthy adults), reinforced by the negative independent Western trial). I tried to lift the score by leaning on the genuine pharmacology and the 400 mcg arm signal in Rafii 2011 (MMSE p=0.007), but that collapses: the primary endpoint was negative (200 mcg BID, p=0.98), the signal is inconsistent (ADAS-Cog decayed to p=0.07 by wk 16), it occurs only in AD patients, and it needs a dose above retail. The entire positive base (Yang 2013, 20 RCTs) is a single-country, self-described high-risk-of-bias cluster with no funding split, so the effect is uninterpretable and compromised. Population transfer is decisive: correcting a cholinergic deficit in dementia is not memory enhancement in healthy adults, for whom there are zero RCTs. Retail 50-200 mcg/day sits below the 300-500+ mcg/day trial dose. Compounded by a real AChE-inhibitor safety profile and abysmal supplement label accuracy (2/22 within spec), the 1.5 holds; a 1.0 is arguable, but genuine (if non-transferable) disease outcome data keep it just above pure marketing.
- **Primary sources:** Yang G, et al. PLoS ONE 2013;8:e74916 (20-RCT meta, all China); Rafii MS, et al. Neurology 2011;76:1389-1394 (US phase II, negative); Xing SH, et al. Evid Based Complement Alternat Med 2014;2014:363985

### NR / NMN (nicotinamide riboside / mononucleotide) — **1.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for memory. These reliably raise blood NAD+ but have no downstream cognitive outcome in humans; the biology outruns the evidence. NMN also has an unresolved US regulatory status. Prior of 1.5 holds.

- **Evidence base:** Multiple small metabolic RCTs; ~1 exploratory cognition trial — total n ≈ Meta-analyses pool small metabolic RCTs; Wu 2025 NR trial small
- **Largest independent trial:** Wu Y, et al. Alzheimers Dement (N Y) TRCI 2025;11:e70023 (NR in SCD/MCI): biomarker shifts, no clear cognitive benefit.
- **Effect size (CI):** NAD+ significantly elevated; clinically relevant metabolic/cognitive outcomes not significantly different from control.
- **Clears MCID?** No cognitive outcome measured that could clear any MCID.
- **Dose — trials:** NMN 250-1000 mg/day; NR 250-1000 mg/day · **retail (active):** NMN 250-500 mg/day; NR ~300 mg/day (doses match trials, but no efficacy endpoint to underdose against)
- **Population studied:** Mostly metabolic-health cohorts; one 2025 NR trial in subjective cognitive decline / MCI. No healthy-adult memory outcome trials. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No, no human memory benefit demonstrated in any population.
- **Funding split:** Small trials often industry-linked; irrelevant because outcomes are null regardless. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable, no positive cognitive outcome to leverage.
- **Regulatory / safety:** FDA (2022) took the position that NMN is excluded from the dietary-supplement definition because it is under investigation as a drug (MIB-626), unresolved US legal status.
- **Primary sources:** Wu Y, et al. Alzheimers Dement (N Y) 2025;11:e70023 (NR, SCD/MCI); NMN meta-analysis, Crit Rev Food Sci Nutr 2024, doi:10.1080/10408398.2024.2387324 (NAD+ up, outcomes null); FDA position on NMN as excluded from supplement definition (2022)


---

## Focus (acute)

### Caffeine (alone) — **4/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** The honest best-in-class acute-focus agent and the reference standard the other compounds are measured against. Well-replicated improvement in vigilance, reaction time and subjective alertness at 40-200 mg within ~30-60 min. It is a stimulant drug sold as a supplement; tolerance and rebound are real, and much of the benefit in habitual users is withdrawal reversal.

- **Evidence base:** Dozens of acute RCTs; multiple meta-analyses — total n ≈ Thousands pooled across the acute-attention literature
- **Largest independent trial:** Numerous independent/military-funded trials (e.g. US Army sustained-operations studies, McLellan 2016 review) show robust vigilance benefit.
- **Effect size (CI):** 2025 Psychopharmacology meta-analysis (10.1007/s00213-025-06775-1) confirms a significant acute caffeine benefit on attention; classic reviews (Einother & Giesbrecht 2013; McLellan 2016) show consistent small-to-moderate improvements in alertness and RT.
- **Clears MCID?** Yes in practical terms for alertness/vigilance, though formal MCIDs for lab attention tasks are not established.
- **Dose — trials:** Typically 40-300 mg (commonly 75-200 mg) single dose. · **retail (active):** Retail caffeine/energy products 50-200 mg per serving - squarely in trial range.
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults across dozens of acute crossover RCTs; effect strongest on simple/sustained attention and vigilance. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Yes - directly demonstrated in healthy adults; benefit partly reflects reversal of caffeine withdrawal in habitual consumers.
- **Funding split:** Effect replicated across academic, government (military) and industry funding; not a funding artifact. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable - effect is one of the most reproduced in cognitive pharmacology.
- **Regulatory / safety:** FDA cautions on pure/highly concentrated caffeine (lethal-dose risk); EFSA single-dose <200 mg / daily <400 mg guidance; pregnancy limits.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 4; decisive check: Effect size vs MCID (check 3), with population transfer / withdrawal-reversal (check 5) as the secondary probe). I attacked the score hardest on effect size and on the withdrawal-reversal confound. The 2025 Psychopharmacology meta-analysis (31 double-blind placebo-controlled RCTs, n=1455, rested healthy adults) gives pooled Hedges g of only ~0.27 for accuracy and ~0.28 for reaction time — a small effect with no formal MCID for lab attention tasks, and benefit is largest in low/non-consumers, meaning much of the effect in habitual retail buyers is withdrawal reversal (narrower net benefit than marketed). But the score holds: the effect is one of the most reproduced in cognitive pharmacology (no outlier dependence), replicates across academic, US military/government, and industry funding (not a funding artifact), retail doses of 50-200 mg sit squarely inside the trial range, non-consumers also benefit so it transfers to healthy adults, and EFSA independently issued a positive efficacy opinion for alertness at 75 mg or more. The FDA/EFSA advisories are safety/dosing warnings, not efficacy warnings. Small-but-genuine, well-replicated effect with a legitimate 'narrower population than marketed' caveat is exactly rubric tier 4 — not a 5 (effect too small to be clinically large), not lower (replication, dose match, and regulatory endorsement all strong).
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Caffeine is a stimulant drug, not a nutrient; it is the active ingredient behind most 'focus supplement' effects.
- **Product:** Nutricost Nutricost Caffeine Pills 200 mg — 200 mg per tablet active/serving; Not NSF/Informed Sport certified (GMP/third-party tested per label). 200 mg is within the effective pre-exercise range (dose_adequate=true); a heavier athlete targeting 3-6 mg/kg could take ~1.5-2 tablets. No widely available NSF/Informed Sport standalone caffeine tablet exists - for a certified option use an NSF Certified for Sport pre-workout. Search URL used because ASINs change.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Nutricost+Caffeine+200mg+tablets
- **Product:** Nutricost Nutricost Caffeine + L-Theanine Capsules — ~200 mg L-theanine + ~100 mg caffeine per capsule active/serving; none (label claims GMP / third-party tested). 2:1 theanine:caffeine ratio matching the classic focus-stack literature. Caffeine 100 mg is squarely in the trial range but below the 160 mg high-dose acute arm. No sport certification available for this SKU.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Nutricost+Caffeine+L-Theanine
- **Primary sources:** Acute caffeine on attention meta-analysis, Psychopharmacology 2025, DOI 10.1007/s00213-025-06775-1; Einother SJL & Giesbrecht T 2013, Psychopharmacology (caffeine and attention review); McLellan TM et al. 2016, Neurosci Biobehav Rev (caffeine, cognition, physical/military performance)

### Caffeine + L-theanine — **3.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Buy if you want a smoother acute alertness/reaction-time boost than caffeine alone, but understand the measurable focus benefit is driven by the caffeine. Multiple acute RCTs show a real, moderate effect on reaction time and attentional switching within the first 1-2 hours. Theanine's added value is subjective (less jitter, calmer alertness), not a distinct attention gain.

- **Evidence base:** 16 RCTs meta-analyzed (49 identified) in the 2025 Nutrition Reviews review — total n ≈ 484 in meta-analyses
- **Largest independent trial:** No single dominant independent trial; evidence is a pool of ~16 small crossover RCTs. Camfield 2014 systematic review (PubMed 24946991) reached the same 'caffeine drives it' conclusion.
- **Effect size (CI):** Combo vs placebo (2025 Nutrition Reviews meta): simple reaction time hr2 SMD -0.71 (95% CI -0.92 to -0.50); attention-switching accuracy hr2 SMD 0.33 (0.13-0.54); digit vigilance accuracy hr2 SMD 0.20 (0.02-0.38); Bond-Lader alertness hr2 SMD 0.28 (-0.08 to 0.65, CI crosses null).
- **Clears MCID?** Partial/unclear - reaction-time SMD -0.71 is moderate-to-large, but the review explicitly notes no MCID (MID) is established for these attention tasks, so clinical meaningfulness cannot be formally confirmed.
- **Dose — trials:** Meta-analysed combos: median L-theanine 82 mg (IQR 48.5-175) + caffeine 89 mg (IQR 54-158). High-dose acute crossover in sleep-deprived adults used 200 mg theanine + 160 mg caffeine. · **retail (active):** Typical retail focus stacks deliver ~100-200 mg L-theanine + ~100 mg caffeine, i.e. within or above the trial range.
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults, weighted mean age 32 (2025 Nutrition Reviews meta: 16 RCTs meta-analyzed, n=484, 229 female, median n=27). Also tested in sleep-deprived young adults and children with ADHD. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Yes - trials were in healthy adults; effect appears within 30-120 min. This is the rare acute-focus claim that does transfer.
- **Funding split:** 8 of 16 meta-analysed trials industry-funded, 4 academic, 2 government, 4 unreported. No pooled-vs-independent split reported, but the caffeine-attribution conclusion holds across funding sources; effect is not a funding artifact. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable - effect is distributed across multiple RTs and vigilance measures, not one extreme study.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None specific to the combination. Caffeine carries standard cardiovascular/anxiety/pregnancy cautions; L-theanine is GRAS.
- **Moved from prior:** Section-3 4 → 3.5
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 3.5; decisive check: Check 3 — Effect size vs MCID: the review establishes no minimum important difference for these attention tasks, so even the moderate-to-large reaction-time SMD cannot be confirmed as clinically meaningful.). I verified the 2025 Nutrition Reviews meta primary numbers directly. The effect survives most refutation attempts: SRT h2 SMD -0.71 (3 trials, I2=0) and attention-switching accuracy 0.33-0.40 (5 trials, tight CIs) are consistent and not outlier-driven; population is genuinely healthy adults (transfers); retail dose sits within/above trial range; no funding-artifact (caffeine attribution holds across strata) and no combination-specific regulatory signal. What bites: there is no established MCID so the impressive SMD has no clinical anchor, the entire base is small crossover trials (n=484, median n=27), several marketed outcomes (Bond-Lader alertness, h1 reaction time) have CIs crossing null, and the synergy premium is unproven — caffeine drives the benefit and theanine's added value is subjective. The claimed 4 largely holds under a rubric that permits 'narrower than marketed,' but these caveats justify shaving to 3.5.
- **Product:** Nutricost Nutricost Caffeine Pills 200 mg — 200 mg per tablet active/serving; Not NSF/Informed Sport certified (GMP/third-party tested per label). 200 mg is within the effective pre-exercise range (dose_adequate=true); a heavier athlete targeting 3-6 mg/kg could take ~1.5-2 tablets. No widely available NSF/Informed Sport standalone caffeine tablet exists - for a certified option use an NSF Certified for Sport pre-workout. Search URL used because ASINs change.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Nutricost+Caffeine+200mg+tablets
- **Product:** Nutricost Nutricost Caffeine + L-Theanine Capsules — ~200 mg L-theanine + ~100 mg caffeine per capsule active/serving; none (label claims GMP / third-party tested). 2:1 theanine:caffeine ratio matching the classic focus-stack literature. Caffeine 100 mg is squarely in the trial range but below the 160 mg high-dose acute arm. No sport certification available for this SKU.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Nutricost+Caffeine+L-Theanine
- **Primary sources:** 2025 Nutrition Reviews meta-analysis, Nutr Rev 83(10):1873 (tea/L-theanine/L-theanine+caffeine on cognition, sleep, mood); Camfield DA et al. 2014, Nutr Rev - PubMed 24946991 (acute tea constituents on cognition/mood); High-dose L-theanine-caffeine in sleep-deprived adults, PMC12491391 (2025 crossover, 200 mg + 160 mg); Kahathuduwa 2020 ADHD neuroimaging RCT, Sci Rep - PMC7403383

### L-Tyrosine — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for everyday focus. Tyrosine only helps when catecholamines are being depleted by an acute stressor - sleep deprivation, cold, noise, sustained multitasking. It does nothing measurable for a rested healthy adult, and effective doses (~100-150 mg/kg, i.e. 7-12 g) dwarf typical retail caps.

- **Evidence base:** 10 RCTs + 4 non-RCTs (Military Medicine REA) — total n ≈ Low hundreds across small crossover studies
- **Largest independent trial:** US military rapid evidence assessment (Military Medicine 2015, 180(7):754) synthesizing government-funded stress trials - independent but weak recommendation.
- **Effect size (CI):** Military Medicine REA (10 RCTs + 4 non-RCTs) made only a WEAK recommendation; all showed directionally positive stress-mitigation but effect sizes/CIs are inconsistent and small-sample. No robust pooled CI exists.
- **Clears MCID?** Unclear - benefits are context-specific (preventing decline under stress) rather than boosting baseline; no MCID framework applied.
- **Dose — trials:** 100-150 mg/kg single dose (~7-12 g for a 70 kg adult); the sleep-deprivation benefit used ~150 mg/kg. · **retail (active):** 500 mg-1 g per serving of L-tyrosine - roughly 7-20x below the effective per-kg trial dose. · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults under acute stress: military trainees, sleep-deprived subjects, cold/altitude exposure, high cognitive load (Jongkees 2015 review, J Psychiatr Res; Military Medicine 2015 rapid evidence assessment). · **transfers to healthy adult?** No - explicitly does NOT improve cognition in unstressed, rested, catecholamine-replete adults. Benefit requires temporary dopamine/noradrenaline depletion.
- **Funding split:** Much of the supportive work is military/government-funded (independent), which is a strength; the limitation is small samples and narrow stressor conditions, not industry bias. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable - no single-study pooled effect; the literature is a set of small task-specific trials.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No major advisory; caution with MAOIs and hyperthyroidism.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For focus lost to sleep deprivation the real fix is sleep; tyrosine only blunts, not reverses, the deficit.
- **Conditional on:** Benefit conditional on an acute catecholamine-depleting stressor (sleep deprivation, cold, sustained high load) AND a per-kg dose far above retail.
- **Primary sources:** Jongkees BJ et al. 2015, J Psychiatr Res - PubMed 26424423 (tyrosine under stress/cognitive demand review); Attipoe S et al. 2015, Military Medicine 180(7):754 - rapid evidence assessment, tyrosine for stress/performance; Behavioral/cognitive effects of tyrosine in healthy adults, ScienceDirect S0091305715000945

### L-theanine (alone) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for acute focus on its own. Across 5 acute RCTs the only significant attention signal was RVIP reaction time at a 400 mg dose; simple reaction time and Stroop were null, and GRADE certainty is low. Its defensible use is subjective calm/anti-stress and taking the edge off caffeine, not standalone attention enhancement.

- **Evidence base:** 5 RCTs — total n ≈ 148
- **Largest independent trial:** Pooled evidence only; largest contributors ~50 participants each. No large independent standalone attention RCT.
- **Effect size (CI):** Simple RT MD -0.46 ms (95% CI -15.65 to 14.73, null); Stroop congruent MD -37.38 ms (95% CI -86.39 to 11.62, null); RVIP at 400 mg MD -15.20 ms (95% CI -28.99 to -1.41, barely clears zero).
- **Clears MCID?** No - the single significant result (a ~15 ms RT change) has no established MCID and its CI nearly touches zero.
- **Dose — trials:** 50-500 mg acute; the one significant attention effect required 400 mg. · **retail (active):** Typical retail 100-200 mg - below the 400 mg needed for the only positive attention endpoint. · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults 20-58 (2025 meta-analysis PMC12609247: 5 RCTs, n=148, acute single doses, measured 40-70 min post-dose; studies from UK, Japan, Turkey, Sri Lanka). · **transfers to healthy adult?** Studies were in healthy adults, but the attention benefit is largely absent at retail doses; the reliable effect is subjective relaxation, not measured focus.
- **Funding split:** The 2025 meta was government-funded (Hungarian Ministry of Innovation/Technology); no industry-driven inflation evident, but only 5 heterogeneous small trials. · **Outlier leverage:** The entire positive attention signal hinges on one 400 mg RVIP result - effectively single-endpoint leverage.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None; L-theanine is generally regarded as safe.
- **Conditional on:** Any attention benefit is conditional on a ~400 mg dose, above typical retail single caps.
- **Primary sources:** L-theanine acute cognition meta-analysis 2025, PMC12609247 (5 RCTs, n=148); Camfield DA et al. 2014, Nutr Rev - PubMed 24946991

### Rhodiola rosea — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for acute baseline focus. Signals exist for mental FATIGUE and burnout, not for sharpening a rested healthy adult. The evidence base could not even be meta-analysed (no two trials shared outcomes), trials are small and methodologically flawed, and positive results cluster in manufacturer SHR-5 studies.

- **Evidence base:** ~11 trials in the 2012 systematic review, none poolable for cognition — total n ≈ Low hundreds across small trials
- **Largest independent trial:** No large independent acute-focus RCT; recent 2025 resistance-trained athlete crossover (PMC12693935) found only modest, outcome-specific effects.
- **Effect size (CI):** No pooled effect size available - Ishaque 2012 could not perform meta-analysis because no two trials reported the same outcome. Individual small trials report reduced fatigue scores but with high risk of bias.
- **Clears MCID?** Cannot assess - no consistent outcome metric, no MCID benchmarking possible.
- **Dose — trials:** Commonly 200-576 mg/day of standardized extract (e.g. SHR-5, ~3% rosavins / 1% salidroside). · **retail (active):** 200-500 mg standardized extract - broadly matches trial doses, so dose is not the limiting issue; evidence quality is.
- **Population studied:** Fatigued students, physicians on night duty, burnout/stress-fatigue patients (Ishaque 2012 systematic review, BMC Complement Altern Med 12:70). Not rested healthy adults seeking acute focus. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Weak - benefit appears only in fatigue/burnout states; does not clearly transfer to acute focus in a rested healthy adult.
- **Funding split:** Positive fatigue findings concentrate in SHR-5 (Swedish Herbal Institute) manufacturer-linked trials; independent replication is sparse and inconsistent. · **Outlier leverage:** Not formally poolable, but the fatigue signal leans on a small cluster of manufacturer trials. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** No major hepatotoxicity or safety advisory found; EMA lists it as a traditional-use herbal (well-tolerated). One isolated case report of herb-herb interaction liver injury.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For fatigue-driven poor focus the durable fixes are sleep sufficiency and load management, not an adaptogen.
- **Conditional on:** Any plausible benefit is conditional on a mental-fatigue/burnout state, not baseline focus.
- **Primary sources:** Ishaque S et al. 2012, BMC Complement Altern Med 12:70 - PubMed 22643043 (systematic review, physical/mental fatigue); Rhodiola dose-response in resistance-trained athletes 2025, PMC12693935

### Citicoline (CDP-choline) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for ACUTE focus. There is essentially no single-dose acute-attention evidence; the supporting human trials are 12-week chronic supplementation, mostly industry-funded (Cognizin/Kyowa) and run in low-attention or clinical populations. Wrong category and compromised evidence base.

- **Evidence base:** A few chronic RCTs plus dementia meta-analyses; 0 acute healthy-adult focus RCTs — total n ≈ ~127 in the key attention trial; hundreds across chronic/dementia pooling
- **Largest independent trial:** No sizable INDEPENDENT acute-focus trial; the dementia meta-analyses (independent) show only modest, heterogeneous benefit in impaired patients.
- **Effect size (CI):** Nakazaki 2021 reported statistically significant improvements in sustained-attention and RVIP reaction time and 'mental energy' composites over 12 weeks, but published effect sizes/CIs are not clearly reported and the trial is manufacturer-run.
- **Clears MCID?** Cannot confirm - effect sizes not transparently reported; no MCID established for the attention endpoints.
- **Dose — trials:** 250-500 mg/day for 6-12 weeks. · **retail (active):** 250-500 mg (often as Cognizin) - matches trial dose, so dose is not the failure point; the problem is no acute data and industry-concentrated chronic data.
- **Population studied:** Adults 35-75 with poor sustained attention (Nakazaki 2021); adolescent males (McGlade); MCI/dementia patients (meta-analyses). Not healthy rested adults acutely. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No - benefit, where seen, is in poor-attention or impaired populations over weeks; it does not transfer to acute focus in a healthy adult.
- **Funding split:** Positive attention trials are Kyowa Hakko / Cognizin (Kirin) funded; no independent pooled-vs-industry split favors it. Independent large acute data absent. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable in the classic sense, but the acute-focus case rests entirely on chronic industry trials. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** No major Western regulator efficacy advisory; sold as a drug (Somazina/Ceraxon) in some countries, supplement in the US.
- **Moved from prior:** Section-3 3 → 2 **(≥1-point move — interesting case)** — Moved 3 -> 2. The prior score reflected general cognition. Re-read against the ACUTE-focus rubric: the two headline trials (Nakazaki 2021, n=127, 500 mg for 12 weeks in 35-75 yr olds with poor attention; McGlade adolescent-male attention study) are chronic, industry-funded (Kyowa Hakko/Cognizin), and in below-average-attention populations. No acute single-dose focus RCT in healthy adults exists. Dementia/MCI meta-analyses (Nutrients 2023, 15(2):386) show only a modest, heterogeneous signal in impaired populations.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: Population transfer (impaired/poor-attention vs healthy), reinforced by funding-source concentration). I tried to break the score upward by hunting for independent acute single-dose data and found none that helps. There IS a registered acute trial the claim missed (NCT05000190, single 250 mg tested 1/2/3 h post-dose, n=104 healthy adults, PepsiCo-funded, completed Nov 2022), but it has no published results - a file-drawer null that, if anything, hurts the compound. Every positive attention trial is manufacturer-funded (Kyowa/Cognizin/Kirin): Nakazaki (127 per-protocol, 12 wk, 500 mg, poor-attention adults 35-75), McGlade women 2012 and adolescent males 2019 - all chronic, none acute, effect sizes/CIs not transparently reported. The decisive checks are population transfer and funding: the independent ADDF review finds insufficient evidence in healthy people with good cognition, benefit concentrated in baseline-deficit populations, and high performers actually DECLINED; a systematic review concludes citicoline ameliorates cognitive deficits but does not improve normal cognitive function. Dose is not the failure point - retail 250-500 mg matches trial dose (retail_below_trial false). No Western regulator efficacy advisory. For ACUTE focus there is zero positive acute evidence in healthy rested adults; a case exists to go below 2, but 2 holds because credible (if industry-concentrated, wrong-population) chronic human outcome data exists, ruling out a 1.
- **Conditional on:** Any benefit is conditional on 6-12 weeks of daily use in a poor-attention or cognitively-impaired population - not acute focus.
- **Primary sources:** Nakazaki E et al. 2021, J Nutr - citicoline 500 mg, 12 wk, attention in poor-attention adults (industry/Cognizin); Nakazaki 2021 memory in healthy older adults, PMC8349115; Citicoline and dementia systematic review/meta-analysis, Nutrients 2023, 15(2):386 (MDPI); McGlade E et al. citicoline and attention in adolescent males

### Panax ginseng — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for acute focus. The cleanest acute trial showed the 200 mg dose actually SLOWED mental-arithmetic responding while only slowing a mood decline; benefits were small, inconsistent in direction, single-lab, and n=30. Mechanism and marketing outrun the outcome data.

- **Evidence base:** A few small acute crossover RCTs — total n ≈ ~30 in the key acute trial
- **Largest independent trial:** No large independent acute-focus RCT; Reay 2010 (n=30) is the anchor and is proprietary-extract based.
- **Effect size (CI):** Reay 2010: 200 mg slowed mental-arithmetic responding (worse speed) while slowing mood decline; 400 mg improved arithmetic and calmness. Small within-subject effects, no clean pooled CI; direction differs by dose and timepoint.
- **Clears MCID?** No - effects are small, timepoint-dependent and partly in the wrong direction; no MCID cleared.
- **Dose — trials:** 200 mg or 400 mg G115 for 8 days. · **retail (active):** Standardized ginseng products commonly 200-400 mg extract - matches trial doses, so dose is not the issue; the effect itself is weak/inconsistent.
- **Population studied:** Healthy young adults, mean age ~23 (Reay 2010, n=30 crossover, G115 standardized extract, Human Psychopharmacol). · **transfers to healthy adult?** Marginal - tested in healthy young adults but the acute cognitive effect is inconsistent and dose-divergent.
- **Funding split:** Acute cognition work centers on the Reay/Kennedy (Northumbria) group using the proprietary G115 extract (Pharmaton); independent replication of acute focus benefit is limited. · **Outlier leverage:** The in-category evidence is essentially one small crossover trial - maximal single-study leverage. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** No major Western efficacy advisory. Safety review (PMC5533164) reports mostly mild transient AEs; cautions for pregnancy, infants/children (fatal poisoning reports), and possible liver effects at high/long-term doses.
- **Primary sources:** Reay JL et al. 2010, Hum Psychopharmacol 25:462 - DOI 10.1002/hup.1138, PubMed 20737519 (G115 working memory/calmness); Safety analysis of Panax ginseng in RCTs, systematic review - PMC5533164
