## Energy

### Caffeine (the honest energy answer - added) — **4.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** This is the one 'energy' agent that actually works. Large independent literature: reliable, dose-related reduction in fatigue and improvement in alertness, vigilance and reaction time, in both rested and sleep-deprived adults. Caveat: it is a stimulant with tolerance and withdrawal, not a nutrient - benefit is largely restoring, not exceeding, baseline in habituated users.

- **Evidence base:** Dozens of RCTs and multiple meta-analyses — total n ≈ Thousands across the independent literature
- **Largest independent trial:** McLellan 2016 (Neurosci Biobehav Rev) comprehensive independent review; 2025 acute-attention meta-analysis - both positive.
- **Effect size (CI):** Consistent dose-related improvements in alertness, vigilance, reaction time and fatigue ratings up to ~300 mg (McLellan 2016 review; 2025 attention meta-analysis shows significant gains in reaction time and accuracy). Non-linear: benefit plateaus and side effects rise above ~300-400 mg.
- **Clears MCID?** Yes - alertness/vigilance improvements are perceptible and behaviorally meaningful, especially under fatigue.
- **Dose — trials:** ~40-300 mg (single dose); ~3-6 mg/kg for ergogenic effect · **retail (active):** 100-200 mg per coffee/tablet/energy product - within the effective range
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults, sleep-deprived subjects, shift/military personnel, athletes - broad and independent. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Yes - directly demonstrated in healthy and sleep-deprived adults.
- **Funding split:** Effect is robust across independent (military/academic) work, not industry-dependent. · **Outlier leverage:** Effect is consistent, not outlier-driven.
- **Regulatory / safety:** FDA warns against pure/highly concentrated powdered caffeine (fatal overdose risk); EFSA: up to 400 mg/day habitual safe for most adults, 200 mg in pregnancy. Non-linear dose-response: low-moderate doses give most of the benefit, higher doses add anxiety/tachycardia/sleep disruption.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 4.5; decisive check: Check 5 - population transfer / mechanism (withdrawal-reversal vs net enhancement)). I tried to break the 5 on all seven checks and it mostly held. Retail dose (100-200 mg) sits squarely inside the trial range (40-300 mg single dose), so retail is NOT below trial. The effect is not industry-dependent or outlier-driven: it rests on dozens of RCTs plus large independent military/academic reviews (NAS/IOM, McLellan) and a 2025 attention meta-analysis, all positive, with dose-related ~11% reaction-time gains and elimination of the vigilance decrement under fatigue - comfortably above MCID for the marketed fatigue/alertness use case, which is directly studied in healthy and sleep-deprived adults (population transfers). Regulatory signals (FDA powdered-caffeine overdose warning, EFSA 400 mg/day and 200 mg pregnancy limits) are safety-only, not efficacy advisories, and are consistent with the grade's honestly-stated non-linear dose-response. The one genuine dent is the withdrawal-reversal debate (Rogers/James): in habitual consumers a substantial share of the alertness 'benefit' is restoration of baseline after overnight withdrawal, not net enhancement, and some trials find no net effect even in rested subjects. The grade itself flags this. But the counter-literature shows clear net, dose-related benefit under sleep deprivation independent of withdrawal, which is the actual energy/fatigue use case. That mechanism ambiguity trims a pristine 5 to 4.5 but does not refute it - caffeine remains the strongest-evidenced compound in the set and the claimed grade survives essentially intact.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Caffeine is a drug/stimulant, not a nutritional supplement; it manages symptoms of fatigue and cannot substitute for adequate sleep.
- **Conditional on:** Largest net benefit in the non-tolerant or sleep-deprived; habitual users partly chase withdrawal relief.
- **Product:** Nutricost Nutricost Caffeine Pills 200 mg — 200 mg per tablet active/serving; Not NSF/Informed Sport certified (GMP/third-party tested per label). 200 mg is within the effective pre-exercise range (dose_adequate=true); a heavier athlete targeting 3-6 mg/kg could take ~1.5-2 tablets. No widely available NSF/Informed Sport standalone caffeine tablet exists - for a certified option use an NSF Certified for Sport pre-workout. Search URL used because ASINs change.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Nutricost+Caffeine+200mg+tablets
- **Product:** Nutricost Nutricost Caffeine + L-Theanine Capsules — ~200 mg L-theanine + ~100 mg caffeine per capsule active/serving; none (label claims GMP / third-party tested). 2:1 theanine:caffeine ratio matching the classic focus-stack literature. Caffeine 100 mg is squarely in the trial range but below the 160 mg high-dose acute arm. No sport certification available for this SKU.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Nutricost+Caffeine+L-Theanine
- **Primary sources:** McLellan 2016 Neurosci Biobehav Rev 71:294, doi:10.1016/j.neubiorev.2016.09.001; Acute caffeine on attention meta-analysis 2025, doi:10.1007/s00213-025-06775-1; Caffeine and cognition in sleep deprivation, NCBI NBK209050

### Iron (oral ferrous salts, for fatigue/energy) — **4/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Buy ONLY if serum ferritin is low. In iron-deficient menstruating women (ferritin <50 ug/L, no anemia) oral iron produces a real, modest reduction in fatigue. In iron-replete adults it does nothing for energy and adds overdose/GI risk. Test ferritin before buying; do not take blind.

- **Evidence base:** 3-4 key RCTs (Vaucher n=198; Verdon n=144; IV-iron blood-donor trial null for fatigue) — total n ≈ ~500 across the fatigue-specific RCTs
- **Largest independent trial:** Vaucher et al. 2012 CMAJ, n=198 women, independent (Swiss academic) - positive but modest. IV-iron blood-donor RCT (PMC7449957) was NULL for fatigue despite raising ferritin.
- **Effect size (CI):** Vaucher 2012: fatigue fell 47.7% (iron) vs 28.8% (placebo), between-group difference -18.9% (95% CI -34.5 to -3.2), p=0.02. Verdon 2003: -8 points on a 1-100 fatigue scale, benefit confined to ferritin <=50.
- **Clears MCID?** Borderline yes in the low-ferritin subgroup; CI lower bound (-3.2%) approaches null, so real but not large. na for replete adults (no effect).
- **Dose — trials:** 80 mg elemental iron/day (ferrous sulfate) for 6-12 weeks · **retail (active):** Ferrous sulfate 325 mg = ~65 mg elemental; ferrous bisglycinate 'gentle' products 18-28 mg elemental
- **Population studied:** Non-anemic menstruating women aged 18-53 with unexplained fatigue and ferritin <50 ug/L (Vaucher, Verdon). Some blood-donor and athlete cohorts. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. Benefit is repletion of a deficit; an iron-replete healthy adult gets no energy benefit and iron accumulates.
- **Funding split:** not reported as a formal subgroup; key positive trials are academic/independent · **Outlier leverage:** Effect rests heavily on the single positive Vaucher trial; the null IV-iron donor trial cuts the other way.
- **Regulatory / safety:** FDA: iron is the leading cause of fatal pediatric poisoning; single-ingredient iron carries mandatory warnings. Excess iron/unmonitored supplementation is harmful. No efficacy advisory.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 4; decisive check: Population transfer (benefit is repletion of an iron deficit in low-ferritin symptomatic women; does not transfer to iron-replete or healthy adults)). I tried to break the 4 mainly on outlier leverage and MCID, and both attempts largely failed. The grade itself worried the effect rested on the single Vaucher trial, but the independent Houston 2018 meta-analysis (4 RCTs, n=714) shows a consistent pooled fatigue effect of SMD -0.38 (95% CI -0.52 to -0.23) with I2=0% and no declared conflicts, which refutes the outlier concern and confirms a real, replicable, small-to-modest signal in low-ferritin non-anemic adults. The genuine dents are (1) the effect is small and Vaucher's between-group CI grazes null, and (2) the well-powered n=405 IV-iron blood-donor RCT was null for fatigue despite fully repleting ferritin, but that trial did not select for baseline symptomatic fatigue, so it reads as a population/selection artifact rather than a clean contradiction. This is a textbook rubric-4: multiple good independent RCTs, effect real but modest, in a population far narrower than the 'energy' marketing implies. Dose in standard ferrous sulfate (~65 mg elemental) matches the 80 mg trial dose, so retail is not below trial; only micro-dose 'gentle' products fall short. The verdict (buy only if ferritin is low; useless and harmful in replete adults; test first) is sound and the 4 holds.</parameter>
</invoke>

- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Identify and treat the cause of iron loss (heavy menses, GI bleeding, malabsorption); dietary iron. Iron is a targeted deficiency correction, not an energy product.
- **Conditional on:** Serum ferritin <30-50 ug/L (iron deficiency without anemia). Collapses to ~1 if ferritin is normal.
- **Product:** Nature Made Nature Made Iron 65 mg (from Ferrous Sulfate 325 mg) — 65 mg elemental iron per tablet active/serving; USP Verified — ⚠ per-serving dose BELOW trial dose. USP Verified is a genuine identity/potency mark (preferred over vague 'third-party tested'). One tablet delivers 65 mg elemental, just under the 80 mg/day trial dose, so a single tablet is marginally short; 2 tablets/day (130 mg) exceeds it and raises GI side-effect risk. Trials used ferrous sulfate specifically, which this matches. Take with vitamin C, away from calcium. Link is a search URL - verify the '65 mg / 325 mg ferrous sulfate' SKU, since Nature Made also sells 18 mg and 27 mg iron.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Nature+Made+Iron+65+mg+ferrous+sulfate+tablets
- **Primary sources:** Vaucher 2012 CMAJ 184(11):1247, doi:10.1503/cmaj.110950 (PMID 22586171); Verdon 2003 BMJ 326:1124 (PMID 12763985); IV iron in non-anemic donors, PMC7449957; Krayenbuehl 2011 Blood 118:3222 (IV iron, ferritin<50 women)

### Creatine monohydrate (for 'energy'/anti-fatigue/mental energy) — **3.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Buy if the goal is strength/power/muscle (that evidence is a 5) or resilience under sleep deprivation/stress. As a general 'energy' or anti-fatigue supplement for a rested healthy adult, the effect is real but small and condition-dependent - a 3.5, not the miracle marketing implies. Cheap and safe, so low downside.

- **Evidence base:** 16 RCTs (cognition meta) plus a large ergogenic literature — total n ≈ ~492 in the cognition meta; thousands in the ergogenic literature
- **Largest independent trial:** Xu 2024 Front Nutr meta-analysis (independent, PRISMA) - positive but modest and somewhat equivocal for cognition.
- **Effect size (CI):** Xu 2024 (Front Nutr, 16 RCTs, n=492): significant but modest SMDs for memory, attention and processing speed; Digit test d=0.77. Gordji-Nejad 2024 (Sci Rep): single 0.2 g/kg dose attenuated - not abolished - sleep-deprivation cognitive decline, peak ~4 h.
- **Clears MCID?** For strength/power: yes. For 'mental energy'/fatigue in rested adults: marginal; several cognition endpoints are equivocal.
- **Dose — trials:** 3-5 g/day maintenance (20 g/day loading); cognition trials often 5-20 g/day; sleep-deprivation study 0.2 g/kg single dose (~14-18 g) · **retail (active):** 5 g/day (standard scoop) - matches muscle dosing, at the LOW end of cognition dosing · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults, athletes, sleep-deprived subjects, vegetarians (larger effect), some clinical cohorts. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Partly. Ergogenic/muscle benefit transfers strongly; anti-fatigue/cognitive benefit shows up mainly under stressors (sleep loss, vegetarian diet, high mental load).
- **Funding split:** not a clean split; effect survives in independent academic work, so not funding-driven · **Outlier leverage:** Cognition pooled effect is heterogeneous across small trials; no single dominating outlier, but wide CIs.
- **Regulatory / safety:** none found; long safety record. Trivial water-weight gain and benign creatinine rise (can confound eGFR).
- **Moved from prior:** Section-3 4 → 3.5 — Held near prior (4 -> 3.5). Docked half a point because the specific 'energy/anti-fatigue' claim (vs strength) is condition-dependent and cognition effects are modest/equivocal; retail 5 g under-doses the cognition trials.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For fatigue from sleep debt, the fix is sleep, not creatine; creatine only blunts the decline.
- **Conditional on:** Meaningful 'energy' effect strongest under sleep deprivation, in vegetarians, or during high cognitive/physical demand; less in a well-rested omnivore.
- **Product:** Thorne Thorne Creatine — 5 g creatine monohydrate per scoop (fully active; no elemental discount) active/serving; NSF Certified for Sport. Best-in-class pick: uses Creapure (the branded form used in most cognition/creatine trials) and is NSF Certified for Sport. 5 g/scoop meets the standard chronic cognition dose. Unflavored powder. ASIN varies by listing; search URL is reliable.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Thorne+Creatine+NSF+Certified+for+Sport
- **Product:** Thorne Thorne Creatine (Creapure) — 5 g creatine monohydrate per scoop active/serving; NSF Certified for Sport. Best-in-class pick: branded Creapure creatine monohydrate AND NSF Certified for Sport (strongest anti-adulteration mark). 5 g/scoop fully covers the 3-5 g maintenance/muscle-energy dose and the low end of cognition dosing; sits at the LOW end of the 5-20 g cognition range and below the ~14-18 g single-dose sleep-deprivation protocol (would need 3-4 scoops). Alternative also NSF: Klean Athlete Klean Creatine (also Creapure).  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Thorne+Creatine+Creapure+NSF+Certified+for+Sport
- **Primary sources:** Xu 2024 Front Nutr, doi:10.3389/fnut.2024.1424972 (PMID 39070254); Gordji-Nejad 2024 Sci Rep 14:4937, doi:10.1038/s41598-024-54249-9; Kreider 2017 JISSN position stand

### Ashwagandha (Withania somnifera) for energy/fatigue — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for energy. The fatigue/energy signal comes almost entirely from manufacturer-linked (KSM-66/Ixoreal) trials with subjective secondary endpoints; independent replication is thin and certainty is graded LOW. There is now a regulatory liver-injury signal. Downside outweighs a weak, industry-driven benefit.

- **Evidence base:** Multiple small RCTs, majority manufacturer-affiliated — total n ≈ Low hundreds per outcome, fragmented across sponsor-linked trials
- **Largest independent trial:** Genuinely independent, adequately powered fatigue RCTs are scarce; the influential energy/QoL data are KSM-66/Ixoreal-linked.
- **Effect size (CI):** KSM-66 trials report ~+18% 'energy/fatigue' and +15% QoL subscale improvements, but as secondary outcomes in sponsor-linked studies. Dose-response meta-analysis: benefit on stress and anxiety with LOW certainty (GRADE); no robust independent fatigue effect size.
- **Clears MCID?** Unclear - subjective self-report changes with no established fatigue MCID and high placebo response in this literature.
- **Dose — trials:** 300 mg twice daily (KSM-66) or 600 mg/day root extract, 8 weeks · **retail (active):** 300-600 mg/day standardized extract - matches trial dose
- **Population studied:** Mostly stressed or anxious healthy adults; fatigue/energy is typically a secondary, subjective outcome, not the primary endpoint. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Weak. Effects are in stressed/anxious cohorts; a 'more energy' claim in a healthy adult is not independently established.
- **Funding split:** Positive results concentrate in industry/manufacturer-affiliated trials; independent evidence is sparse and weaker. · **Outlier leverage:** Small-trial, high-placebo literature; pooled stress/anxiety effects are low-certainty and sensitive to study choice. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** TGA (Feb 2024): risk of liver injury - 12 reports, 4 hospitalizations, onset 6 weeks-22 months; Netherlands Pharmacovigilance (2023/2025) additional cases; NIH LiverTox lists ashwagandha hepatotoxicity. India/AYUSH disputes causality.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: Funding-source subgroup / independence (Check 1), reinforced by regulatory safety signal (Check 7)). I tried to break the grade upward (find an independent fatigue trial) and downward (push below 2), and it held at 2. Check 1 is decisive: every positive energy/fatigue RCT I located is manufacturer-linked - KSM-66/Ixoreal, Witholytin/Verdure Sciences, ASVAMAN - with no genuinely independent, adequately powered fatigue trial. The flagship Witholytin RCT (n=120, industry-funded) actually failed its PRIMARY stress endpoint (PSS p=0.867) and only showed fatigue benefit as a secondary outcome, which strengthens the 'compromised/secondary-endpoint' concern. The 2026 dose-response meta-analysis grades certainty very low to low (Check 2: high heterogeneity, publication bias, small trials). Check 3 holds: subjective self-report with no established fatigue MCID and high placebo response. Check 5 holds: benefit is in stressed/anxious cohorts, not a clean 'more energy in healthy adults' claim. Check 7 is confirmed and now stronger than the grade states - the TGA has issued a SECOND liver-injury safety alert (2024 plus follow-up), LiverTox lists hepatotoxicity. The one claim I softened: retail dose (300-600mg) genuinely matches trial dose, so retail_below_trial is false and Check 4 is not a downgrade lever here. A case for 1.5 exists (no independent fatigue support, primary endpoints sometimes null), but a real if low-certainty stress/anxiety signal underpins the modest fatigue effect, so 2 is fair. Verdict stands: weak, industry-driven benefit against a live regulatory liver signal.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For stress-driven fatigue: sleep, exercise, and evidence-based stress management; if genuinely anxious, seek diagnosis rather than an OTC hepatotoxicity risk.
- **Conditional on:** Any modest benefit is really an anti-stress/anxiety effect in stressed people, not a primary energy effect.
- **Primary sources:** TGA safety advisory: Withania somnifera and rare liver injury (2024); NIH LiverTox NBK548536 (Ashwagandha); Ashwagandha stress/anxiety dose-response meta-analysis (low-certainty GRADE); KSM-66 QoL/energy RCTs (Ixoreal-affiliated)

### CoQ10 / Ubiquinol — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for general energy. 'Fatigue' benefit is concentrated in specific patient groups (fibromyalgia, CKD, heart failure, statin users) in small, heterogeneous, often industry-adjacent trials, and even the statin-myopathy evidence is split. No credible energy benefit in a healthy adult.

- **Evidence base:** 13 RCTs (fatigue meta); 7 RCTs (statin myopathy 2024 meta) — total n ≈ ~1126 (fatigue) / 389 (statin myopathy)
- **Largest independent trial:** Taylor 2015 Atherosclerosis, RCT in confirmed statin myopathy - independent, NULL.
- **Effect size (CI):** Tsai 2022 fatigue meta (13 RCTs, n=1126): pooled SMD favors CoQ10 but across highly heterogeneous clinical populations. Statin myopathy: 2024 meta (7 RCTs, n=389) positive in only 4/7; Taylor 2015 RCT in CONFIRMED statin myopathy found NO benefit on pain, strength or exercise capacity despite raised serum CoQ10.
- **Clears MCID?** Unclear/no in most settings; the confirmed-myopathy RCT is a clean null.
- **Dose — trials:** 100-600 mg/day (fatigue and statin trials) · **retail (active):** 100-200 mg/day (ubiquinone); ubiquinol 100 mg · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Statin users, fibromyalgia, chronic fatigue, CKD, heart failure, older adults - NOT healthy energetic adults. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. Any signal is in depleted/diseased states; healthy adults are not CoQ10-limited.
- **Funding split:** Not formally split, but positive fatigue trials skew small and industry-adjacent; the cleanest independent RCT is null. · **Outlier leverage:** Pooled fatigue effect depends on heterogeneous small trials; statin-myopathy 'signal' hinges on 4 of 7 trials. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** none found; well tolerated. Higher doses raise serum levels without translating to muscle/energy benefit (Taylor).
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: Population transfer — the fatigue benefit exists only in diseased/fatigued cohorts; the healthy-adult subgroup was non-significant (g=-0.351, CI -0.756 to +0.053, p=0.089), so it does not transfer to the marketed "energy for healthy adults" use.). I attempted to raise the score by leaning on the fatigue meta's significant pooled effect (g=-0.398), which the authors report as robust to one-study removal. It did not hold for the marketed use. The healthy-subject subgroup is non-significant with a CI crossing zero (p=0.089), the effective signal is confined to diseased/fatigued patient groups (fibromyalgia, CKD, heart failure, statin users), and the meta is contaminated by industry funding (6/13 trials backed by Kaneka/Asahi/Vitae) plus documented publication bias (Egger p=0.008). The statin-myopathy meta is worse than the grade implies: WMD -0.96 on a 0-10 scale sits below the ~2-point pain MCID, I2=93%, it is funded by a vitamin-industry society, the CI nearly crosses zero (-1.88 to -0.03), and the cleanest independent confirmed-myopathy RCT (Taylor 2015) is null. Two independent checks (population non-transfer to healthy adults, and effect-below-MCID in the statin arm) each independently justify a 2; funding compromise and publication bias reinforce it. Retail ubiquinol 100 mg sits at the very bottom of an effective range where dose-response favors higher doses, so retail<trial is effectively true. No regulatory safety signal and no adverse dose-response direction, but neither rescues efficacy. Score of 2 survives; if anything the marketed healthy-energy claim edges toward 1.5.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For statin muscle symptoms the evidence-based options are dose reduction, statin switch, or intermittent dosing - not CoQ10.
- **Conditional on:** At best a trial in statin-associated muscle symptoms or a documented mitochondrial/deficiency state - not general tiredness.
- **Primary sources:** Tsai 2022 fatigue meta, PMC9449413; CoQ10 statin myopathy 2024 meta, PMC12554813; Qu 2018 JAHA 7:e009835, doi:10.1161/JAHA.118.009835; Taylor 2015 Atherosclerosis (confirmed statin myopathy RCT)

### Cordyceps (Cs-4 / C. sinensis / C. militaris) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for energy. Endurance/VO2max signals come from small, short, often industry- or TCM-linked trials with inconsistent results, and retail products under-dose the trials. No convincing energy benefit in a healthy adult.

- **Evidence base:** A handful of small RCTs plus a heterogeneous meta-analysis — total n ≈ Low hundreds, fragmented
- **Largest independent trial:** Chen 2010 J Altern Complement Med (Cs-4 in older adults) - small, modest positive.
- **Effect size (CI):** Chen 2010 (Cs-4, healthy older adults): modest VO2max/ventilatory-threshold gains in a small RCT. C. militaris acute crossover (2025 PLOS One): raised resting VO2 and faster reaction time - acute, small n. Fungal-supplement meta (2025): 'potential' endurance benefit, heterogeneous, low quality. No pooled fatigue effect with tight CIs.
- **Clears MCID?** Unclear - small samples, surrogate endpoints (VO2, reaction time), not a validated energy/fatigue MCID.
- **Dose — trials:** Cs-4 fermentation product 3-4.5 g/day for 6-12 weeks; C. militaris acute 1 g standardized extract · **retail (active):** ~1 g/day mushroom/mycelium (often low active content) - typically below the 3-4.5 g Cs-4 trial dose · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Small groups of older adults, recreational athletes; some acute crossover work. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Weak/uncertain; some data in older adults and athletes but small and inconsistent.
- **Funding split:** Many trials TCM/manufacturer-linked; independent replication limited. · **Outlier leverage:** Heterogeneous small trials; meta conclusions are fragile and quality is low.
- **Regulatory / safety:** none prominent for efficacy; wild Cordyceps and some products carry heavy-metal/adulteration concerns. Effective doses (3-4.5 g Cs-4) exceed typical retail 1 g.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For aerobic capacity, structured endurance training is the intervention with a real effect size.
- **Conditional on:** Possible marginal ergogenic effect at 3-4.5 g/day Cs-4 - not at typical 1 g retail doses.
- **Primary sources:** Chen 2010 J Altern Complement Med (Cs-4, older adults; PMID 20804368); C. militaris acute crossover 2025 PLOS One, doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0351725; Fungal supplementation endurance meta-analysis 2025, PMC12631420

### Rhodiola rosea (added - common in 'energy'/adaptogen products) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for energy. The fatigue literature is contradictory and uniformly high risk of bias; positive trials are a minority and methodologically weak. Thin evidence dressed up as an adaptogen.

- **Evidence base:** 11 controlled trials, all high risk of bias — total n ≈ Low hundreds, fragmented
- **Largest independent trial:** Ishaque 2012 BMC Complement Altern Med systematic review (independent) - verdict: contradictory, needs a rigorous RCT.
- **Effect size (CI):** Ishaque 2012 systematic review (11 trials): only 2/6 physical-fatigue and 3/5 mental-fatigue RCTs positive; ALL studies had high risk of bias or reporting flaws precluding a reliable pooled effect size.
- **Clears MCID?** Cannot be determined - biased, inconsistent trials with no trustworthy pooled estimate.
- **Dose — trials:** ~200-600 mg/day standardized extract (SHR-5, ~3% rosavins/1% salidroside) · **retail (active):** 200-500 mg/day, standardization highly variable/unverified
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults, students, physicians on night duty, stressed cohorts - small, biased trials. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Uncertain; even the healthy-adult trials are methodologically weak.
- **Funding split:** Not formally split; overall quality too low for a reliable independent estimate. · **Outlier leverage:** Conclusions swing on a few small biased trials.
- **Regulatory / safety:** none prominent; product standardization/adulteration is a quality concern. No reliable dose-response established given trial quality.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For stress/fatigue: sleep, exercise, and workload/recovery management; caffeine for acute alertness.
- **Primary sources:** Ishaque 2012 BMC Complement Altern Med 12:70, doi:10.1186/1472-6882-12-70 (PMID 22643043)

### Adaptogen blends (proprietary multi-herb 'energy'/'adrenal support' formulas) — **1/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. Proprietary blends have essentially no whole-product RCTs, hide doses behind 'proprietary blend' labels that are almost always sub-therapeutic, and lean on mechanism and single-herb extrapolation. Marketing.

- **Evidence base:** 0 informative RCTs on the finished blends — total n ≈ 0 (as sold)
- **Largest independent trial:** None on the blends.
- **Effect size (CI):** No credible whole-product RCT with an energy outcome. Effect size unknown.
- **Clears MCID?** No - no outcome data exist to clear anything.
- **Dose — trials:** n/a - the finished multi-herb products are not tested · **retail (active):** Proprietary blend totals often 300-1000 mg split across 5-10 herbs = tens of mg each, far below any single-herb trial dose · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** None for the blends as sold; evidence is borrowed from single-herb studies at doses the blends rarely deliver. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No basis to claim transfer; the products themselves are untested.
- **Funding split:** not applicable · **Outlier leverage:** not applicable
- **Regulatory / safety:** Blends frequently contain ashwagandha (see TGA liver advisory) and undisclosed stimulant amounts; 'proprietary blend' labeling obscures actual doses.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Fix the actual drivers of low energy: sleep duration/quality and apnea screening, iron/B12/thyroid labs, physical activity, alcohol reduction. Single well-dosed agents (caffeine; iron/B12 if deficient) beat any blend.
- **Primary sources:** No qualifying whole-product RCT located; Single-herb reviews (Rhodiola PMID 22643043; Ashwagandha low-certainty GRADE) do not validate the blends

### Vitamin B12 (cyanocobalamin/methylcobalamin, for fatigue/energy) — **1/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do NOT buy for energy unless you are actually B12-deficient. In deficiency (pernicious anemia, vegans, metformin/PPI users, malabsorption) repletion is a 5. In replete people - which is most tired people - B12 does nothing; the 'energy shot' is theatre. Test B12/MMA first.

- **Evidence base:** 16 RCTs in the fatigue/cognition meta, but ~0 informative fatigue RCTs in non-deficient adults — total n ≈ 6276 (Markun 2021), but not fatigue-in-replete
- **Largest independent trial:** Markun 2021 Nutrients systematic review/meta-analysis (independent) - concluded evidence for fatigue in non-deficient people is essentially absent.
- **Effect size (CI):** Markun 2021 meta-analysis (16 RCTs, 6276 participants) found NO reliable fatigue benefit in non-deficient populations; only a single eligible fatigue study, insufficient to pool. No clinically meaningful energy effect established.
- **Clears MCID?** No - no measurable fatigue effect to clear an MCID in replete adults. na in deficiency (repletion is corrective).
- **Dose — trials:** Deficiency correction: 1000 ug oral/day or IM injections; marketed energy dose 500-5000 ug with no fatigue evidence · **retail (active):** 1000 ug cyanocobalamin typical (~40,000% RDA); 'energy' sublinguals/shots 2500-5000 ug
- **Population studied:** Mostly older adults and mixed clinical cohorts; almost no eligible RCTs in non-deficient people with fatigue. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. Benefit is entirely repletion of a deficit; megadoses in replete people are renally excreted.
- **Funding split:** not applicable - no positive body of trials to split · **Outlier leverage:** not applicable
- **Regulatory / safety:** none found for efficacy; B12 is safe but that is not the same as effective.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Treat the cause of deficiency (diet, pernicious anemia, metformin/PPI review). For non-deficient fatigue: sleep, screen for anemia/thyroid/depression.
- **Conditional on:** Documented B12 deficiency (low serum B12 or elevated methylmalonic acid/homocysteine).
- **Primary sources:** Markun 2021 Nutrients 13(3):923, doi:10.3390/nu13030923 (PMID 33809274); LiverTox/clinical reviews of B12 repletion

## Skin

### Nicotinamide (niacinamide, oral vitamin B3) — **3.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Buy ONLY if you are a high-risk immunocompetent patient with a prior nonmelanoma skin cancer, and dose it correctly at 500 mg TWICE daily. This is a chemoprevention drug-like use, not a cosmetic/anti-aging supplement — there is essentially no credible evidence it improves the appearance of healthy skin.

- **Evidence base:** 1 pivotal phase 3 RCT (ONTRAC) + 1 phase 3 RCT in transplant recipients (ONTRANS, null) + 1 large independent retrospective cohort (2025 VA, n=33,822) — total n ≈ ONTRAC n=386; ONTRANS n=158; VA cohort n=33,822 (12,287 exposed)
- **Largest independent trial:** 2025 Veterans Affairs retrospective cohort (VA Corporate Data Warehouse), 33,822 patients / 12,287 nicotinamide-exposed: HR 0.86 any new skin cancer, HR 0.78 SCC — independent, supports ONTRAC in high-risk patients.
- **Effect size (CI):** ONTRAC: 23% relative reduction in new NMSC, rate ratio 0.77 (95% CI 0.63-0.95, P=0.02); SCC -30%, BCC -20% (NS); AK -11% to -20%. VA cohort: HR 0.86 any skin cancer, HR 0.78 for SCC (0.44 if started after first cancer), BCC HR 1.00 (no effect).
- **Clears MCID?** Yes for the chemoprevention endpoint — a ~1 in 4 reduction in new skin cancers is clinically meaningful in a high-risk population. NA for cosmetic outcomes (never measured).
- **Dose — trials:** 500 mg twice daily = 1000 mg/day for 12 months · **retail (active):** Nicotinamide/niacinamide capsules commonly labeled 500 mg once daily (500 mg/day) — half the trial dose; many 'B3' products are nicotinic acid (niacin), the wrong molecule. · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Immunocompetent adults with >=2 histologically confirmed nonmelanoma skin cancers in the prior 5 years (ONTRAC); large VA cohort of veterans with prior keratinocyte cancer. NOT tested for cosmetic/anti-aging endpoints. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. Benefit is confined to high-risk skin-cancer patients; no evidence it helps a healthy adult's skin, and ONTRANS shows it does NOT work in immunosuppressed transplant recipients.
- **Funding split:** ONTRAC funded by Australia's NHMRC (independent/government); 2025 VA cohort is independent. Not an industry-funding artifact. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable — pivotal RCT plus a large independent cohort concur; effect is not driven by one study.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No efficacy advisory; JAAD 2025 safety reviews note nicotinamide is well tolerated (distinct from niacin — no flushing).
- **Moved from prior:** Section-3 4 → 3.5
- **Adversarial re-check:** **does NOT survive** — score cut (revised 3.5; decisive check: Check 2 (outlier / single-RCT leverage): the RCT base is one statistically-criticized trial (ONTRAC) plus a failed replication (ONTRANS) and a 2024 meta-analysis finding pooled evidence insufficient). I tried to break the score on outlier leverage, funding, MCID, dose, and population. Funding survives (ONTRAC is NHMRC/government, VA cohort independent — not an industry artifact) and the practical verdict is honest (high-risk immunocompetent only, not cosmetic, dose at 1000 mg/day). But the RCT evidence is thinner than a 4 implies: ONTRAC (n=386) is the only positive RCT, its 23% effect has a lower CI bound near null (4%), it drew published criticism of non-reproducibility, ONTRANS failed to replicate, and a 2024 meta-analysis found pooled evidence insufficient. The benefit is also SCC-specific (VA BCC HR 1.00), and retail 500 mg once-daily sits below the 1000 mg/day trial dose. The large independent VA cohort (SCC HR 0.78, tight CI) genuinely rescues the SCC signal from collapse, so the effect is real — but 'one fragile RCT + a null + one observational cohort, SCC-only, narrow population' is a 3.5, not the 'multiple good RCTs' a 4 asserts.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For general skin health/appearance the effective interventions are broad-spectrum sunscreen, sun avoidance/protective clothing, and dermatologic surveillance — not a B3 pill.
- **Conditional on:** Applies only to immunocompetent patients with a prior nonmelanoma skin cancer, at 500 mg BID. Does not apply to transplant/immunosuppressed patients (ONTRANS null) or to cosmetic use.
- **Product:** NOW Foods NOW Foods Niacinamide (Vitamin B-3) 500 mg — 500 mg nicotinamide per capsule active/serving; none (NOW is UL GMP / in-house tested) — ⚠ per-serving dose BELOW trial dose. Correct molecule: niacinamide/nicotinamide, NOT nicotinic acid (niacin) - many shelf 'B3' products are the wrong molecule and cause flushing. Single 500 mg cap is HALF the 1000 mg/day trial dose; take one morning + one evening (500 mg BID) to match the 12-month skin-cancer-prevention protocol. Verify label reads 'Niacinamide', not 'Niacin', before buying.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=NOW+Foods+Niacinamide+500+mg+capsules
- **Primary sources:** Chen AC et al. A Phase 3 Randomized Trial of Nicotinamide for Skin-Cancer Chemoprevention (ONTRAC). NEJM 2015;373:1618-1626. PMID 26488693; Allen NC et al. Nicotinamide for Skin-Cancer Chemoprevention in Transplant Recipients (ONTRANS). NEJM 2023;388:804-812. PMID 36856616. DOI 10.1056/NEJMoa2203086; 2025 Veterans Affairs retrospective cohort, nicotinamide and keratinocyte carcinoma (n=33,822); reported AJMC/News-Medical Sep 2025; JAAD Reviews 2025: Examining the safety of nicotinamide for skin cancer prevention. S2950-1989(25)00079-0

### Polypodium leucotomos extract (Fernblock / Heliocare) — **3/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Conditional / low priority. Real but modest photoprotective signal on surrogate endpoints (raises MED, blunts UV erythema). It is an ADJUNCT, never a substitute for sunscreen, and most of the literature comes from the manufacturer. Reasonable add-on for photodermatoses/melasma under a dermatologist; not worth buying as stand-alone sun defense.

- **Evidence base:** Multiple small RCTs and open-label studies (systematic reviews pool ~10-20 studies); no large hard-endpoint RCT — total n ≈ Individual trials typically 20-60 subjects; no large pooled hard-outcome trial
- **Largest independent trial:** Thin — the most robust data (healthy-adult MED RCTs; El Mansouri 2023 xeroderma pigmentosum RCT) are small and frequently manufacturer-linked; no large independent hard-endpoint trial.
- **Effect size (CI):** Surrogate endpoints only: RCTs report subjects markedly more likely to increase MED and reduce erythema (one healthy-adult RCT reported ~22x higher odds of MED increase vs placebo); a 2025 red-orange+PLE RCT reported +23.8% MED and -46.2% erythema intensity. No wrinkle/skin-cancer hard-endpoint RCT.
- **Clears MCID?** Uncertain/likely no in isolation — a fractional MED increase is far below the protection of an SPF 30-50 sunscreen; no defined MCID and no hard-outcome data.
- **Dose — trials:** 240 mg once or twice daily (240-480 mg/day), often started ~15 days before sun exposure · **retail (active):** Heliocare and equivalents are 240 mg capsules dosed 240-480 mg/day — retail matches trial dose.
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults (MED/erythema studies) and patients with photodermatoses (polymorphic light eruption, solar urticaria), melasma, and xeroderma pigmentosum. Mostly small, short trials. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Partially — MED/erythema studies are in healthy adults, so the surrogate effect transfers, but real-world value over proper sunscreen use is unproven.
- **Funding split:** Not cleanly split in a meta-analysis, but a large share of trials are authored by or affiliated with the manufacturer (Industrial Farmaceutica Cantabria / IFC, Fernblock). Independent replication is limited. · **Outlier leverage:** Not formally assessed; small heterogeneous trials on surrogate endpoints.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found (efficacy or safety) from TGA/EMA/FDA/MHRA.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Primary evidence-based sun protection is topical broad-spectrum SPF 30-50+ sunscreen, shade, and UPF clothing. Note nicotinamide, by contrast, shows NO MED effect — different mechanism, not interchangeable.
- **Conditional on:** Value is greatest as an adjunct in diagnosed photodermatoses/melasma alongside sunscreen; not as monotherapy sun protection.
- **Product:** Heliocare (Cantabria Labs/Ferndale) Heliocare Antioxidant Supplement (Fernblock, 240 mg) — 240 mg Fernblock PLE per capsule active/serving; none (proprietary branded Fernblock ingredient). Branded, clinically-studied Fernblock form used in the trials - the preferred ingredient. 240 mg/cap matches the 240-480 mg/day trial range exactly. Best started ~15 days before sun exposure; an adjunct to, not a replacement for, topical sunscreen. Confirm the standard 240 mg 'Antioxidant' SKU vs the 'Heliocare Ultra' variant.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Heliocare+Fernblock+240+mg+60+capsules
- **Primary sources:** Middelkamp-Hup/Nestor healthy-adult PLE MED/erythema RCTs (240 mg dosing); El Mansouri et al. Combined oral+topical Fernblock in xeroderma pigmentosum, Photodermatol Photoimmunol Photomed 2023. DOI 10.1111/phpp.12904; 2025 RCT: Red orange + Polypodium leucotomos on UVB-induced skin responses. PMC11990338; Clinical Efficacy & Safety of Oral PLE for Photoprotection: A Systematic Review, SKIN J Cutan Med (dermsquared #413)

### Astaxanthin (oral, Haematococcus pluvialis) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Weak buy at best. A meta-analysis shows statistically significant but small improvements in skin elasticity and moisture and NO effect on wrinkle depth, from small, short (6-12 week) trials with heavy involvement from the ingredient maker (AstaReal/Fuji) and high heterogeneity. Signal is real but fragile.

- **Evidence base:** ~9 RCTs in the Nutrients 2021 meta-analysis (11 studies total including open-label) — total n ≈ Small — individual trials typically 20-65 subjects; pooled meta across ~9 RCTs
- **Largest independent trial:** Thin — most robust trials are small and frequently industry-affiliated; no large independent RCT.
- **Effect size (CI):** Meta-analysis: elasticity SMD 0.77 (95% CI 0.19-1.35, I2=75%, p=0.009); moisture SMD 0.53 (95% CI 0.05-1.01, I2=52%, p=0.03); wrinkle depth NOT significantly reduced.
- **Clears MCID?** Unclear — moderate instrument effects from short trials with high heterogeneity; no established MCID and wrinkle depth (the outcome consumers care about) is null.
- **Dose — trials:** 2-12 mg/day (most 4-9 mg/day) · **retail (active):** Retail astaxanthin 4-12 mg/day — matches trial dosing.
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults (often middle-aged, several Japanese-population trials); 6-12 week durations. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Yes for the studied endpoints (healthy adults), but magnitude and durability beyond 12 weeks are unknown.
- **Funding split:** No clean meta-analysis funding subgroup, but several included trials are authored/funded by AstaReal / Fuji Chemical Industry (dominant ingredient supplier). Independent replication limited; treat pooled effect with caution. · **Outlier leverage:** High heterogeneity (I2 up to 75%) suggests the pooled elasticity effect may be sensitive to individual studies; no formal leave-one-out analysis reported.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found from TGA/EMA/FDA/MHRA.
- **Moved from prior:** Section-3 2 → 2.5 — Nudged 2 -> 2.5 (below the 1-point flag threshold): a formal meta-analysis shows consistent significant elasticity/moisture effects, slightly above pure marketing, though still small, industry-heavy, and wrinkle-null.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For photoaging: daily broad-spectrum sunscreen and topical retinoids remain the higher-evidence interventions.
- **Conditional on:** Any claim should be limited to modest hydration/elasticity, not wrinkle reduction.
- **Primary sources:** Ng QX et al. Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis on the Effects of Astaxanthin on Human Skin Ageing. Nutrients 2021;13(9):2917. PMC8472736. DOI 10.3390/nu13092917; Ito N et al. Protective Role of Astaxanthin for UV-Induced Skin Deterioration in Healthy People: RCT. Nutrients 2018. PMC6073124; Frontiers Med 2025 meta-analysis of dietary supplements for skin photoaging. PMC12318760

### Oral hyaluronic acid (sodium hyaluronate) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Weak/conditional. Multiple RCTs at 120 mg/day for 12 weeks report improved hydration, elasticity, and reduced wrinkle depth, but the positive evidence is concentrated in trials authored or funded by the ingredient maker (Kewpie), in small, ethnically narrow (Japanese) samples, and whether oral HA reaches the skin in humans is unresolved. Real signal, shaky foundation.

- **Evidence base:** ~4-6 small RCTs plus a 2025 larger RCT (n=150) — total n ≈ Most trials 40-129; 2025 sodium hyaluronate RCT n=150
- **Largest independent trial:** Thin — the largest recent trials still involve the manufacturer; no large fully independent RCT.
- **Effect size (CI):** 120 mg/day x12 wk RCTs report significant improvement in wrinkle assessment, stratum corneum hydration, TEWL, and elasticity; 2025 n=150 RCT reports significant gains in dermal density, hydration, elasticity, epidermal thickness, and wrinkle depth. Effect sizes/CIs inconsistently reported across the small trials.
- **Clears MCID?** Uncertain — instrument-measured improvements are statistically significant but small; no established MCID, and visible clinical relevance is modest.
- **Dose — trials:** 120 mg/day (range 120-240 mg/day), various molecular weights (2 kDa to ~300-400 kDa) · **retail (active):** Retail oral HA commonly 100-200 mg/day — matches the studied 120 mg dose.
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults with dry skin/wrinkles, predominantly Japanese cohorts aged ~22-60; 8-12 week trials. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Yes for the studied endpoints (healthy adults), but generalizability beyond Japanese cohorts and beyond 12 weeks is untested.
- **Funding split:** No formal meta subgroup, but the core hydration/wrinkle evidence comes largely from authors employed by Kewpie Corporation (Hyabest ingredient); a 2025 trial was manufacturer-funded (Ritual) with Kewpie supplying HA. Independent confirmation thin. · **Outlier leverage:** Not formally assessed; small heterogeneous trials. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found from TGA/EMA/FDA/MHRA.
- **Moved from prior:** Section-3 2 → 2.5 — Nudged 2 -> 2.5 (below the 1-point flag threshold): several concordant positive RCTs including a 2025 n=150 trial exceed pure marketing, but funding concentration keeps it low.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2.5; decisive check: Funding-source subgroup (check 1): every substantial RCT is run by an ingredient manufacturer (Kewpie, Contipro, Bioiberica, Ritual/Kewpie); zero independent RCT exists, and the one independent meta-analysis merely pools these industry trials.). I tried to push 2.5 down to 2.0 and up to 3.0, and it held. Downward: funding capture is total and worse than the grade stated — I verified four separate manufacturers behind every trial (Kewpie 2021 n=40, Contipro 2025 n=150, Bioiberica 2025 n=60, Ritual with Kewpie-supplied HA), no fully independent RCT, and all endpoints are instrument/surrogate measures with no MCID (firmness, wrinkle volume, and TEWL were non-significant even in the pooled meta-analysis), with mechanism unresolved — a textbook score-2 profile. Upward: the grade's 'predominantly Japanese, untested beyond' weakness is now partly refuted, since two 2025 Caucasian-cohort trials reproduce the hydration signal, retail dose (100-200 mg) matches the trial 120 mg, and an independent academic meta-analysis (UC Irvine) reached significance for hydration, elasticity, and wrinkle depth. These forces cancel; the real, replicated-but-modest signal sitting on an entirely industry-funded foundation is exactly what 2.5 encodes. No regulatory advisory and no adverse dose-response found.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Topical humectants/moisturizers (including topical HA) and daily sunscreen are the higher-evidence, cheaper route to skin hydration.
- **Conditional on:** Best-supported claim is modest short-term skin hydration; wrinkle claims rest on manufacturer-linked trials.
- **Primary sources:** Oe M et al. Oral hyaluronan relieves wrinkles: 12-week double-blind placebo-controlled study. Clin Cosmet Investig Dermatol 2017;10:267-273. PMC8308347 (Kewpie-funded); Oral sodium hyaluronate improves skin hydration, barrier function and signs of aging: RCT in 150 healthy adults. Sci Rep 2025. DOI 10.1038/s41598-025-32758-5. PMC12827323; JDDonline 2025: Oral HA supplement efficacy in hydration, elasticity, wrinkle depth. PMID 40911749

### Collagen peptides (hydrolyzed collagen) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for wrinkles. Two 2025/2026 meta-analyses show the anti-wrinkle claim is a funding-source-and-outlier artifact: it survives pooled analysis but vanishes in independent and high-quality studies, and collapses when one outlier study is removed. A modest hydration/elasticity signal is the only thing that partly survives, and even that shrinks.

- **Evidence base:** 23 RCTs (AmJMed 2025 meta-analysis); overlapping set re-analyzed by Batool 2026 — total n ≈ 1,474 (AmJMed 2025)
- **Largest independent trial:** Independent, high-quality studies in the AmJMed 2025 pool showed no significant effect on hydration, elasticity, or wrinkles — the benefit is confined to industry-funded and low-quality trials.
- **Effect size (CI):** Batool 2026 primary pooled: hydration SMD 0.44 (95% CI 0.15-0.73), elasticity SMD 0.62 (95% CI 0.15-1.10), TEWL -0.39 (95% CI -0.62 to -0.16). After removing high-leverage outliers: wrinkle effect non-significant; hydration and elasticity diminished. Week-8 heterogeneity fell from I2=79.2% to 40.4% on outlier removal.
- **Clears MCID?** No for wrinkles (effect disappears in sensitivity/independent analysis). Borderline for hydration/elasticity — statistically present but small and attenuated once outliers/funding are controlled.
- **Dose — trials:** Skin RCTs used ~2.5-10 g/day hydrolyzed collagen (distinct from the 15-30 g used in tendon studies) · **retail (active):** Retail skin collagen 2.5-10 g/day — matches the skin-trial dose (the dose-shortfall trap applies to tendon/joint collagen, not skin dosing).
- **Population studied:** Predominantly women 35-65 with visible photoaging; short (8-12 week) trials. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Marginal — studied in mild photoaging; any residual hydration benefit is small and cosmetic, not structural.
- **Funding split:** AmJMed 2025 (23 RCTs, n=1474): industry-funded studies POSITIVE; non-industry-funded studies showed NO benefit for hydration, elasticity, or wrinkles. High-quality studies showed NO significant effect on any endpoint; only low-quality studies showed an elasticity gain. · **Outlier leverage:** Yes — Batool 2026: pooled wrinkle/roughness/dermal-density effects were driven by high-leverage outlier studies; excluding them removed the wrinkle effect and roughly halved heterogeneity. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found; the issue is efficacy/bias, not safety.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Evidence-based anti-aging: daily broad-spectrum sunscreen, topical retinoids, and topical moisturizers/humectants.
- **Conditional on:** Any defensible claim is limited to modest short-term skin hydration; the wrinkle/anti-aging claim is not supported.
- **Primary sources:** Effects of Collagen Supplements on Skin Aging: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of RCTs. Am J Med 2025. S0002-9343(25)00283-9; 23 RCTs, n=1474; Batool et al. Oral Collagen Peptides and Skin Rejuvenation: Updated Meta-Analysis. J Cosmet Dermatol 2026. DOI 10.1111/jocd.71041; Pu SY et al. Effects of hydrolyzed collagen on skin aging: systematic review/meta-analysis. PMID 33742704

### Biotin (vitamin B7) — **1/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for skin/hair/nails unless you have a documented, genuine biotin deficiency (rare). No credible RCT shows benefit in non-deficient adults, and it actively causes dangerous laboratory-test interference — including falsely low troponin (a reported death) and false thyroid results. Actively worse than inert.

- **Evidence base:** No credible RCTs for skin/hair/nails in non-deficient people; brittle-nail evidence = ~3 small studies without placebo controls — total n ≈ Negligible controlled evidence
- **Largest independent trial:** None of adequate quality; dermatology reviews (JAAD 2022) confirm the lack of RCTs.
- **Effect size (CI):** No reliable placebo-controlled effect size exists for skin/hair/nails in non-deficient adults.
- **Clears MCID?** No — there is no credible controlled effect to clear any MCID.
- **Dose — trials:** Case/series doses vary (e.g., 2.5 mg/day for nails) but were uncontrolled · **retail (active):** Retail biotin 5,000-10,000 mcg (5-10 mg) — 150-300x the ~30 mcg adequate intake; megadose with no efficacy rationale and clear assay-interference risk.
- **Population studied:** Efficacy data limited to case reports and small uncontrolled series, mostly in genuine deficiency or specific genetic/nail-fragility conditions. No adequate placebo-controlled RCT in healthy, non-deficient adults. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. Any benefit is confined to correcting true deficiency; supplementation in replete/healthy adults has no demonstrated benefit.
- **Funding split:** Not applicable — no body of RCTs to split; the problem is absence of evidence, not funding bias. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable.
- **Regulatory / safety:** FDA Safety Communication (2017, updated 2019): high-dose biotin interferes with immunoassays via the biotin-streptavidin system, causing falsely low troponin (one patient death reported), and false thyroid (FT4/FT3 high, TSH low, mimicking Graves) and hCG results. AACC/ADLM guidance echoes this. Lives in the safety literature, not the efficacy literature.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For hair/skin/nails address the actual cause (iron/ferritin, thyroid, protein/caloric adequacy, androgenetic alopecia treatment); biotin is not the fix in non-deficient people.
- **Conditional on:** Only justified to correct a laboratory-confirmed biotin deficiency; discontinue >=48-72 h before blood tests to avoid assay interference.
- **Primary sources:** NIH ODS Biotin Health Professional Fact Sheet (efficacy and deficiency); FDA Safety Communication: Biotin (Vitamin B7) Interference with Troponin and Other Laboratory Tests (2017, updated 2019); AACC/ADLM Academy Guidance Document on Biotin Interference in Laboratory Tests; JAAD 2022: Biotin beware - perils of biotin supplementation. S0190-9622(22)00352-8; How Biotin Induces Misleading Results in Thyroid Bioassays: Case Series. PMC6663274
