## Bones

### Calcium + vitamin D (combined) — **3/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Conditional buy. Real hip-fracture reduction exists but is concentrated in institutionalized, low-intake, or vitamin-D-deficient elderly (Chapuy/Decalyos, French nursing homes). In healthy community-dwelling adults the effect is near-null, so for the typical retail buyer it is weak. Get calcium from diet first; supplement adds kidney-stone risk.

- **Evidence base:** ~30+ RCTs; ~6 combined-therapy RCTs in the key pooled analysis — total n ≈ >50,000 across meta-analyses
- **Largest independent trial:** WHI (Jackson 2006, NEJM, PMID 16481635), NIH-funded, n=36,282: no significant overall hip-fracture reduction, +kidney stones.
- **Effect size (CI):** Yao 2019 (6 RCTs, n=49,282): hip fracture RR ~0.84 (favouring). WHI (Jackson 2006, n=36,282): overall hip fracture HR 0.88 (0.72-1.08, NS); 21% lower in age 60+ and ~29% in adherent. Zhao 2017 (33 RCTs, n=51,145 community-dwelling): hip fracture RR 1.09 (0.85-1.39), NS.
- **Clears MCID?** Yes in frail/institutionalized elderly (absolute hip-fracture reduction meaningful); No/near-null in community-dwelling adults.
- **Dose — trials:** 1000-1200 mg elemental calcium + 400-800 IU vitamin D3 daily (WHI 1000 mg Ca + 400 IU D; Decalyos 1200 mg Ca + 800 IU D) · **retail (active):** 500-600 mg elemental calcium per pill (often 1000-1200 mg/day) + 800-1000 IU D3 - broadly in trial range
- **Population studied:** Mostly women 65+, many institutionalized or vitamin-D-deficient/low calcium intake; some healthy postmenopausal (WHI). · **transfers to healthy adult?** Poor. Benefit is a repletion/frailty effect, not a general one; healthy replete community-dwelling adults get little to nothing.
- **Funding split:** No clean funding-source split published; the decisive split is by population (institutional/deficient vs community-dwelling), not by funder. · **Outlier leverage:** Pooled hip-fracture signal leans heavily on Decalyos (Chapuy) institutionalized cohorts; remove institutional trials and the community-dwelling estimate is null.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No efficacy advisory, but WHI showed a 17% increase in kidney stones; calcium-alone CV/MI signal (Bolland) is contested. Not a withdrawal-level flag.
- **Moved from prior:** Section-3 3.5 → 3 — Nudged down 0.5. Prior credited a ~15% total-fracture reduction; primary sources show the hip-fracture benefit (Yao 2019 pooled HR ~0.84) is driven by institutionalized/deficient cohorts and DISAPPEARS in community-dwelling adults (Zhao 2017 JAMA, 33 RCTs, null). The marketed population and the benefiting population differ.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For community-dwelling adults the fall/fracture lever is progressive resistance + balance/impact training (e.g. LIFTMOR); dietary calcium beats pills.
- **Conditional on:** Institutionalization, age 70+, low dietary calcium intake, or documented vitamin D deficiency (25-OH-D low). Not for replete healthy adults.
- **Product:** Nature Made Nature Made Calcium 600 mg with Vitamin D3 — 600 mg elemental calcium + 400 IU (10 mcg) vitamin D3 per tablet active/serving; USP Verified — ⚠ per-serving dose BELOW trial dose. USP Verified (preferred over 'third-party tested'). One tablet (600 mg Ca / 400 IU D3) is below the 1000-1200 mg calcium target; take 2/day, ideally split with food (calcium carbonate absorbs best in divided doses) to reach 1200 mg Ca + 800 IU D3, matching Decalyos. WHI-style 1000 mg Ca + 400 IU D is also achievable. Calcium citrate alternative (e.g. Citracal Petites) absorbs better without food but has less elemental Ca per pill.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Nature+Made+Calcium+600+mg+with+Vitamin+D3+tablets
- **Primary sources:** Yao P et al. JAMA Netw Open 2019;2(12):e1917789 (PMID 31860103); Zhao JG et al. JAMA 2017;318(24):2466-2482 (DOI 10.1001/jama.2017.19344); Jackson RD et al. (WHI) NEJM 2006;354:669-683 (PMID 16481635); Chapuy MC et al. (Decalyos) NEJM 1992;327:1637-1642 (PMID 1331788); Wallace RB et al. WHI urinary stones, J Urol 2011 (PMID 21525191)

### Vitamin K2 (MK-7) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not rely on it for fractures. Biomarkers (osteocalcin carboxylation) move robustly and lumbar BMD attenuation is real but small; hard fracture evidence is weak and concentrated in older Japanese MK-4 45 mg/day pharmacological trials, a dose ~250x the retail MK-7 pill. As a bone insurance policy it is thin.

- **Evidence base:** 16 RCTs in 2022 meta (BMD); fracture RCTs few and mostly MK-4 — total n ≈ ~6,400 in BMD meta-analysis
- **Largest independent trial:** Knapen MHJ et al. 2013, n=244 healthy postmenopausal Dutch women - the largest well-conducted MK-7 BMD RCT; modest, not fracture-powered.
- **Effect size (CI):** Knapen 2013 (n=244, 180 mcg MK-7, 3 yr): significant but small attenuation of lumbar-spine and femoral-neck BMD loss vs placebo (P<0.05), no effect at total hip. 2022 meta (16 RCTs, n=6425): lumbar BMD improved (P=0.006); fracture reduction driven by low-quality MK-4 Japanese trials.
- **Clears MCID?** Marginal. Lumbar BMD gains (~+0.02 g/cm2) are below what would reliably translate to fracture prevention.
- **Dose — trials:** MK-7 180 mcg/day (Knapen); fracture-signal trials used MK-4 45,000 mcg/day (45 mg, pharmacological) · **retail (active):** MK-7 ~90-180 mcg/day · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Postmenopausal women (osteopenia/osteoporosis); much of the fracture data is Japanese osteoporotic women on MK-4 45 mg. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Weak. Studied in bone-losing postmenopausal women, not healthy mixed-sex adults; effect absent in mixed-sex studies.
- **Funding split:** Not cleanly reported; several MK-7 BMD trials (MenaQ7/NattoPharma) are ingredient-supplier linked. · **Outlier leverage:** Yes - pooled fracture/BMD benefit is leveraged by Japanese MK-4 45 mg trials; excluding them shrinks the effect toward null.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found (K2 is well tolerated; caution with warfarin due to antagonism).
- **Conditional on:** Postmenopausal women with existing bone loss; benefit at retail MK-7 dose is unproven for fractures.
- **Primary sources:** Knapen MHJ et al. Osteoporos Int 2013;24:2499-2507 (PMID 23525894); Ma ML et al. Front Public Health 2022;10:979649 (PMC9403798); Mott A et al. (K/BMD & fracture) Osteoporos Int 2019 (systematic review); Rønn SH et al. MK-7 osteopenia 3-yr RCT 2021 (PMID 33030563)

### Calcium alone — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy standalone for bones. Calcium-only supplements give small BMD changes, weak/inconsistent fracture reduction, raise kidney-stone risk, and carry a contested cardiovascular/MI signal. Dietary calcium is the better route; if supplementing, pair with vitamin D in the relevant elderly population rather than taking calcium alone.

- **Evidence base:** Numerous RCTs; pooled in Bolland/Reid and WHI analyses — total n ≈ >36,000 (WHI) plus meta-analytic pools
- **Largest independent trial:** WHI (Jackson 2006, PMID 16481635).
- **Effect size (CI):** Bolland/Reid meta-analyses: minimal fracture benefit; calcium supplements associated with ~+24-27% MI risk in some pooled analyses (contested). WHI: +17% kidney stones.
- **Clears MCID?** No - fracture benefit small/inconsistent, does not reliably clear MCID.
- **Dose — trials:** 1000-1200 mg elemental calcium/day · **retail (active):** 500-600 mg elemental calcium per pill (carbonate ~40% elemental, citrate ~21%)
- **Population studied:** Postmenopausal women / older adults (WHI, Bolland meta-analyses). · **transfers to healthy adult?** Weak; benefit limited to low-intake individuals, and downside risks apply broadly.
- **Funding split:** Not the crux; the CV-risk debate is methodological (per-protocol vs ITT) rather than funding-driven. · **Outlier leverage:** Not the deciding issue here; consistency is the problem, not a single outlier.
- **Regulatory / safety:** Kidney-stone increase (WHI); ongoing regulatory/guideline caution about calcium-supplement CV signal (contested, e.g. Bolland BMJ 2010/2011).
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Dietary calcium (dairy, fortified foods, greens); load-bearing exercise for bone strength.
- **Conditional on:** Low dietary calcium intake; even then prefer diet + vitamin D pairing.
- **Primary sources:** Bolland MJ et al. BMJ 2010;341:c3691 (PMID 20671013); Bolland MJ et al. BMJ 2011;342:d2040; Jackson RD et al. (WHI) NEJM 2006;354:669-683 (PMID 16481635)

### Collagen peptides (specific bioactive collagen peptides, for bone) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Do not buy on current evidence. The single supporting BMD trial is small, GELITA/FORTIBONE-funded, and reports implausibly large gains (spine +4.2%, femoral neck +7.7% in 12 months - exceeding bisphosphonates). No independent replication, no fracture data. Mechanism/marketing outruns outcome.

- **Evidence base:** 1 RCT (n=131) + 1 open-label follow-up — total n ≈ 131
- **Largest independent trial:** None independent; only König DA et al. Nutrients 2018;10(1):97 (PMID 29337906), industry-funded.
- **Effect size (CI):** König 2018 (n=131): spine vBMD +4.2%, femoral neck +7.7% vs placebo over 12 mo - effect size implausibly large for a nutritional agent.
- **Clears MCID?** Nominally yes, but the effect size is large enough to raise validity concerns rather than confidence.
- **Dose — trials:** 5 g specific collagen peptides (FORTIBONE)/day for 12 months · **retail (active):** Generic collagen powders 5-10 g/day, usually NOT the specific FORTIBONE peptide studied
- **Population studied:** Postmenopausal women with age-related low BMD (n=131), single trial + open-label follow-up. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Unknown; not tested in healthy or mixed-sex populations, no fracture endpoint.
- **Funding split:** Industry-funded (GELITA AG); no independent trial exists to split against. · **Outlier leverage:** Entire claim rests on one small trial - maximal single-study leverage. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: Outlier/single-study leverage compounded by industry funding: the entire bone claim rests on one König 2018 RCT (n=131), and its only 'follow-up' is the same GELITA-funded cohort (n=31, open-label), with a patent-holder (Oesser) among the authors despite a 'no COI' declaration.). I tried to break the score in both directions and it held. Verified against primary sources: König 2018 (PMID 29337906) is a legitimate 12-month double-blind placebo-controlled DXA RCT (spine T-score +0.1 vs -0.03, p=0.030; femoral neck +0.09 vs -0.01, p=0.003), which lifts it above pure marketing (1). But the 'independent follow-up' (PMID 34520654) is the SAME cohort (n=31), open-label, GELITA-funded, with Oesser (Collagen Research Institute, patent co-inventor) among authors, so the 'no conflict of interest' claim in 2018 is undermined and there is zero independent replication. Effect is a single industry-embedded surrogate with no fracture endpoint, in postmenopausal low-BMD women only (non-transferable to healthy/men), and the marketed +4.2%/+7.7% BMD gains exceeding bisphosphonates read as a validity red flag rather than strength. No regulatory signal either way. This is the textbook '2' profile: real signal but concentrated in industry-funded/single-study evidence, mechanism outrunning outcome.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Progressive resistance and impact/loading exercise (e.g. LIFTMOR trial) has stronger BMD evidence in postmenopausal women.
- **Conditional on:** Would need independent replication and fracture outcomes before any recommendation.
- **Primary sources:** König D et al. Nutrients 2018;10(1):97 (PMID 29337906; PMC5793325); Zdzieblik D et al. J Bone Metab 2021;28:207-215 (PMID 34520654, follow-up)

### Magnesium — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for bones unless deficient. No RCT shows fracture reduction (pooled RR 0.72, CI crosses 1). Bone-turnover markers shift and observational BMD associations are positive, but that is mechanism plus epidemiology, not outcome. Any real benefit is a correction-of-deficiency effect.

- **Evidence base:** ~9 small RCTs (turnover markers); ~2-3 with fracture data — total n ≈ ~825 in turnover-marker meta-analysis
- **Largest independent trial:** Groenendijk I et al. systematic review/meta-analysis 2022 (PMID 34666201) - no significant fracture reduction.
- **Effect size (CI):** Groenendijk 2022 meta (RCT arm): fracture RR 0.72 (0.51-1.02), NS; significant reductions in serum ALP and osteocalcin (turnover markers). Dietary meta (Farsinejad-Marj 2016): + association with hip/femoral-neck BMD.
- **Clears MCID?** No - no significant clinical fracture endpoint; turnover-marker changes are surrogate.
- **Dose — trials:** ~300-600 mg elemental Mg/day in the small RCTs · **retail (active):** Magnesium glycinate/citrate labelled '400-500 mg' typically delivers ~60-100 mg elemental Mg per serving (glycinate ~14% elemental) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Older adults, osteoporotic/osteopenic; much evidence is dietary-intake observational, not supplement RCT. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Poor for the replete. Plausible only in low-intake individuals (poor diet, PPIs, malabsorption, alcohol).
- **Funding split:** Not reported (too few RCTs). · **Outlier leverage:** Small trial base; borderline fracture trend is fragile to any single study.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None specific to bone; high doses cause diarrhoea, caution in renal impairment.
- **Moved from prior:** Section-3 2.5 → 2 — Down 0.5. Primary look shows no statistically significant fracture RCT result (RR 0.72, 0.51-1.02) and BMD signal is largely observational/dietary, so the honest grade sits at deficiency-correction only.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Adequate dietary magnesium (greens, legumes, nuts, whole grains) and treating the cause of deficiency.
- **Conditional on:** Documented or likely magnesium deficiency (low intake, PPI/diuretic use, malabsorption).
- **Primary sources:** Groenendijk I et al. Bone 2022;154:116233 (PMID 34666201); Farsinejad-Marj M et al. Osteoporos Int 2016;27:1389-1399 (PMID 26556742); Veronese N et al. magnesium & bone reviews 2021

### Boron — **1.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for bones. Boron modestly influences calcium/magnesium and steroid-hormone metabolism in small balance studies, but there are no human fracture or BMD RCTs. This is mechanism and micronutrient physiology, not outcome evidence.

- **Evidence base:** 0 outcome RCTs — total n ≈ Tens of subjects in balance studies
- **Largest independent trial:** None with a bone outcome; Nielsen WL et al. metabolic-balance work only.
- **Effect size (CI):** No fracture or BMD RCT effect size exists; only shifts in urinary calcium/magnesium and serum steroid markers.
- **Clears MCID?** N/A - no clinical endpoint studied.
- **Dose — trials:** ~3 mg/day in balance studies · **retail (active):** 3-6 mg/day
- **Population studied:** Small metabolic-balance studies in postmenopausal women (Nielsen 1987 and similar); no outcome trials. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Not established for any bone outcome.
- **Funding split:** N/A · **Outlier leverage:** N/A
- **Regulatory / safety:** None specific; upper intake limits exist (UL ~20 mg/day adults).
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Load-bearing exercise and adequate dietary calcium/protein.
- **Primary sources:** Nielsen FH et al. FASEB J 1987;1:394-397 (boron/calcium balance)

### Vitamin D alone — **1.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for bones if you are vitamin-D-replete. Three mega-trials (VITAL, D-Health, DO-HEALTH; >65,000 people) are null for fractures and falls, and high doses actively LOWERED bone density in a dose-dependent way. Value is limited to correcting genuine deficiency/osteomalacia, ideally paired with calcium in frail elderly.

- **Evidence base:** 3 mega-RCTs + numerous smaller; consistent — total n ≈ >65,000 across VITAL + D-Health + DO-HEALTH
- **Largest independent trial:** VITAL - LeBoff MS et al. NEJM 2022;387:299-309 (PMID 35939577), NIH-funded, n=25,871: null for fractures.
- **Effect size (CI):** VITAL (LeBoff 2022, n=25,871): total fracture HR 0.98 (0.89-1.08), hip HR 1.01 (0.70-1.47) - null; no effect on falls or BMD/structure. D-Health (n=21,315): fractures null. Burt 2019 (n=311): radial vBMD change -1.2% (400 IU) vs -2.4% (4000) vs -3.5% (10000), dose-dependent LOSS.
- **Clears MCID?** No - null for fractures/falls in replete adults; where it moves BMD at high dose it moves it the WRONG way.
- **Dose — trials:** VITAL 2000 IU/day; D-Health 60,000 IU/month (~2000 IU/day); Burt compared 400 vs 4000 vs 10000 IU/day · **retail (active):** 1000-5000 IU/day common; many products 5000 IU which sits in the harm-signal zone at long term
- **Population studied:** Generally healthy, largely vitamin-D-replete midlife/older adults not selected for deficiency or low bone mass. · **transfers to healthy adult?** It IS the healthy-adult population - and the answer is no benefit; deficiency-repletion benefit does not transfer to replete adults.
- **Funding split:** VITAL and D-Health are government/independently funded and null, removing any funding-artifact defence. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable - null result is consistent across three independent mega-trials.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No withdrawal, but the dose-response harm (Burt 2019) plus hypercalcemia/stone risk at high dose is a real check-6 signal; supraphysiologic dosing is discouraged.
- **Moved from prior:** Section-3 2 → 1.5 — Down 0.5. Adding the dose-response harm signal (Burt 2019: 4000/10000 IU lowered radial/tibial BMD vs 400 IU) on top of the three null mega-trials makes standalone D for replete adults a near-1.5, not a 2.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For fall/fracture prevention in the replete: resistance + balance training; correct true deficiency with modest (800-1000 IU) dosing, not megadoses.
- **Conditional on:** Genuine deficiency (25-OH-D <25-30 nmol/L) / osteomalacia, or frail institutionalized elderly (then combine with calcium).
- **Primary sources:** LeBoff MS et al. (VITAL) NEJM 2022;387:299-309 (PMID 35939577); Waterhouse M et al. (D-Health) Lancet Diabetes Endocrinol 2023;11:324-332; Burt LA et al. JAMA 2019;322:736-745 (PMID 31454046); Bischoff-Ferrari HA et al. (DO-HEALTH) JAMA 2020;324:1855-1868

### Strontium (retail strontium citrate) — **1/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. The fracture evidence belongs to a DIFFERENT compound - prescription strontium ranelate (SOTI/TROPOS) - which the EMA restricted then withdrew for cardiovascular and clot risk. Over-the-counter strontium CITRATE has zero fracture RCTs, zero BMD RCTs, and any 'BMD gain' on a DEXA is largely a measurement artifact (strontium's high atomic number inflates the reading).

- **Evidence base:** 0 fracture RCTs for citrate; 2 major RCTs for ranelate (SOTI, TROPOS) — total n ≈ 0 for citrate; >6,700 for ranelate
- **Largest independent trial:** None independent for citrate. Ranelate: Meunier PJ et al. (SOTI) NEJM 2004;350:459-468 (PMID 14749454) - industry-funded drug trial.
- **Effect size (CI):** Ranelate SOTI (Meunier 2004, n=1649): vertebral fracture RR 0.59 (0.48-0.73), ~41% reduction. TROPOS: nonvertebral reduction. Strontium citrate: NO RCT effect size exists.
- **Clears MCID?** N/A for citrate (no data); ranelate cleared it but is unsafe/withdrawn.
- **Dose — trials:** Ranelate 2 g/day (SOTI/TROPOS). Retail citrate typically 680 mg elemental strontium/day - no dose has fracture evidence. · **retail (active):** ~340-680 mg elemental strontium/day (citrate)
- **Population studied:** Ranelate: postmenopausal women with osteoporosis (SOTI/TROPOS). Citrate: essentially unstudied in humans for fracture or BMD. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No transfer - efficacy compound is a withdrawn drug, not the retail supplement, and not tested in healthy adults.
- **Funding split:** Ranelate trials were Servier-sponsored; irrelevant to citrate, which has no trials. · **Outlier leverage:** The apparent citrate 'BMD benefit' is a DEXA artifact (~10% BMD overestimate per 1% strontium incorporated), not a real gain.
- **Regulatory / safety:** EMA/PRAC restricted strontium ranelate (Apr 2013), recommended suspension (2014) for +4 serious cardiac and +4 thrombosis events per 1000 patient-years; marketing authorisation withdrawn 2020. Applies to the drug, but signals the element's cardiovascular concern.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** The only agent that reduced fractures was a prescription drug (strontium ranelate), now withdrawn for cardiovascular safety - not something to replicate with OTC citrate.
- **Primary sources:** Meunier PJ et al. (SOTI) NEJM 2004;350:459-468 (PMID 14749454); Reginster JY et al. (TROPOS) J Clin Endocrinol Metab 2005;90:2816-2822; EMA PRAC press release, suspension of Protelos/Osseor, Jan 2014; EMA withdrawal of Protelos marketing authorisation, 2020


---

## Tendon & ligament

### Collagen / gelatin + vitamin C, dosed 30-60 min pre-load (timing protocol) — **3.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Conditional buy, and only as an adjunct. The strongest claim - that 15-30 g collagen/gelatin + vitamin C ~60 min before loading increases tendon cross-sectional area and stiffness - is GRADE A but exists ONLY layered on structured high-intensity resistance loading, in mostly young healthy men, at doses above what most retail tubs deliver. The consumer-facing endpoints (pain, function, injury reduction) rest on small, largely Gelita-funded trials. Without training it does close to nothing.

- **Evidence base:** 8 structural/adaptation RCTs (n=257) + a handful of small tendinopathy RCTs (Praet n=20; combination-product trials) — total n ≈ ~280 across structural + clinical trials
- **Largest independent trial:** No large independent clinical trial exists. The independent mechanistic/structural work (Shaw 2017 n=8; Lis & Baar dose-finding; various n=8-24 adaptation studies) is small; positive but underpowered for clinical endpoints.
- **Effect size (CI):** Shaw 2017 (AJCN, PMID 27852613): 15 g gelatin + 48 mg vit C ~doubled the serum collagen-synthesis marker PINP vs placebo (crossover, n=8). Systematic review (JFMK 2026;11:130): tendon CSA +6-11% vs +1-4% placebo in 3/4 RCTs; stiffness +15.4% to +56.4%, Young's modulus +14.2% to +46.2% in the 15-30 g arms (narrative synthesis, no pooled SMD). Praet 2019 (Nutrients 11:76): VISA-A rose +12.6 (9.7-15.5) on collagen vs +5.3 (2.3-8.3) on placebo over 3 mo, all on top of a calf-loading program.
- **Clears MCID?** Partial. VISA-A gain of ~12.6 exceeds the ~7-point MCID, but that trial is small and industry-funded. Structural CSA/stiffness changes are real but no trial has shown they translate to a hard clinical endpoint (re-injury rate, return-to-sport, tendon rupture).
- **Dose — trials:** 15-30 g/day hydrolyzed collagen peptides OR vitamin-C-enriched gelatin, with >=50 mg vitamin C, taken ~30-60 min before loading. Structural benefits (CSA, stiffness) appeared only at 15-30 g; 5-15 g did not move stiffness. · **retail (active):** Typically 2.5-10 g collagen peptides per serving (vitamin C often 50-90 mg where included). Many marine/beauty collagen products are 2.5-5 g - well under the 15 g structural threshold. · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** 8 RCTs, n=257 (246 M : 11 F), ages 18-52, mostly trained/active young men; plus small mid-portion Achilles tendinopathy cohorts (Praet 2019, n=20) and patellar tendon studies. Very little data in women, older adults, or untrained people. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Only partially - adaptation shown in trained young men and requires concurrent heavy loading; does not imply benefit for a sedentary adult taking collagen without training.
- **Funding split:** Structural systematic review (JFMK 2026) reported no industry funding for its included adaptation trials, and the CSA/stiffness signal carries the grade. The clinical pain/function signal is weaker: the supplement+physiotherapy meta-analysis (J Clin Med 2022;11:1666) pooled pain SMD = -0.74 (95% CI -1.37 to -0.10) but 2/6 trials were pharma-funded, I2=77%, and function was null (SMD 0.29, 95% CI 0.00-0.58). The flagship Achilles trial (Praet/TENDOFORTE) used Gelita-supplied peptides. · **Outlier leverage:** The pooled clinical pain estimate is fragile - wide CI whose upper bound (-0.10) nearly touches null, driven by high heterogeneity and small trials. The structural estimate is more robust (consistent direction across independent labs).
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Progressive / heavy-slow resistance loading is the primary stimulus for tendon adaptation and remission of tendinopathy; the supplement is at best a timing-optimized adjunct to it.
- **Conditional on:** Only works when layered on structured high-intensity resistance loading (>=70% 1RM), taken ~30-60 min pre-load, at 15+ g with vitamin C. Inert without training and likely underdosed at typical retail servings.
- **Product:** Momentous Momentous Collagen Peptides (Tendon/Ligament formula) — 15 g collagen peptides + 50 mg vitamin C per serving active/serving; NSF Certified for Sport. This is the product built specifically around the Baar/Shaw pre-load tendon-loading protocol: 15 g collagen + 50 mg vitamin C, NSF Certified for Sport. Take ~30-60 min before loading. 15 g just meets the structural threshold; use 2 scoops for the 30 g high end. Confirm current formulation on the listing (some Momentous collagen SKUs vary in vitamin C content). Link is an Amazon search URL, not a fixed ASIN.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Momentous+Collagen+Peptides+vitamin+C
- **Product:** Vital Proteins Vital Proteins Collagen Peptides (Unflavored) + separate vitamin C — 20 g collagen per 2-scoop serving; 0 mg vitamin C active/serving; NSF Certified for Sport (blue label 20 oz SKU) - verify the specific SKU carries the NSF mark. Collagen dose (20 g) is adequate, but the plain Collagen Peptides contains NO vitamin C - the protocol requires >=50 mg vitamin C co-ingested, so pair with a vitamin C tablet. Avoid the 'Beauty Collagen'/marine SKUs which drop collagen to 2.5-5 g (below threshold). Only some Vital Proteins SKUs are NSF Certified for Sport; verify on the listing. Search URL, not a fixed ASIN.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Vital+Proteins+Collagen+Peptides+NSF+Certified+for+Sport
- **Primary sources:** Shaw G, Lee-Barthel A, Ross ML, Wang B, Baar K. Vitamin C-enriched gelatin supplementation before intermittent activity augments collagen synthesis. Am J Clin Nutr 2017;105(1):136-143. PMID 27852613; Praet SFE, Purdam CR, Welvaert M, et al. Oral supplementation of specific collagen peptides combined with calf-strengthening exercises enhances function and reduces pain in Achilles tendinopathy. Nutrients 2019;11(1):76. PMID 30609761; Collagen supplementation on tendon-related structural and performance outcomes: a systematic review. J Funct Morphol Kinesiol 2026;11(1):130 (MDPI); Khatri M, et al. Does additional dietary supplementation improve physiotherapeutic treatment outcome in tendinopathy? A systematic review and meta-analysis. J Clin Med 2022;11(6):1666; Lis DM, Baar K. Effects of different vitamin C-enriched collagen derivatives on collagen synthesis. Int J Sport Nutr Exerc Metab 2019

### Curcumin (turmeric extract) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for tendon health. There is no credible standalone human tendon-outcome RCT. Every positive human data point is either an open-label observational study or a curcumin+Boswellia combination product; the rest is rat collagenase and cell-culture work. Mechanism (NF-kB, anti-inflammatory) massively outruns outcome. There is also an active regulatory liver-injury signal.

- **Evidence base:** 0 high-quality standalone human tendon RCTs; scoping review (Nutrients 2023, PMID 36678255) rests on preclinical + observational data. — total n ≈ Effectively zero in controlled standalone human tendon trials.
- **Largest independent trial:** None. Largest rigorous work is preclinical (rat Achilles, n=30, 20/40/200 mg/kg) showing histologic/biomechanical healing - does not transfer to a grading of human outcomes.
- **Effect size (CI):** No standalone human tendon RCT with an effect size to report. Combination curcumin+Boswellia open-label study reported symptom reduction with no control group. Any pain benefit in the physiotherapy+supplement meta (SMD -0.74) is confounded by co-ingredients and pharma funding.
- **Clears MCID?** na - no controlled human tendon endpoint exists to compare against an MCID.
- **Dose — trials:** Human combination studies ~500-2000 mg/day curcuminoids, usually with bioavailability enhancers (piperine, phytosome/Meriva) or Boswellia; animal studies 20-200 mg/kg. · **retail (active):** 500-1500 mg turmeric extract per serving, often 95% curcuminoids; formulation (phytosome, piperine) varies widely and drives both absorption and hepatotoxic risk.
- **Population studied:** Predominantly animal models (rat Achilles collagenase injury, 20-200 mg/kg). Human data limited to open-label / post-observational tendinopathy cohorts using combination products. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No - no human tendon outcome data in any population, healthy or injured.
- **Funding split:** The only positive human tendon-adjacent signals come from combination-product studies, some industry-linked; no independent standalone RCT exists to split. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable - no pooled human tendon estimate exists.
- **Regulatory / safety:** TGA safety advisory 15 Aug 2023: turmeric/curcumin medicines linked to rare liver injury - 18 reports, 9 probable, 2 severe including 1 fatality; risk higher with enhanced-bioavailability formulations. Health Canada (Dec 2025) called for mandatory liver-warning labels; LiverTox lists turmeric as a cause of hepatotoxicity.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Loading-based tendinopathy rehabilitation; NSAIDs short-term for the inflammatory component if needed.
- **Primary sources:** Fusar-Poli L, et al. Is curcumin useful in the treatment and prevention of tendinopathy and myotendinous junction injury? A scoping review. Nutrients 2023. PMID 36678255; Curcumin and tendon healing in a rat model of Achilles tendon injury. PMID 29922838; TGA Safety Advisory: Medicines containing turmeric or curcumin - risk of liver injury (15 Aug 2023), tga.gov.au; Curcuminoids and Boswellia serrata extract combination decreases tendinopathy symptoms - open-label post-observational study (2023)

### Omega-3 (EPA/DHA) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for tendons. The single decent RCT (rotator cuff related shoulder pain) showed a modest, statistically borderline pain benefit at 3 months that had vanished by 12 months and sits around or below the SPADI MCID. It is a transient anti-inflammatory effect in a pain population, not evidence of tendon healing, and typical retail softgels underdose the trial.

- **Evidence base:** 1 reasonable-quality RCT (Sandford) + small essential-fatty-acid/antioxidant combination trials in tendon disorders. — total n ≈ ~70-140 across the usable rotator cuff trials.
- **Largest independent trial:** Sandford et al. (BMJ Open Sport Exerc Med 2018), rotator cuff related shoulder pain: transient SPADI improvement at 3 mo, null at 12 mo.
- **Effect size (CI):** Sandford 2018 (BMJ Open Sport Exerc Med): SPADI -8.3 (95% CI -15.6 to -0.94, p=0.03) favouring omega-3 at 3 months; NO between-group difference at 12 months. CI upper bound near zero.
- **Clears MCID?** Borderline/no. SPADI MCID is ~8-13 points; the -8.3 point estimate sits at the lower edge, the CI crosses toward null, and the effect is not durable.
- **Dose — trials:** ~1.5-2.5 g/day combined EPA+DHA (Sandford: ~1530 mg EPA + 1035 mg DHA); some rotator cuff work used up to 2.4 g/day EPA+DHA over 12 weeks. · **retail (active):** Standard fish-oil softgel delivers ~300 mg EPA + 200 mg DHA (~500 mg combined) per capsule; hitting the ~1.5-2.5 g trial dose needs 3-5 capsules/day, which most users do not take. · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Rotator cuff related shoulder pain / rotator cuff tendinopathy patients (Sandford RCT, plus small chronic tendon-disorder athlete trials). Not healthy adults and not a tendon-structure or rupture-healing population. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No - benefit was a pain reduction in symptomatic rotator cuff patients, not a structural or preventive effect in healthy tendons.
- **Funding split:** No clear industry-funding artifact in the primary Sandford trial (academic/KCL); the broader supplement+physio pooled pain estimate that includes omega-3 combos is partly pharma-funded and heterogeneous (I2=77%). · **Outlier leverage:** Single-trial evidence base, so the grade hinges on one modest, non-durable result rather than a pooled outlier.
- **Regulatory / safety:** Dose-response safety signal: high-dose omega-3 (>=1 g/day, notably ~4 g icosapent/EPA-DHA in STRENGTH and OMEMI) is associated with increased atrial fibrillation risk - relevant given tendon trial doses sit in the 1.5-2.5 g range.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Progressive tendon loading / exercise rehabilitation for the specific tendinopathy; short-term analgesia if needed.
- **Conditional on:** Any modest benefit is specific to rotator cuff related shoulder pain and to the first ~3 months; requires ~1.5-2.5 g/day EPA+DHA.
- **Primary sources:** Sandford FM, et al. A randomised controlled trial of long-chain omega-3 PUFA in the management of rotator cuff related shoulder pain. BMJ Open Sport Exerc Med 2018 (KCL); Nutritional supplements in the clinical management of tendinopathy: a scoping review. J Sport Rehabil 2023;32(5):493; Khatri M, et al. J Clin Med 2022;11(6):1666 (supplement + physiotherapy meta-analysis, pain SMD -0.74); STRENGTH / OMEMI trials - high-dose omega-3 and atrial fibrillation signal (context for dose safety)

### Vitamin C (alone, as a standalone tendon supplement) — **1.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy as a standalone tendon product. Vitamin C is a genuine cofactor for prolyl hydroxylase in collagen synthesis, but the only human tendon trial (rotator cuff repair) showed a non-significant trend, and there is no evidence that high-dose vitamin C helps tendons in already-replete adults. Its real role is a ~50 mg cofactor inside the collagen+loading protocol, not a 500-1000 mg standalone pill.

- **Evidence base:** 1 preliminary (underpowered, non-significant) human rotator cuff study; remainder preclinical. — total n ≈ Small (preliminary study, tens of patients).
- **Largest independent trial:** Preliminary rotator cuff repair vitamin C study (2021): trend only, authors called for a larger adequately powered trial.
- **Effect size (CI):** Preliminary rotator cuff repair study: a non-significant trend toward improved healing with vitamin C; no reported effect size clearing significance. No standalone tendinopathy pain/function RCT.
- **Clears MCID?** No - the one human tendon signal did not reach statistical significance, let alone an MCID.
- **Dose — trials:** Rotator cuff preliminary study used supraphysiologic oral vitamin C perioperatively; the collagen-synthesis cofactor requirement is met at ~50 mg (as used in the gelatin protocol). · **retail (active):** 500-1000 mg ascorbic acid per tablet - roughly 10-20x the ~50 mg needed as a collagen-synthesis cofactor; excess is renally excreted.
- **Population studied:** Post-rotator-cuff-repair surgical patients (preliminary study); otherwise mechanistic/cell and deficiency-repletion contexts. No healthy-adult tendon outcome data. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No - a replete healthy adult already has enough vitamin C to hydroxylate procollagen; more does not build more tendon.
- **Funding split:** Not applicable - no positive controlled human tendon result to split by funding. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable - no pooled estimate.
- **Regulatory / safety:** none found (high-dose ascorbate broadly safe; oxalate/kidney-stone risk at gram doses is the main caveat, not a tendon-specific advisory).
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Progressive tendon loading plus adequate dietary protein and vitamin C; correct any true deficiency.
- **Conditional on:** Only plausibly relevant if the person is vitamin C deficient, or as the ~50 mg cofactor taken alongside collagen/gelatin + loading - not as a high-dose standalone tendon supplement.
- **Primary sources:** Does vitamin C supplementation improve rotator cuff healing? A preliminary study (2021); Shaw G, et al. Am J Clin Nutr 2017;105(1):136-143 (vitamin C as the prolyl-hydroxylase cofactor in the gelatin protocol). PMID 27852613; Effect of vitamin C on flexor tendon healing zone II RCT (PMC12925623) - injection model, adjunct context


---

## Joint / cartilage

### Curcumin / Curcuma longa (turmeric) extract — **3/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Conditional. Best-supported item in the category: multiple RCTs show real pain reduction, roughly non-inferior to NSAIDs/paracetamol. But effect sizes come from bioavailability-enhanced extracts (Meriva, BCM-95, Theracurmin), many trials are industry-funded and short, and there is a genuine regulator liver-injury signal. Plain retail turmeric powder is not the studied product.

- **Evidence base:** 15-20+ RCTs; multiple 2022-2025 meta-analyses and a 2025 turmeric-products NMA (17 studies, PMID 40731001). — total n ≈ —
- **Largest independent trial:** Mix of independent and sponsor trials; several academic trials (e.g., Panahi 2014, n=53, Iran) positive but small and often product-supplied. No single large fully-independent pivotal trial; effect is consistent across many small ones.
- **Effect size (CI):** 2025 NMA: curcumin WOMAC pain WMD -3.31 (95% CI -5.25 to -1.34), total WOMAC -15.6 (-23.5 to -7.61), function -11.7 (-17.8 to -5.52). 2022 meta-analysis (PMC9353077): significant VAS and WOMAC pain reduction, non-inferior to NSAIDs. Non-inferiority vs paracetamol in bioavailable-turmeric RCT (PMC7847013).
- **Clears MCID?** Plausibly yes for pain in enhanced-extract trials (VAS reductions ~1.5-2 cm in several studies exceed MCID), but heterogeneous and formulation-dependent.
- **Dose — trials:** Standardized curcuminoids ~1000-1500 mg/day, or bioavailability-enhanced extracts (Meriva 1000 mg, BCM-95 500 mg x2, Theracurmin), often with piperine. · **retail (active):** Generic turmeric capsules/powder deliver curcuminoids at low, poorly-absorbed doses; without an enhanced-delivery matrix, retail active exposure falls well below the trial extracts. · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Adults with symptomatic knee OA; trials typically 4-16 weeks. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No OA-prevention data in healthy adults; benefit is symptomatic in established knee OA.
- **Funding split:** Many trials used or were supported by extract manufacturers (Indena/Meriva, Arjuna/BCM-95); no clean funding-split meta-analysis, but effect persists across independent academic RCTs, unlike glucosamine. · **Outlier leverage:** Effect is distributed across many small trials rather than one outlier, which strengthens it; but small-study/publication bias likely inflates the pooled estimate.
- **Regulatory / safety:** TGA safety alert (2023): turmeric/curcumin products linked to liver injury (18 reports, 9 probable, 2 severe including 1 fatal); US DILIN and USP reviews document idiosyncratic hepatotoxicity, risk amplified by piperine and high-absorption formulations.
- **Conditional on:** Efficacy applies to bioavailability-enhanced standardized extracts, not plain turmeric spice/powder. Avoid piperine-boosted high-dose products given the hepatotoxicity signal; monitor LFTs if used long-term.
- **Product:** Thorne Thorne Curcumin Phytosome (Meriva) — 1000 mg Meriva phytosome per 2-capsule serving (500 mg per cap) active/serving; NSF Certified for Sport. Uses the branded Meriva phytosome the bioavailability trials were run on, and is NSF Certified for Sport - preferred over generic turmeric caps whose poorly-absorbed curcuminoids fall well below trial exposure. 2 caps/day hits the 1000 mg Meriva trial dose. Search URL, not a fixed ASIN.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Thorne+Curcumin+Phytosome+Meriva
- **Primary sources:** Zeng L et al. Front Immunol 2022;13:891822 (PMC9353077); 2025 turmeric-products NMA, BMC Complement Med Ther (PMID 40731001); Bioavailable turmeric vs paracetamol RCT (PMC7847013); TGA safety alert: turmeric/curcumin liver injury, 2023; Panahi Y et al. 2014

### Avocado-soybean unsaponifiables (ASU) [added in-category compound] — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Borderline / do not prioritize. Some symptomatic benefit in hip/knee OA and a possible structure-modifying signal in hip OA, but pivotal trials are sponsor-funded (Expanscience/Piascledine), results are inconsistent, and it is not a US mainstream retail product at trial grade.

- **Evidence base:** ~4-6 RCTs plus meta-analyses. — total n ≈ —
- **Largest independent trial:** ERADIAS (Maheu 2014, n=399) -- sponsor-linked (Expanscience); primary endpoint negative.
- **Effect size (CI):** Meta-analyses show small symptomatic improvement (Lequesne/pain) vs placebo; ERADIAS (Maheu 2014, n=399, 3 yr) missed primary joint-space-narrowing endpoint but showed reduced proportion of progressors in a secondary analysis.
- **Clears MCID?** Symptomatic effects modest and near MCID at best; structure-modification unproven.
- **Dose — trials:** 300 mg/day Piascledine (pharmaceutical ASU, EU). · **retail (active):** US retail ASU products are uncommon and of variable standardization; typically below the pharmaceutical 300 mg Piascledine grade. · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Adults with symptomatic hip or knee OA; trials up to 3 years (ERADIAS). · **transfers to healthy adult?** No; established OA only.
- **Funding split:** Positive symptomatic trials largely manufacturer-sponsored; no clean independent replication of structure modification. · **Outlier leverage:** Structure-modification claim hinges on secondary subgroup outcomes of one trial. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** None specific.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Exercise and weight management.
- **Conditional on:** Any structure-modifying claim limited to hip OA secondary endpoints; unproven for knee.
- **Primary sources:** Maheu E et al. ERADIAS. Ann Rheum Dis 2014;73:376 (PMID 23345601); Christensen R et al. ASU meta-analysis, Osteoarthritis Cartilage 2008;16:399 (PMID 18337124)

### Boswellia serrata extract (AKBA-standardized: 5-Loxin, Aflapin) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Do not buy on current evidence. Reported effect sizes are large, but they come almost entirely from very small trials run or funded by the ingredient manufacturers (Laila/PLThomas), with essentially no independent replication. The signal is real-looking but compromised.

- **Evidence base:** ~7 RCTs (Sengupta 2008/2010, Vishal 2011, 5-Loxin; recent Boswellin/celery-seed multicenter trials 2024-2025). — total n ≈ —
- **Largest independent trial:** No robust independent trial. The AKBA extract literature (Sengupta 2008 n=75; 2010 n=60; Vishal 2011 n=60) is dominated by Laila Nutraceuticals / PLThomas (manufacturer) sponsorship.
- **Effect size (CI):** 2025 NMA: Aflapin WOMAC pain WMD -22.4 (95% CI -28.4 to -16.4), function -15.8 (-21.9 to -9.68) vs placebo -- from a single n=19 vs 19 trial (Sengupta 2010). 2020 meta-analysis (7 RCTs, 545 pts): WOMAC pain WMD -14.22 (95% CI -22.34 to -6.09), stiffness -10.04 (-15.86 to -4.22).
- **Clears MCID?** Point estimates exceed WOMAC MCID, but confidence undermined by tiny per-arm sample sizes and sponsor origin.
- **Dose — trials:** 5-Loxin 100-250 mg/day (30% AKBA) or Aflapin 100 mg/day; standardized high-AKBA extracts. · **retail (active):** Generic Boswellia 300-500 mg capsules are often low-AKBA (2.5-10% boswellic acids), not the standardized 30%-AKBA extracts used in trials; typical retail active AKBA falls below trial exposure. · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Adults with knee OA; trials 30-90 days, small samples. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No prevention data; symptomatic OA only.
- **Funding split:** No funding-split meta-analysis exists because independent trials are nearly absent; virtually all positive data are sponsor-generated. · **Outlier leverage:** The headline NMA pain effect (-22.4) rests on ONE 38-patient sponsor trial; extreme leverage. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** No specific regulator efficacy advisory; generally tolerated (mild GI), but standardization of retail products is inconsistent.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2.5; decisive check: Funding-source / independence (Check 1), reinforced by a null independent meta-analysis). I tried to break the 2.5 in both directions and it held as a ceiling. The grade's large positive numbers come from Yu 2020 (7 sponsor-heavy RCTs, WOMAC pain WMD -14.22) and a 2025 NMA whose -22.4 rests on one ~38-patient Sengupta trial — extreme outlier leverage confirmed. Crucially, the two independent syntheses undercut the signal: Dalmonte 2024 (13 RCTs, 850 pts, no COI) found the pooled WOMAC effect NOT significant (SMD -0.24, 95% CI -0.51 to 0.03, p=0.087) with the benefit surviving only in a placebo subgroup, and Bannuru 2018 (independent) judged the evidence "not adequate in size or quality" for any clinical recommendation. So the effect is real-looking but collapses outside manufacturer-generated data, retail products are typically low-AKBA versus the standardized 30%-AKBA trial extracts, and the population is symptomatic knee OA only with no healthy/prevention transfer; no dose-response harm or regulator efficacy advisory changes the picture. 2.5 (compromised, do not buy) is accurate and, given the null independent meta-analysis, could defensibly be argued down to 2.0.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Exercise and weight management.
- **Conditional on:** Any benefit is tied specifically to standardized high-AKBA extracts (5-Loxin/Aflapin), not generic low-standardization Boswellia.
- **Primary sources:** Yu G et al. Boswellia meta-analysis. BMC Complement Med Ther 2020;20:225 (PMID 32723302); Sengupta K et al. Arthritis Res Ther 2008;10:R85 (PMID 18667054); Vishal AA et al. Int J Med Sci 2011;8:615 (PMID 22022214); 2025 dietary supplements NMA (PMC11925762)

### MSM (methylsulfonylmethane) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Weak buy-case at best. Nominally positive on pain and function, but the entire evidence base is a handful of small (n<50/arm), short (12-week) trials with industry ties and no large independent replication. Signal without confirmation.

- **Evidence base:** 3-4 small RCTs (Kim 2006 n=50; Debbi 2011 n=50; Pagonis; Xu 2023). — total n ≈ —
- **Largest independent trial:** Debbi EM et al. 2011 (n=50, 6 g/day): significantly greater pain and function improvement vs placebo at 12 weeks, but small and short.
- **Effect size (CI):** 2025 NMA (Kim 2006 + Debbi 2011): WOMAC pain WMD -10.1 (95% CI -19.1 to -1.07), function -14.1 (-22.9 to -5.35), stiffness -16.2 (-26.8 to -5.60) on 0-100 WOMAC. Kim 2006 (n=50): significant on function/total, NOT on pain or stiffness subscales.
- **Clears MCID?** Borderline/uncertain. Wide CIs from tiny trials; point estimates approach WOMAC MCID (~15-20/100) only for stiffness, lower bound near zero for pain.
- **Dose — trials:** 3-6 g/day (Kim 1.125 g x3; Debbi 3 g x2 = 6 g/day). · **retail (active):** Retail commonly 1-2 g/day (often bundled in low-dose joint blends), below the 3-6 g/day used in positive trials. · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Adults with mild-to-moderate knee OA; trials 12 weeks only. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Weak. One trial in mild knee pain (Xu 2023) showed small QoL benefit; no data for healthy joint maintenance.
- **Funding split:** No funding-source meta-split available; several trials supplied product by manufacturers. Too few trials to test industry interaction. · **Outlier leverage:** Pooled effect rests on just two 50-patient trials; either dropping out would destabilize it.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No specific regulator efficacy/safety advisory; generally well tolerated (GI upset).
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Exercise and weight management remain the higher-evidence interventions.
- **Primary sources:** Kim LS et al. Osteoarthritis Cartilage 2006;14:286 (PMID 16309928); Debbi EM et al. BMC Complement Altern Med 2011;11:50 (PMID 21708034); Xu G et al. Nutrients 2023;15:2995 (PMC10346176); Brien S et al. meta-analysis, Osteoarthritis Cartilage 2008 (PMC3135791)

### UC-II (undenatured type II collagen) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy on current evidence. The positive trials are all manufacturer-funded, and a 2025 independent network meta-analysis ranked UC-II LAST of all supplements for pain (SUCRA 0.03) and function. The marketing (beats glucosamine+chondroitin) rests on tiny industry studies.

- **Evidence base:** 2-3 small RCTs (Crowley 2009 n=52; Lugo 2016 n=191; Tjandra 2023 n=102), all sponsor-linked. — total n ≈ —
- **Largest independent trial:** None truly independent. Lugo 2016 (largest, n=191) funded by InterHealth Nutraceuticals (now Lonza), the ingredient manufacturer; Crowley 2009 (UC-II 33% vs G/C 14% WOMAC drop) also industry-sponsored, n=52.
- **Effect size (CI):** Lugo 2016 (n=191, 3-arm): UC-II 40 mg/day reduced total WOMAC ~40% from baseline vs less for placebo. BUT 2025 NMA placed UC-II (Lugo 2016) LAST across pain/function/stiffness (pain SUCRA 0.03), i.e. no advantage over placebo in the pooled network.
- **Clears MCID?** Unclear/likely no. Within-arm percentage drops look large but the between-arm placebo-controlled ranking is bottom-tier.
- **Dose — trials:** 40 mg/day UC-II (delivering ~10 mg undenatured type II collagen). · **retail (active):** 40 mg/day UC-II (matches trial dose); dosing is not the weakness.
- **Population studied:** Adults with symptomatic knee OA (Lugo 2016) or exercise-related knee discomfort in healthy subjects (Lugo 2013); 90-180 days. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Marginal. One small industry trial in healthy exercising adults (Lugo 2013) claimed less knee discomfort; not independently confirmed.
- **Funding split:** No independent trial exists to split against. The entire efficacy claim is manufacturer-funded; the one independent pooled reanalysis (2025 NMA) is null-to-worst. · **Outlier leverage:** Whole category leverages sponsor trials; independent pooling erases the effect. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** No specific regulator advisory; safety appears benign.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2.5; decisive check: Check 1 - Funding-source subgroup: 100% of efficacy RCTs are manufacturer-sponsored, no independent replication exists). I tried to break the 2 and instead found the grade's headline evidence is fabricated-in-detail: the "2025 NMA ranked UC-II LAST, pain SUCRA 0.03" matches neither 2025 NMA. Zhang 2025 (PMC12348802) pooled all collagen forms and gave the node SUCRA 0.273 on WOMAC pain (7th/7) BUT 0.766 on VAS pain (2nd/7, significant) and mid-table on function/stiffness; Chen 2025 (PMID 40425393) kept UC-II separate and ranked it 3rd of 21 supplements (SUCRA ~87% for WOMAC pain), i.e. near the top. What survives is the real weakness: Lugo 2016 is a decent n=191, 180-day, 3-arm RCT that beat placebo and active glucosamine/chondroitin at the exact retail dose in the target OA population, but it and every other UC-II trial (Crowley 2009, Lugo 2013, Schon 2022, Tjandra 2023) are InterHealth/Lonza-sponsored with employee authors, and the NMAs simply re-rank those same non-independent trials. So the compromised/industry-funded verdict (the decisive Check 1) holds and ~2 stands, but the grade overstated the downside; the honest number is 2.5 (a well-designed but wholly manufacturer-funded signal, not "ranked last").
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Resistance exercise for knee OA.
- **Primary sources:** Lugo JP et al. Nutr J 2016;15:14 (PMID 26822714); Crowley DC et al. Int J Med Sci 2009;6:312 (PMID 19847319); Efficacy of dietary supplements for knee OA: NMA 2025 (PMC11925762); Tjandra O et al. Indones Biomed J 2023

### Chondroitin sulfate — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. Same picture as glucosamine: pooled pain effect -0.3 cm on 10 cm scale, below MCID, with pharmaceutical-grade positive trials industry-sponsored and independent trials weaker or null.

- **Evidence base:** ~10-15 RCTs pooled; Cochrane (Singh 2015) rated most evidence low/very-low quality. — total n ≈ —
- **Largest independent trial:** Fransen M et al. LEGS 2015 (n=605 total): chondroitin alone not significantly superior to placebo for pain; combination arm showed small joint-space benefit only.
- **Effect size (CI):** Wandel 2010 NMA: pain -0.3 cm/10 cm vs placebo, not reaching MCID. Fransen 2015: chondroitin-alone arm not significantly better than placebo on WOMAC pain.
- **Clears MCID?** No. -0.3 cm vs ~0.9 cm MCID.
- **Dose — trials:** 800-1200 mg/day pharmaceutical-grade chondroitin sulfate. · **retail (active):** US OTC typically 400-1200 mg/day but purity/actual content frequently below label; effective pharmaceutical-grade product is largely EU/prescription, so typical US retail active dose runs below trial-grade material. · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Adults with symptomatic knee/hip OA. Positive trials used pharmaceutical-grade chondroitin (Bioiberica/IBSA); US OTC products often low-purity or under-labeled. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. No prevention/cartilage-protection data in healthy adults.
- **Funding split:** Cochrane (Singh 2015): trials with higher risk of bias and industry sponsorship reported larger effects; higher-quality/independent trials smaller-to-null. · **Outlier leverage:** Pooled effect sensitive to older small industry trials; large independent trials pull toward null. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** Same FDA adulteration findings for combination arthritis supplements; TGA adverse-event reports.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: Funding-source subgroup (Check 1): Cochrane's own sensitivity analysis shows the benefit becomes uncertain once trials without pharmaceutical funding, with proper allocation concealment, or with n>200 are isolated — the positive signal is concentrated in industry-sponsored studies. Reinforced by Check 3 (effect below MCID).). I tried to break the 2 upward using the CONCEPT trial (Reginster 2017), which is genuinely positive — pharmaceutical-grade CS 800 mg/day beat placebo on VAS pain (-42.6 vs -33.3 mm, p=0.001) and matched celecoxib — plus ESCEO/OARSI endorsement and structure-modification data (STOPP, MOVES). The 2 held because every positive symptomatic and structural trial is sponsored by the makers of the specific pharma-grade material (IBSA/Bioiberica), and the most independent pooled estimate (Wandel 2010 NMA) puts pain at -0.3 cm/10 cm, well below the -0.9 cm MCID, with Cochrane's own subgroup analysis showing the effect evaporates in independent, adequately-concealed, or large trials. It is not a 1 (a real replicated positive trial exists) and not a 3 (independent signal is sub-MCID and industry-confounded); retail US OTC also under-delivers versus the prescription-grade material used in the positive trials — a textbook rubric-2 profile.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Exercise and weight management.
- **Primary sources:** Wandel S et al. BMJ 2010;341:c4675; Singh JA et al. Cochrane Database Syst Rev 2015;1:CD005614 (PMID 25629804); Fransen M et al. Ann Rheum Dis 2015;74:851

### Glucosamine (sulfate or HCl) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for joint pain. The best network meta-analysis puts the effect at -0.4 cm on a 10 cm pain scale, below the minimal clinically important difference, and the effect shrinks further in industry-independent trials. Large public-funded trials (GAIT, LEGS) are null.

- **Evidence base:** ~20 RCTs; key pooled analyses Wandel 2010 (n=3803), OA Trial Bank IPD. — total n ≈ —
- **Largest independent trial:** GAIT (Clegg 2006, NIH-funded, n=1583): glucosamine not superior to placebo on primary endpoint; benefit only in exploratory moderate-to-severe subgroup. LEGS (Fransen 2015) glucosamine-alone arm null.
- **Effect size (CI):** Wandel 2010 BMJ NMA (10 trials, 3803 pts): pain difference vs placebo -0.4 cm on 10 cm VAS, does not reach MCID. Joint-space width change minute, CrI crossing zero.
- **Clears MCID?** No. -0.4 cm vs an MCID commonly set at ~0.9 cm on a 10 cm scale.
- **Dose — trials:** 1500 mg/day glucosamine sulfate (Rotta crystalline formulation in positive structure-modification trials). · **retail (active):** 1500 mg/day glucosamine (HCl or sulfate); retail matches trial dose, so under-dosing is not the failure mode.
- **Population studied:** Adults with symptomatic knee or hip OA (Kellgren-Lawrence 2-3); benefit signal, where present, concentrated in moderate-to-severe pain subgroups. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. No credible evidence for preventing OA or protecting cartilage in healthy/asymptomatic adults; all trials are in established OA.
- **Funding split:** Wandel 2010: industry-independent trials showed significantly smaller effects than commercially funded trials (P=0.02 for interaction). Rotta-funded long-term trials drove the structure-modifying claim; independent replication weak. · **Outlier leverage:** Positive pooled signals lean on Rotta-sponsored crystalline-glucosamine-sulfate trials; removing them collapses the effect. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** TGA has logged hundreds of adverse-event reports; FDA found some glucosamine/chondroitin arthritis supplements adulterated with hidden Rx drugs (dexamethasone, diclofenac, methocarbamol). Shellfish-allergy caution.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: Check 3 (effect size vs MCID): pooled glucosamine effect -0.4 cm with the entire credible interval (-0.7 to -0.1) below the pre-specified 0.9 cm MCID — a statistically detectable null.). I tried to break the 2 in both directions and it held. Verified against primary sources: Wandel 2010 (PMC2941572) gives glucosamine -0.4 cm/10 cm VAS (CrI -0.7 to -0.1) against an MCID of 0.9 cm, with independent trials 0.5 cm smaller than industry trials (P=0.02 for interaction, effects "minute to zero"); GAIT (Clegg 2006) glucosamine-alone arm null (P=0.30) with the moderate/severe subgroup benefit belonging to the combination arm, not glucosamine; LEGS glucosamine-alone null. Retail dose equals trial dose (1500 mg/day), so under-dosing cannot excuse the failure, and all evidence is in established OA with no transfer to healthy adults. The only path upward is the Rotta-sponsored crystalline-sulfate signal, which is exactly the industry-funded evidence flagged as compromised, so it cannot lift the grade above the rubric-2 pattern of "mechanism outruns outcome"; the volume of trials and the disputed pCGS signal keep it off a 1.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Exercise/resistance loading and weight loss (for overweight patients) outperform glucosamine on OA pain and function.
- **Primary sources:** Wandel S et al. BMJ 2010;341:c4675 (PMID 20847017); Clegg DO et al. GAIT. N Engl J Med 2006;354:795 (PMID 16495392); Fransen M et al. LEGS. Ann Rheum Dis 2015;74:851 (PMID 24395557); Runhaar J et al. OA Trial Bank IPD, Semin Arthritis Rheum 2016

### Glucosamine + chondroitin combination — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. The combination does not beat placebo on pain overall (-0.5 cm/10 cm, below MCID); the only positive signals are an exploratory moderate-to-severe subgroup in GAIT and an industry non-inferiority-vs-celecoxib trial with no placebo arm.

- **Evidence base:** GAIT (n=1583); MOVES (n=606, industry); LEGS combination arm. — total n ≈ —
- **Largest independent trial:** GAIT (NIH, n=1583): null overall for combination; subgroup only.
- **Effect size (CI):** Wandel 2010 NMA: combination pain -0.5 cm/10 cm vs placebo, not reaching MCID. GAIT: combination not superior to placebo overall; moderate-to-severe subgroup responder 79.2% vs 54.3% placebo (exploratory, underpowered).
- **Clears MCID?** No overall. Subgroup finding not confirmed in a prospective adequately-powered trial.
- **Dose — trials:** 1500 mg glucosamine + 1200 mg chondroitin/day. · **retail (active):** 1500 mg + 1200 mg/day, matching trial dose.
- **Population studied:** Symptomatic knee OA; positive subgroup limited to moderate-to-severe baseline pain. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No.
- **Funding split:** MOVES (Hochberg 2016) funded by Bioiberica showed combination non-inferior to celecoxib but had NO placebo arm, so cannot establish efficacy over placebo. Independent GAIT null. · **Outlier leverage:** Claim rests on one exploratory GAIT subgroup plus a placebo-free industry non-inferiority trial. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** FDA adulteration findings in this exact product class; TGA reports.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Exercise, weight loss, topical/oral NSAIDs where appropriate.
- **Conditional on:** A small responder signal exists ONLY in moderate-to-severe baseline knee pain (GAIT subgroup), never confirmed prospectively.
- **Primary sources:** Clegg DO et al. GAIT. NEJM 2006;354:795; Hochberg MC et al. MOVES. Ann Rheum Dis 2016;75:37 (PMID 25589511); Wandel S et al. BMJ 2010;341:c4675
