## Sleep

### Melatonin (dose & timing) — **3.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Buy for the RIGHT problem: circadian misalignment (delayed sleep-wake phase disorder, jet lag, shift work, non-24, ASD/child sleep). For garden-variety adult primary insomnia it is a weak agent the AASM recommends AGAINST. Use LOW dose (0.5-1 mg) timed hours before target sleep for phase-shifting; high-dose 5-10 mg products are chronobiologically wrong.

- **Evidence base:** 26 RCTs in the 2024 dose-response meta (Cruz-Sanabria); 19 RCTs/1683 subjects in Ferracioli-Oda 2013 — total n ≈ >1600 (2013 pooled); ~1689 observations (2024 dose-response)
- **Largest independent trial:** Wade 2007/2010 prolonged-release melatonin in adults >=55 (Neurim-funded); independent pediatric/DSWPD trials (Gringras MENDS 2017 n=146) confirm circadian use.
- **Effect size (CI):** Sleep onset latency WMD -7.06 min (95% CI -9.75 to -4.37; Ferracioli-Oda 2013). Prolonged-release: subjective SOL -6.30 min, objective SOL -5.05 min (2023 meta, n=4875). Total sleep time +8-13 min. Dose-response peaks ~4 mg.
- **Clears MCID?** For insomnia: NO - a 5-7 min SOL reduction sits below the ~10-min MCID; a null in a suit. For circadian phase disorders: YES - phase-shifting is the mechanism, not the minutes.
- **Dose — trials:** 0.5-5 mg; phase-shift work uses 0.5 mg; insomnia trials 2-5 mg PR · **retail (active):** 3-10 mg immediate-release (typically ABOVE the effective circadian dose; gummies often overshoot)
- **Population studied:** Mixed: primary insomnia adults, DSWPD, jet-lag travelers, children with ASD/ADHD, elderly. Effect is population-specific. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Poor as a hypnotic for a healthy non-circadian-disrupted adult; strong for a healthy traveler crossing time zones.
- **Funding split:** not reported as a formal subgroup; effect not funding-dependent · **Outlier leverage:** Pooled SOL effect robust across many trials; not one-study-driven.
- **Regulatory / safety:** UK MHRA: prescription-only medicine (Circadin), not an OTC supplement. US OTC potency unregulated - Erland & Saxvig found actual content 83% below to 478% above label; CDC MMWR 2022: US pediatric melatonin ingestions rose >500% 2012-2021.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For chronic insomnia the first-line guideline treatment is CBT-I (AASM/ACP), not melatonin.
- **Conditional on:** Grade of 4+ applies only to circadian indications (DSWPD, jet lag, shift work, child/ASD). For adult primary insomnia it is closer to 2.5.
- **Product:** Life Extension Life Extension Melatonin 500 mcg (0.5 mg) — 0.5 mg (500 mcg) per capsule active/serving; none. 0.5 mg is the physiologic circadian/phase-shift dose and sits at the low end of the 0.5-5 mg trial range - deliberately AVOIDS the 3-10 mg retail overshoot common in gummies. For insomnia (2-5 mg) use multiple caps or a 1 mg SKU. Pure Encapsulations Melatonin 0.5 mg is an equivalent clean alternative. Search URL, not a fixed ASIN.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Life+Extension+Melatonin+500+mcg
- **Primary sources:** Ferracioli-Oda E 2013, PLoS One, PMID 23691095 (DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0063773); Cruz-Sanabria F 2024, J Pineal Res, PMID 38888087 (DOI 10.1111/jpi.12985); Sateia MJ 2017 AASM Clinical Practice Guideline, J Clin Sleep Med (weak recommendation AGAINST melatonin for chronic insomnia); Gringras P 2017 MENDS RCT, J Am Acad Child Adolesc Psychiatry; Erland LAE & Saxvig 2017 J Clin Sleep Med (label-content analysis)

### L-tryptophan — **3/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** A real but narrow effect: at >=1 g it reduces wake-after-sleep-onset, best in mild insomnia or long-latency normal sleepers. Modest and dose-gated. Carries a serious historical safety scar (1989 contaminated-batch eosinophilia-myalgia syndrome, >1500 cases, ~30+ deaths - a manufacturing contaminant, not the molecule). Buy only reputable, high-purity product at adequate dose.

- **Evidence base:** Sutanto 2022 meta: 18 articles screened, 4 pooled; plus older Hartmann-era trials — total n ≈ Small pooled sample across 4 meta-analyzed RCTs
- **Largest independent trial:** Historical Hartmann/Schneider-Helmert 1 g trials (independent, academic).
- **Effect size (CI):** Sutanto 2022: WASO shortened ~-81 min per gram (SMD -1.08; 95% CI -1.89 to -0.28; P=0.017). Dose-gated: >=1 g gave WASO 28.9 min vs 56.6 min for <1 g (P=0.001). No significant effect on other sleep components.
- **Clears MCID?** YES for WASO at >=1 g (a ~28-min reduction is clinically meaningful) - but only that endpoint.
- **Dose — trials:** >=1 g at bedtime (older SOL work used 1-4 g) · **retail (active):** 500 mg per capsule typical; many users take <1 g and fall below the effective threshold · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Mild insomnia and normal subjects with longer-than-average sleep latency; some clinical insomnia. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Transfers to healthy long-latency sleepers (studied population); little effect in good sleepers.
- **Funding split:** not reported · **Outlier leverage:** Only 4 trials pooled - the WASO effect could shift with additional trials; treat CI with caution.
- **Regulatory / safety:** 1989-90 EMS outbreak (Showa Denko contaminated batch, 'Peak-X'); FDA import restrictions lifted 2005; product now sold OTC. Caution with serotonergic drugs (serotonin syndrome).
- **Conditional on:** Benefit conditional on >=1 g dose and on mild insomnia / long-latency sleepers.
- **Product:** NOW Foods NOW Foods L-Tryptophan 1000 mg (double-strength tablets) — 1000 mg (1 g) per tablet active/serving; none (NOW is UL/NSF GMP-audited but not NSF Certified for Sport for this SKU). Choosing the 1000 mg single-tablet SKU avoids the common failure mode where users take one 500 mg cap and fall below the 1 g effective dose. Take at bedtime. Search URL, not a fixed ASIN.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=NOW+Foods+L-Tryptophan+1000+mg+tablets
- **Primary sources:** Sutanto CN 2022, Nutr Rev, PMID 33942088 (DOI 10.1093/nutrit/nuab027; WASO -81 min/g); Hartmann E 1974/1977 (L-tryptophan sleep latency, mild insomnia); Belongia EA 1990, NEJM (EMS / L-tryptophan contamination)

### Ashwagandha (KSM-66, Withania somnifera) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Conditional and safety-flagged. Meta-analysis shows a moderate sleep effect, but essentially all trials are industry-funded (Ixoreal/KSM-66), single-region, and short. More important: multiple regulators (TGA 2024 and 2026, MHRA Yellow Card, Netherlands Lareb, NIH LiverTox) have posted liver-injury signals. Efficacy is real-ish; the risk-benefit for a sleep aid is unattractive.

- **Evidence base:** 5 RCTs in Cheah 2021 meta (n=400) — total n ≈ 400 (meta); individual trials 40-150
- **Largest independent trial:** No large independent (non-manufacturer) RCT identified - the central weakness.
- **Effect size (CI):** Pooled SMD on overall sleep -0.59 (95% CI -0.75 to -0.42; Cheah 2021). Larger in the insomnia subgroup, dose >=600 mg/day, >=8 wk. HAM-A anxiety and sleep-onset latency improved (Langade 2019/2021).
- **Clears MCID?** Plausibly yes on PSQI in the insomnia subgroup, but confounded by uniform industry funding.
- **Dose — trials:** 300-600 mg/day root extract (KSM-66), 6-10 wk · **retail (active):** 300-600 mg KSM-66 - retail matches trial dose
- **Population studied:** Adults with insomnia/stress, largely Indian trial sites; healthy stressed adults. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Uncertain; sleep effect strongest in insomnia, weaker/soft in healthy adults.
- **Funding split:** No independent-only pooled estimate exists - the SMD -0.59 is effectively an industry-funded pooled result (Ixoreal supplied material in the key trials). That is the compromise. · **Outlier leverage:** Small meta; a couple of Langade trials carry the effect. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** TGA safety advisory (2024) + second alert (2026) on Withania somnifera and very rare liver injury; MHRA Yellow Card hepatotoxicity reports; Netherlands Lareb alerts 2023/2025 (12 cases to mid-2025); WHO VigiBase 15 hepatobiliary reports (Dec 2023); NIH LiverTox monograph. Some cases required hospitalization; most resolved on withdrawal.
- **Conditional on:** Any benefit conditional on 600 mg/day, >=8 wk, diagnosed insomnia; and on accepting the liver-signal risk (avoid with hepatic disease, other hepatotoxins, alcohol).
- **Primary sources:** Cheah KL 2021, PLoS One, PMID 34559859 (DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0257843; 5 RCTs, SMD -0.59); Langade D 2019/2021, Cureus / J Ethnopharmacol S0378874120331585 (KSM-66, Ixoreal-supplied); TGA 2024/2026 safety advisory 'Withania somnifera (Ashwagandha) and very rare risk of liver injury'; NIH LiverTox, NBK548536

### Glycine — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** A genuine but fragile signal. Two small polysomnography crossover studies show faster sleep onset and better subjective quality at 3 g, but both trace to the amino-acid manufacturer and neither has been independently replicated at scale. Low-risk to try; not decision-grade.

- **Evidence base:** 2 small crossover RCTs (Inagawa 2006, Yamadera 2007) + mechanistic follow-ups — total n ≈ ~10-20 per study
- **Largest independent trial:** None truly independent identified.
- **Effect size (CI):** PSG: shortened latency to sleep onset and to slow-wave sleep; improved subjective sleep quality/efficiency and reduced daytime sleepiness. No CIs reported in the small crossover papers; direction consistent but underpowered.
- **Clears MCID?** Unclear - PSG latency shortened but small-n crossover without CI/MCID framing.
- **Dose — trials:** 3 g before bedtime · **retail (active):** 3 g (bulk glycine or sleep blends) - retail meets trial dose
- **Population studied:** Adults with mild/unsatisfactory sleep (self-reported poor sleepers), small samples. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Plausible for a healthy poor sleeper (that IS the studied group), but evidence is thin.
- **Funding split:** No formal split; the foundational trials are linked to Ajinomoto (glycine manufacturer) - an industry-concentration flag. · **Outlier leverage:** Whole case rests on 2 small same-group studies. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** none found; well tolerated.
- **Conditional on:** Signal is in mildly poor sleepers, not diagnosed insomnia.
- **Primary sources:** Yamadera W 2007, Sleep Biol Rhythms 5:126-131, DOI 10.1111/j.1479-8425.2007.00262.x (PSG crossover); Inagawa K 2006, Sleep Biol Rhythms, DOI 10.1111/j.1479-8425.2006.00193.x; Bannai M 2012, Front Neurol (mechanism)

### L-theanine — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Modest and mostly about relaxation/anxiety, not a hypnotic. Improves subjective sleep-quality subscales in stressed/anxious adults; weak-to-absent effect on objective sleep latency. Reasonable for stress-linked poor sleep, low harm; not a fix for clinical insomnia.

- **Evidence base:** ~11 sleep RCTs (13-trial systematic review 2025); ~9 stress/anxiety RCTs — total n ≈ several hundred pooled; individual trials small (n=30 typical)
- **Largest independent trial:** Hidese 2019 (n=30) is the cleanest sleep RCT; still small.
- **Effect size (CI):** PSQI global and subscales (latency, disturbance, sleep-med use) improved vs placebo in stressed adults (Hidese 2019, n=30, 200 mg x4 wk). Objective-sleep effects weak; benefit skews to acute-stress anxiety reduction.
- **Clears MCID?** Borderline - PSQI subscale shifts small; unlikely to clear a clinical insomnia MCID.
- **Dose — trials:** 200-450 mg/day · **retail (active):** 100-200 mg (Suntheanine) - broadly matches trial dose
- **Population studied:** Healthy stressed adults, anxiety, ADHD boys, schizophrenia adjunct - not primary insomnia. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Reasonable for a healthy stressed adult (studied population); not for diagnosed insomnia.
- **Funding split:** Many trials use Suntheanine (Taiyo) material; no formal funding subgroup, but industry material is common. · **Outlier leverage:** No single dominating study; effect diffuse and small.
- **Regulatory / safety:** none found; well tolerated.
- **Conditional on:** Best-supported use is stress/anxiety-associated poor sleep.
- **Primary sources:** Hidese S 2019, Nutrients, PMID 31623400 (200 mg RCT, PSQI); Rao TP / Williams JL L-theanine sleep systematic review; 2025 systematic review of L-theanine on sleep, PMID 41176609 (DOI 10.1080/1028415X.2025.2556925)

### Lavender oil - Silexan (Lavandula angustifolia) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** The best-evidenced botanical here - but it is an ANXIOLYTIC, and sleep improvement is downstream of anxiety relief, not a primary hypnotic action. Multiple RCTs (incl. vs paroxetine) show real HAMA reductions in GAD/subsyndromal anxiety; disturbed sleep improves as the anxiety lifts. Most trials are manufacturer-funded (Schwabe), which tempers confidence. Reasonable if the problem is anxiety-driven sleeplessness.

- **Evidence base:** Multiple double-blind RCTs (Kasper et al. 2010-2015) + network meta-analysis — total n ≈ 170 (restlessness/disturbed-sleep trial); 539 vs paroxetine/placebo (Kasper 2014); thousands pooled across program
- **Largest independent trial:** No large sponsor-independent Silexan trial identified.
- **Effect size (CI):** Kasper 2014 GAD: HAMA reduction superior to placebo, comparable to paroxetine (HAMA -14.1 vs paroxetine -11.3 vs placebo -9.5). Sleep sub-scores and PSQI improved secondary to anxiolysis. Effect sizes moderate for anxiety; sleep effect derivative.
- **Clears MCID?** For anxiety: YES (HAMA change clinically meaningful). For sleep as a primary endpoint: not established independent of anxiety.
- **Dose — trials:** 80 mg/day Silexan (single standardized capsule) · **retail (active):** 80 mg Silexan (CalmAid/Lasea) - retail matches trial dose exactly
- **Population studied:** GAD, subsyndromal/subthreshold anxiety, 'restlessness and disturbed sleep' out-patients (n=170) - anxious populations, not primary insomnia. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Limited transfer to a healthy, non-anxious poor sleeper.
- **Funding split:** No independent-only pooled estimate; the Silexan program is overwhelmingly Dr. Willmar Schwabe-funded - the main caveat. · **Outlier leverage:** Consistent across several trials, not one-study-driven, but all from one sponsor. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** Generally well tolerated (eructation/lavender belching main AE). No liver signal. Not FDA-approved as a drug; sold as a supplement in the US.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **does NOT survive** — score cut (revised 2.5; decisive check: Check 1 (funding-source subgroup) compounded by Check 5 (population transfer)). I tried to break the 3 and it mostly held as an ANXIOLYTIC but not in the Sleep category it is graded under. The refutation that lands: the entire Silexan program is Dr. Willmar Schwabe-funded, so an independent-only pooled estimate cannot even be produced (worse than the collagen trap, where an independent subgroup at least existed to fail) — by rubric, positive results 'concentrated in industry-funded studies' is score-2 language. Two checks did NOT break it: retail dose equals the trial dose exactly (80 mg CalmAid/Lasea = 80 mg tested arm, so retail<trial is false), and the effect is replicated across multiple trials rather than hinging on one outlier, and it is an EU-licensed herbal medicine with no liver or regulatory advisory. What pulls it below 3 for SLEEP specifically: the hypnotic benefit is derivative of anxiolysis (never demonstrated as a primary sleep endpoint independent of anxiety, PSQI never disaggregated) and the studied population is anxious patients, not healthy non-anxious poor sleepers, so transfer is limited; the paroxetine comparator also failed significance, tempering confidence in the trial platform. Net: genuinely the best-evidenced botanical in this set, but 2.5 for sleep given the single-sponsor monoculture and the anxiety-contingent, non-transferable sleep effect.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** If the driver is an anxiety disorder, first-line care is CBT/SSRIs, not an OTC oil.
- **Conditional on:** Benefit conditional on anxiety-related sleep disturbance; not demonstrated for non-anxious primary insomnia.
- **Primary sources:** Kasper S 2014, Int J Neuropsychopharmacol, DOI 10.1017/S1461145714000017 (vs paroxetine & placebo); Kasper S 2015, Eur Neuropsychopharmacol S0924977X15002424 (restlessness/disturbed sleep, n=170); Yap WS 2019, Sci Rep, DOI 10.1038/s41598-019-54529-9 (network meta-analysis)

### Magnesium glycinate (bisglycinate) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Weak, condition-dependent. A reasonable low-risk trial IF dietary magnesium is low; do not expect a hypnotic effect in a replete adult. Effect sizes are small and the older-adult meta-analysis is explicitly rated substandard by its own authors.

- **Evidence base:** ~3 RCTs in the elderly meta (Mah 2021); one 2024 bisglycinate RCT — total n ≈ 151 (Mah 2021 meta); 155 (Rawji 2024)
- **Largest independent trial:** Rawji 2024 (n=155, healthy poor sleepers) - small ISI benefit, larger in low-intake subgroup.
- **Effect size (CI):** Pooled SOL -17.36 min in older adults (Mah 2021, 3 RCTs n=151, but low quality). Bisglycinate 2024: ISI reduction vs placebo at wk 4, Cohen's d ~0.2 (small).
- **Clears MCID?** Borderline/NO - the elderly SOL figure would clear MCID but rests on low-quality trials; the healthy-adult d=0.2 does not.
- **Dose — trials:** 500 mg elemental Mg/day (Abbasi 2012, as oxide); ~200 mg elemental as bisglycinate (Rawji 2024) · **retail (active):** ~90-100 mg elemental Mg (a '500 mg magnesium glycinate' capsule is mostly glycine by mass) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Older adults with insomnia (Abbasi 2012); healthy adults reporting poor sleep (Rawji 2024). Benefit skews to low-baseline-intake subgroups. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Poor transfer to a magnesium-replete healthy adult; closer to correcting a shortfall than a sleep drug.
- **Funding split:** not reported · **Outlier leverage:** Elderly pooled effect leans heavily on Abbasi 2012, a single small trial.
- **Regulatory / safety:** none found; high doses cause osmotic diarrhea.
- **Conditional on:** Meaningful benefit conditional on low dietary magnesium / relative deficiency (subgroup effect in Rawji 2024).
- **Primary sources:** Abbasi B 2012, J Res Med Sci, PMID 23264875 (elderly primary insomnia RCT); Mah J & Pitre T 2021, BMC Complement Med Ther, DOI 10.1186/s12906-021-03297-z (authors call evidence 'substandard'); Rawji A 2024/2025, Nat Sci Sleep, DOI 10.2147/NSS.S524348 (bisglycinate RCT n=155)

### Montmorency tart cherry (Prunus cerasus) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Thin and small-study-driven. A few small trials show more sleep time/efficiency and raised urinary melatonin, but samples are tiny (n=8-20), some industry-linked, and a 2024 RCT in overweight/obese adults was null. High-anthocyanin juice concentrate was tested; retail gummies/capsules are nowhere near that dose.

- **Evidence base:** 3-4 small RCTs + 1 null RCT; 2025 systematic review — total n ≈ ~15-20 per positive trial
- **Largest independent trial:** 2024 MDPI Nutrients RCT (tart cherry powder, overweight/obese) - NO improvement in sleep or inflammation.
- **Effect size (CI):** Howatson 2012: +total sleep time and +sleep efficiency, raised urinary 6-sulfatoxymelatonin (n=20 healthy crossover). Losso 2018: +84 min sleep time on PSG but n=8 pilot - hypothesis-generating only. Pigeon 2010: modest ISI improvement.
- **Clears MCID?** Possibly in the tiny insomnia pilots, but n=8-15 with wide uncertainty - not decision-grade.
- **Dose — trials:** 2 x 30 mL Montmorency concentrate/day (~ equivalent of 90-100 cherries), or 240 mL juice x2 · **retail (active):** Gummies/capsules and single small juice servings delivering a fraction of the trial anthocyanin/phytomelatonin load · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults (Howatson n=20), older adults with insomnia (Losso n=8; Pigeon n=15). Null in overweight/obese (2024). · **transfers to healthy adult?** Small positive signal in healthy adults exists (Howatson) but underpowered.
- **Funding split:** No formal split; Cherry Marketing Institute / grower funding present in several studies. · **Outlier leverage:** Losso n=8 +84 min is a classic single-tiny-study outlier that dominates the popular claim. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2.5; decisive check: Check 4 - trial dose vs retail dose (the 2024 null RCT used 500 mg capsules; positive trials used 60 mL concentrate / 480 mL juice per day, so the null confirms underdosing rather than refuting efficacy)). I tried to break 2.5 in both directions and it holds. Downward-to-2 fails cleanly: the level-2 signature is "mechanism outruns outcome," but Howatson (n=20, healthy) showed elevated urinary 6-sulfatoxymelatonin AND improved total sleep time/efficiency in the same trial, so mechanism and outcome move together, not apart. Upward-to-3 also fails: the positive base is genuinely tiny (n=8-20), industry (Cherry Marketing Institute/grower) money is present, there is no pooled meta-analysis, and the popular +84 min claim rests on the Losso n=8 outlier. The most decisive finding is the dose gap - the 2024 Tucker RCT that everyone cites as null used only 500 mg capsules (a retail-equivalent dose) and the authors themselves called for higher doses, so it confirms retail<trial rather than refuting the compound; direction is favorable (more=better), not adverse, and no regulator advisory exists. Net: a real but thin, small-study, dose-sensitive signal sitting exactly on the 2/3 boundary - 2.5 is the honest score.
- **Conditional on:** Any grade above 2 conditional on using high-dose concentrate, not pills/gummies.
- **Primary sources:** Howatson G 2012, Eur J Nutr, DOI 10.1007/s00394-011-0263-7; Losso JN 2018, Am J Ther, PMID 28901974 (n=8 PSG pilot); Pigeon WR 2010, J Med Food, PMID 20438325; 2024 Nutrients tart cherry powder RCT, MDPI 2072-6643/16/23/4125 (null)

### 5-HTP (5-hydroxytryptophan) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Thin. No adequately powered stand-alone insomnia RCT; recent evidence is a modest older-adult trial helping poor sleepers on some PSQI components, plus combination products that confound the ingredient. Serotonin-load and historical Peak-X contamination concerns argue for caution. Not decision-grade for sleep.

- **Evidence base:** 1-2 stand-alone RCTs + combination trials — total n ≈ ~100 (Singapore older-adult RCT)
- **Largest independent trial:** Singapore older-adult 5-HTP RCT (Clinical Nutrition 2024, PMID 38309227).
- **Effect size (CI):** Singapore older-adult RCT: favorable change in some PSQI components and reduced sleep latency at 4-8 wk, more evident in poor sleepers; raised serum serotonin. Modest, subjective, not PSG. No pooled CI for stand-alone insomnia.
- **Clears MCID?** Unclear - subjective subscale changes; no PSG MCID demonstrated.
- **Dose — trials:** 100-300 mg/day (older-adult trial ~100 mg) · **retail (active):** 50-100 mg - often at/below the lower trial dose · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Older adults (Singapore RCT), often poor sleepers; some combination-product trials (GABA+5-HTP). · **transfers to healthy adult?** Weak; not shown in healthy younger adults as monotherapy.
- **Funding split:** not reported · **Outlier leverage:** Sparse evidence base; the older-adult RCT carries most weight.
- **Regulatory / safety:** Historical: 5-HTP 'Peak-X' contaminant associated with EMS-like cases (1990s); theoretical serotonin-syndrome risk with serotonergic drugs. No current ban.
- **Conditional on:** Signal conditional on being a poor sleeper / older adult.
- **Primary sources:** Sutanto CN 2024, Clin Nutr, PMID 38309227 (5-HTP older adults, sleep & gut microbiota); Byun JI 2018, J Clin Neurol (GABA/5-HTP combination - confounded); NIH ODS / LiverTox 5-HTP entries; Peak-X/EMS historical literature

### Apigenin / Chamomile (Matricaria chamomilla) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for insomnia. The best-designed primary-insomnia RCT (Zick 2011) was flatly NULL on every sleep-diary measure. Positive results are in special populations (elderly nursing-home, postpartum) and heterogeneous low-quality meta-analyses. Isolated apigenin has essentially no direct human sleep outcome data - it is a mechanism (GABA-A ligand), not a proven product.

- **Evidence base:** ~10 clinical trials in 2024 meta (n=772); key insomnia pilot Zick 2011 — total n ≈ 34 (Zick 2011); 772 pooled (2024 meta)
- **Largest independent trial:** Zick SM 2011 (rigorous, placebo-controlled, primary insomnia) - NULL.
- **Effect size (CI):** Zick 2011 (270 mg chamomile BID x28 d, n=34): NO significant difference vs placebo in total sleep time, sleep efficiency, latency, WASO, or quality. 2024 meta: significant PSQI reduction but across heterogeneous populations and low study quality.
- **Clears MCID?** NO in primary insomnia (null); questionable in the positive elderly subgroups.
- **Dose — trials:** 270-540 mg standardized extract/day, or ~400-1600 mg chamomile; apigenin content low and variable · **retail (active):** 'Apigenin 50 mg' pills or chamomile tea (~1-2 mg apigenin/cup) - far below any pharmacologic exposure
- **Population studied:** DSM-IV primary insomnia (Zick, null); elderly nursing-home residents and postpartum women (positive). · **transfers to healthy adult?** No transfer to a healthy adult with insomnia (best insomnia RCT null).
- **Funding split:** Not formally split; the positive meta is dominated by low-quality, heterogeneous trials. · **Outlier leverage:** PSQI meta-effect leans on a few positive special-population studies (nursing home, postpartum). ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** none found; allergy risk in Asteraceae-sensitive individuals.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: Effect size vs MCID (check 3), reinforced by population transfer (check 5)). I tried to break the score of 2 in both directions and it held. Refuting upward to 3: the two significant meta-analyses (Kazemi 2024 PSQI WMD -1.88 [-3.46, -0.31], I2 88.4%, n=5; Hieu 2019 SMD -0.73 [-1.23, -0.23]) look like a real signal, but the Kazemi pooled effect sits below the ~2.5-3 point PSQI MCID with a trivial upper CI bound, rests on only 5 severely heterogeneous studies, and is driven by special populations (postpartum, elderly nursing-home). Meanwhile the only rigorous DSM-IV primary insomnia RCT (Zick 2011, n=34) was independently confirmed NULL on every sleep-diary measure, so the effect does not transfer to a healthy adult with insomnia. Refuting downward to 1.5: isolated apigenin has essentially no direct human sleep-outcome data (pure GABA-A mechanism), but chamomile extract has ~10 trials, so it clears the pure-marketing floor. retail_below_trial is false because chamomile capsules can reach the 270-540 mg trial dose and isolated apigenin has no trial dose to be below; is_compromised is true because the positive evidence is concentrated in low-quality, heterogeneous, special-population studies.
- **Conditional on:** Any signal conditional on elderly/postpartum populations, not general adult insomnia.
- **Primary sources:** Zick SM 2011, BMC Complement Altern Med, PMID 21939549 (DOI 10.1186/1472-6882-11-78; NULL primary-insomnia RCT); Abdullahzadeh M 2017 (elderly nursing-home, positive); 2024 chamomile-on-sleep meta-analysis, PMID 39106912

### GABA (gamma-aminobutyric acid, oral) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. Oral GABA poorly crosses the blood-brain barrier; the small positive sleep RCTs are largely industry-funded (PharmaGABA/Pharma Foods), soft-endpoint, and any effect likely runs through peripheral gut-vagal signaling, not central inhibition. Mechanism outruns outcome.

- **Evidence base:** A handful of small RCTs — total n ≈ typically 20-40 per trial
- **Largest independent trial:** No convincing independent PSG trial; no direct evidence of human BBB crossing.
- **Effect size (CI):** Modest reductions in sleep latency and small non-REM/EEG changes in small trials (100-300 mg); one Western RCT reported sleep-efficiency and HRV improvement. No robust PSG replication; CIs not meaningful given size.
- **Clears MCID?** NO / unclear - endpoints soft and small.
- **Dose — trials:** 100-300 mg (PharmaGABA) · **retail (active):** 100-750 mg - retail meets or exceeds trial dose (dose is not the limiter; the BBB is)
- **Population studied:** Small Japanese RCTs; sedentary overweight women (one Western RCT); healthy adults. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Weak; a central effect is biochemically implausible at oral doses.
- **Funding split:** No formal split, but the supportive trials cluster around the PharmaGABA manufacturer (Pharma Foods). · **Outlier leverage:** Small, sponsor-linked studies carry the claim. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Primary sources:** Yamatsu A 2016, J Nutr Sci Vitaminol (PharmaGABA sleep latency); Byun JI 2018, J Clin Neurol (GABA/5-HTP combination - confounded by co-ingredient); Boonstra E 2015, Front Psychol (review: oral GABA BBB uncertainty)

### Kava (Piper methysticum) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Recommend AGAINST for sleep. Efficacy evidence is for ANXIETY (Cochrane-level, kavain), not sleep per se, and it is overshadowed by a serious hepatotoxicity signal: FDA 2002 consumer advisory, temporary bans in Germany/UK/Switzerland, >100 suspected severe liver-injury cases including transplants and deaths. The risk-benefit for an OTC sleep aid is unacceptable.

- **Evidence base:** Cochrane review for anxiety (Pittler & Ernst): ~12 RCTs; sleep-specific RCTs sparse — total n ≈ ~700 across anxiety RCTs
- **Largest independent trial:** Kava-anxiety Cochrane pooled analysis (Pittler & Ernst 2003).
- **Effect size (CI):** Anxiety: significant HAMA reduction vs placebo (Cochrane weighted mean difference favoring kava). Sleep-specific outcome data are thin and secondary.
- **Clears MCID?** For anxiety possibly; for sleep as a primary endpoint - not established.
- **Dose — trials:** Standardized to ~70-210 mg kavalactones/day in anxiety trials · **retail (active):** Variable kavalactone content; often poorly standardized
- **Population studied:** Generalized/situational anxiety adults (efficacy); sleep is not the primary studied endpoint. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Not a demonstrated hypnotic in healthy adults.
- **Funding split:** not reported · **Outlier leverage:** not applicable - the decisive issue is safety, not efficacy leverage.
- **Regulatory / safety:** FDA 2002 consumer advisory (severe liver injury); FDA 2023 memorandum concluding recreational/relaxation use 'not safe for human consumption'; historical bans/restrictions in Germany, UK, Switzerland, Australia; >100 suspected hepatotoxicity cases with transplants and deaths; NIH LiverTox monograph.
- **Primary sources:** Pittler MH & Ernst E 2003, Cochrane Database Syst Rev (kava for anxiety); FDA 2002 Consumer Advisory on kava-containing supplements; FDA 2023 scientific memorandum (fda.gov/media/169556); NIH LiverTox Kava, NBK548637

### Magnesium L-threonate (Magtein) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not pay the premium. Every supportive sleep/cognition trial is tied to the ingredient owner (Magtein/Threotech/Neurocentria) and relies on subjective or wearable endpoints. No independent replication. The 'brain-bioavailable magnesium' story is marketing ahead of independent outcome data.

- **Evidence base:** 2 small RCTs, both industry-linked — total n ≈ ~100 (Lopresti Magtein 2026) + ~80 (2024 MgT sleep RCT)
- **Largest independent trial:** None identified - this is the core problem.
- **Effect size (CI):** Subjective sleep and daytime-functioning improvements; wearable (Oura) deltas. No polysomnography. Effect sizes small and endpoint-soft; headline claims (e.g. '7.5-year brain-age reduction') are cognitive, not sleep, and industry-reported.
- **Clears MCID?** NA - no validated objective sleep MCID reported; subjective-only.
- **Dose — trials:** 2 g magnesium L-threonate/day (~144 mg elemental Mg) · **retail (active):** ~2 g L-threonate (~144 mg elemental) - retail matches trial dose
- **Population studied:** Adults 18-45 with self-reported dissatisfied sleep (Lopresti 2024/2026); prior work in older adults. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Unestablished; no independent evidence it beats cheap magnesium glycinate.
- **Funding split:** No independent-vs-industry split possible because there is essentially no independent trial. Magtein supplied by Threotech LLC; program historically Neurocentria-associated. · **Outlier leverage:** Entire evidence base is a handful of sponsor-connected trials - the grade is a funding artifact. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Primary sources:** Lopresti AL & Smith SJ 2026, Front Nutr, PMID 41601871 (DOI 10.3389/fnut.2025.1729164; Magtein supplied by Threotech); Hausenblas HA / Lopresti 2024, J Affect Disord Rep, ScienceDirect S2590142724000193 (MgT self-reported sleep RCT)

### Passionflower (Passiflora incarnata) - added, in-category — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Thin. One small positive tea RCT and a few low-quality trials; frequently bundled with valerian, confounding attribution. Not decision-grade as a stand-alone sleep aid.

- **Evidence base:** 1-2 small RCTs + combination products — total n ≈ ~40
- **Largest independent trial:** Ngan & Conduit 2011, Phytother Res.
- **Effect size (CI):** Ngan 2011: small but significant improvement in subjective sleep-quality diary rating vs placebo tea; no objective PSG effect. Underpowered.
- **Clears MCID?** NO - subjective-only, small.
- **Dose — trials:** 2 g passionflower as tea · **retail (active):** Capsule extracts of variable strength; often in multi-herb blends
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults with mild sleep complaints (Ngan & Conduit 2011, n=41 tea crossover). · **transfers to healthy adult?** Studied in healthy mild poor sleepers; signal weak.
- **Funding split:** not reported · **Outlier leverage:** Single small study drives the claim.
- **Regulatory / safety:** none found; generally well tolerated.
- **Primary sources:** Ngan A & Conduit R 2011, Phytother Res, PMID 21294203 (DOI 10.1002/ptr.3400)

### Valerian (Valeriana officinalis) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for efficacy. The 2024 umbrella review found NO evidence of efficacy for insomnia. Older positive meta-analyses rest on a subjective dichotomous 'improved sleep?' outcome, heterogeneous and poorly standardized extracts, and industry involvement. Good safety is not the same as working.

- **Evidence base:** Bent 2006 meta: 16 RCTs; 2020 review: 60 studies; 2024 umbrella review of prior meta-analyses — total n ≈ ~1000+ (Bent 2006); ~6894 across 2020 review
- **Largest independent trial:** Independent objective-endpoint trials (Diaper & Hindmarch) are null on PSG.
- **Effect size (CI):** Bent 2006: RR 1.8 (95% CI 1.2-2.9) for dichotomous 'improved sleep' - an outcome prone to response bias; objective PSG endpoints largely null. 2024 umbrella review: no consistent efficacy.
- **Clears MCID?** NO - objective sleep parameters show no reliable clinically meaningful change.
- **Dose — trials:** 300-900 mg extract at night · **retail (active):** 300-600 mg extract (variable valerenic-acid content; poor batch standardization)
- **Population studied:** Mixed insomnia/poor-sleep adults; highly heterogeneous extracts and doses. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No reliable effect to transfer.
- **Funding split:** Not formally split; heterogeneity, extract-quality variance, and industry trials drive the unstable positive older results. · **Outlier leverage:** Positive pooled signals are sensitive to a few subjective-outcome trials; drop them and it collapses (as the 2024 umbrella review effectively shows). ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** Generally good safety (rare hepatotoxicity in multi-herb products); no efficacy-based advisory.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: Check 3 (effect size vs MCID / subjective-vs-objective split): objective polysomnographic endpoints are null, so the only positive signal lives in a subjective dichotomous outcome prone to response bias.). I tried to raise the score and could not. The lone positive pooled result (Bent 2006, RR 1.8, CI 1.2-2.9) rests entirely on a subjective "improved sleep?" outcome, and Bent's own funnel plot flagged publication bias (Kendall's tau P=.03) plus "significant methodologic problems" across studies — so the signal is both bias-laden and confined to soft endpoints. The 2024 Valente umbrella review (PMID 38359657) independently confirms "no evidence of efficacy for insomnia," with subjective benefit not reproduced on objective/quantitative measures, echoing the null PSG findings. I also could not rescue it via the dose check: retail 300-600 mg overlaps trial doses of 300-900 mg (retail_below_trial = false), so the failure is genuine lack of effect at adequate dose, not underdosing. No transferable population benefit exists (mixed insomnia adults, heterogeneous poorly standardized extracts), and safety is good but EMA lists it only as a traditional-use product, which by definition requires no efficacy proof. Score 2 holds — positive results concentrated in low-quality/biased subjective trials, mechanism outrunning outcome; not a 1 only because a substantial RCT base with a consistent subjective signal exists.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** CBT-I is the evidence-based route for chronic insomnia.
- **Primary sources:** Bent S 2006, Am J Med, PMID 17145239 (meta-analysis, dichotomous outcome); Baglioni C et al. umbrella review 2024, Eur Neuropsychopharmacol, DOI 10.1016/j.euroneuro.2024.01.008 ('no evidence of efficacy'); Taibi DM 2007, Sleep Med Rev (critical review)


---

## Immunity

### Probiotics for URTI prevention — **3/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Conditional and modest. Cochrane finds probiotics reduce the number of people getting at least one URTI and cut episode rates, but certainty is low-to-moderate, effects are strain-specific, heterogeneity is high, and many trials are industry-funded. A reasonable but not compelling buy, and only specific strains.

- **Evidence base:** ~23-24 RCTs (Cochrane 2022) — total n ≈ >6,000; incidence analysis 2,032
- **Largest independent trial:** Mixed; several independent daycare/school trials contribute alongside sponsor trials
- **Effect size (CI):** At least one URTI episode: RR ~0.76 (0.65-0.89). Episode rate ratio 0.79 (0.71-0.89), p<0.001. Also reduced antibiotic use and school absence. GRADE low-to-moderate.
- **Clears MCID?** Borderline/modest — meaningful at population level (fewer episodes, less antibiotic use) but individual effect is small
- **Dose — trials:** Strain-specific, typically 1e9-1e10 CFU/day (e.g., L. rhamnosus GG, L. casei Shirota/DN-114001, B. animalis) · **retail (active):** CFU and strain vary enormously; many products do not use the studied strains or guarantee viable CFU to end of shelf life
- **Population studied:** General adults and children (community, daycare, some athletes) · **transfers to healthy adult?** Reasonably — trials include healthy community populations
- **Funding split:** Many trials manufacturer-funded (Yakult, Danone, Chr. Hansen); pooled effect persists but certainty is downgraded partly for this · **Outlier leverage:** High heterogeneity; effect not reliant on a single study but strain mix matters
- **Regulatory / safety:** No general advisory; caution/sepsis risk in immunocompromised and critically ill — not for that population
- **Conditional on:** Use a specifically studied strain at studied CFU; benefit is strain-dependent, not a class effect
- **Product:** Culturelle Culturelle Daily Probiotic (Digestive Daily) — 10 billion CFU L. rhamnosus GG per capsule active/serving; none (guarantees LGG CFU through end of shelf life). Uses the specific studied strain (L. rhamnosus GG) at 10 billion CFU with a through-expiry CFU guarantee - preferred over generic multi-strain blends that don't name or guarantee the trial strain. For the B. animalis or L. casei Shirota arms of the URTI literature you'd instead choose Yakult (Shirota) or a Bifidobacterium animalis product. Search URL, not a fixed ASIN.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Culturelle+Daily+Probiotic+Lactobacillus+rhamnosus+GG
- **Primary sources:** Zhao Y et al. Cochrane Database Syst Rev 2022;CD006895.pub4, PMID 36001877

### Zinc (acute cold lozenges vs prophylaxis) — **3/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Conditional buy for TREATMENT only, and only a correctly formulated lozenge. High-dose ionic zinc acetate/gluconate lozenges started within 24h can meaningfully shorten a cold. Most retail products fail on formulation, not the headline mg. Prophylactic daily zinc does little or nothing for colds.

- **Evidence base:** 7 RCTs (Hemilä 2017 lozenge meta); 2024 Cochrane broader — total n ≈ 575 (Hemilä 2017 lozenges)
- **Largest independent trial:** Hemilä 2017 pooled (independent reanalysis of Prasad, Petrus, Mossad et al.)
- **Effect size (CI):** Cold duration -33% (95% CI 21-45%); acetate -40%, gluconate -28% (difference NS). No added benefit above ~100 mg/day. 2024 Cochrane (Nault) rated certainty low and was more equivocal; Hemilä published a formal rebuttal.
- **Clears MCID?** Yes if formulation is right — roughly 2 fewer days of a 7-day cold clears the threshold
- **Dose — trials:** 80-207 mg/day ELEMENTAL zinc as acetate/gluconate lozenges, dissolved in mouth every ~2h, no chelating fillers (no citric/tartaric acid, mannitol/sorbitol) · **retail (active):** Lozenges commonly 5-23 mg elemental per lozenge; most consumers take too few, or products bind zinc to citrate/aspartate or contain flavor acids that neutralize ionic zinc — delivering far less bioavailable ionic Zn2+ than trials · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Adults with naturally acquired common colds, treatment started within 24-48h · **transfers to healthy adult?** Transfers to a healthy adult with an acute cold; does NOT support daily prophylactic capsules
- **Funding split:** Hemilä's base is largely independent; 2024 Cochrane independent · **Outlier leverage:** Effect consistent across 7 trials, not outlier-dependent; the acetate/gluconate split is the main heterogeneity source
- **Regulatory / safety:** FDA 2009 warning against INTRANASAL zinc gluconate (Zicam) — permanent anosmia. Applies to nasal gels/sprays, not oral lozenges.
- **Conditional on:** Ionic zinc acetate/gluconate lozenge, ~75-100 mg/day elemental, started <24h, no citrate/mannitol/sorbitol chelators, dosed through the day
- **Product:** Life Extension Life Extension Zinc Lozenges (zinc acetate) — 18.75 mg ELEMENTAL zinc as zinc acetate per lozenge active/serving; none — ⚠ per-serving dose BELOW trial dose. One lozenge (18.75 mg elemental) is far below the 80-207 mg/day acute protocol - dose_adequate=false; you must take ~4-5+ lozenges spread every ~2 h. This is one of the few zinc ACETATE lozenges (Hemila's criterion) rather than gluconate/citrate. CAUTION: verify the current label has no citric/tartaric acid and no mannitol/sorbitol, which chelate/neutralize ionic Zn2+ and negate the effect - formulations change, so read the 'other ingredients' before buying. Search URL, not a fixed ASIN.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Life+Extension+Zinc+Acetate+Lozenges
- **Primary sources:** Hemilä H. JRSM Open 2017;8(5), PMID 28515951; Hemilä H et al. Open Forum Infect Dis 2017 IPD, PMID 28480298; Nault D et al. Cochrane 2024;CD014914, PMID 38719213

### Andrographis paniculata — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Efficacy is the strongest of the herbals here — meta-analysis shows genuine improvement in cough and sore throat versus placebo — but a serious, active regulatory safety signal caps this. Not a recommended buy: the anaphylaxis risk outweighs a symptom-relief benefit you can get more safely.

- **Evidence base:** ~33 RCTs (Hu 2017 meta), quality mixed; many Chinese-language, some industry (KalmCold/Kan Jang) — total n ≈ >7,000 (Hu 2017)
- **Largest independent trial:** Hu 2017 pooled base; Coon & Ernst 2004 earlier systematic review
- **Effect size (CI):** Hu 2017: improved cough and sore throat and shortened symptom course vs placebo/usual care; effect real but heterogeneity high and several trials low quality / combination products confound the single-agent claim
- **Clears MCID?** Plausibly for symptom relief, but confounded by combination formulas and trial quality
- **Dose — trials:** ~60-200 mg/day andrographolides (standardized extracts SHA-10/KalmCold) · **retail (active):** Extracts standardized to andrographolides, typically in the trial range
- **Population studied:** Adults and children with acute respiratory tract infections; many trials use fixed combos (Kan Jang = andrographis + eleuthero) · **transfers to healthy adult?** Applies to acutely ill patients; not a prophylactic for the healthy
- **Funding split:** Positive trials include manufacturer products (Swedish Herbal Institute Kan Jang, KalmCold); independent low-quality trials also positive but heterogeneous · **Outlier leverage:** Heterogeneity and combination-product confounding rather than a single outlier
- **Regulatory / safety:** STRONG: Australian TGA — 287 anaphylaxis reports to 31 Dec 2025 including a 2024 fatality; mandatory allergy warning since 2019; TGA has proposed REMOVING andrographis from permitted ingredients. This is the deciding factor.
- **Conditional on:** Symptomatic relief of an active ARTI, not prevention
- **Primary sources:** Hu XY et al. PLoS One 2017;12:e0181780, PMID 28783758 (+2018 correction); Coon JT, Ernst E. Planta Med 2004;70:293, PMID 15095142; TGA safety advisory, Andrographis and anaphylaxis, updated 2024-2025

### Beta-glucans (yeast 1,3/1,6, Wellmune) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy on current evidence. Trials show modest reductions in cold/flu symptom-days, but the positive literature is overwhelmingly funded by the ingredient manufacturer, with no adequately powered independent RCT. Mechanism (innate immune priming) outruns the outcome data.

- **Evidence base:** ~6-8 small/medium RCTs — total n ≈ Largest ~370 (marathoners); others 50-250
- **Largest independent trial:** No adequately powered independent trial; Talbott/Fuller/McFarlin studies are manufacturer-linked
- **Effect size (CI):** Reductions in URTI symptom-days and symptom severity (e.g., fewer symptomatic days post-marathon; fewer symptom days in older adults). Effect sizes modest with wide CIs; endpoints often self-reported symptom diaries.
- **Clears MCID?** Uncertain — symptom-day reductions are modest and rest on soft, self-reported endpoints
- **Dose — trials:** 250 mg/day Wellmune (yeast beta-1,3/1,6-glucan) · **retail (active):** 250-500 mg/day — matches or exceeds trial dose
- **Population studied:** Marathon runners, stressed adults, older adults (50-70), some general adults · **transfers to healthy adult?** Marginal; strongest signals in physically stressed or older cohorts
- **Funding split:** Effect concentrated in Kerry/Biothera (Wellmune manufacturer)-funded trials; independent replication absent · **Outlier leverage:** Small trials, sponsor-linked; no single outlier but the whole base is sponsor-driven ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** No safety advisory; generally well tolerated
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2.5; decisive check: Funding-source subgroup / independent replication (Check 1): the one academic-led trial with a physician-confirmed endpoint (Fuller 2017, older adults) was null (OR 0.55, p=0.149), while the significant pooled signal rests on smaller, mostly manufacturer-linked, self-reported-diary trials with high heterogeneity.). I tried to break the score by finding an adequately powered independent win and a below-trial retail dose; neither held up. The Zhong 2021 meta-analysis (13 RCTs) does show a significant pooled effect (incidence OR 0.345; episode/duration SMD approximately -0.31), but with high heterogeneity, entirely soft self-reported endpoints, and no funding subgroup, and the most rigorous academic-led trial (Fuller 2017, confirmed URTI) was null on its primary outcome. The claim's flags are correct: results concentrated in Wellmune-manufacturer-linked trials (compromised = true), and retail dose 250-500 mg meets or exceeds the 250 mg trial dose (retail_below_trial = false). The only honest upward pressure is that a replicated pooled signal plus adequate dosing and clean safety is slightly more than pure marketing, nudging it to 2.5; it cannot reach 3 because the independent-leaning replication failed and the endpoints are diary-based.
- **Primary sources:** Fuller R et al. Nutrition 2017 (older adults, yeast beta-glucan); Talbott SM, Talbott JA. J Sports Sci Med 2009 (marathoners); McFarlin BK et al. 2013

### Bovine colostrum — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Do not buy at retail doses. Meta-analyses in exercising adults show reduced URTI incidence, but trials are small and imprecise, the population is athletes under training load, and — critically — retail servings are a fraction of the 20-60 g/day used in trials.

- **Evidence base:** 5 RCTs (2016 meta) to 7 RCTs (2022 meta) — total n ≈ 152 (2016 meta); 445 (2022 meta)
- **Largest independent trial:** Jones AW et al. meta 2016; no single large definitive trial exists
- **Effect size (CI):** 2016 meta (Jones): ~44% fewer URTI symptom-days, ~38% fewer episodes. 2022 meta: RR 0.64 for URTI, duration-dependent. Individual estimates imprecise; authors call for an adequately powered trial.
- **Clears MCID?** Uncertain — nominal reductions are meaningful for athletes but precision is low
- **Dose — trials:** 20-60 g/day bovine colostrum (commonly 20-25 g/day) · **retail (active):** Typically 2-10 g/day capsules or scoops — well below trial doses · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Endurance/exercise-trained adults under training load (not sedentary healthy adults) · **transfers to healthy adult?** Unclear — benefit shown under exercise-induced immune stress, not in sedentary adults
- **Funding split:** Some dairy/supplement-industry involvement; base too small to split cleanly · **Outlier leverage:** Small trials; pooled effect vulnerable to any single study given low n
- **Regulatory / safety:** None specific; caution with dairy allergy
- **Conditional on:** Athletes/heavy training load, AND a trial-level dose (20+ g/day) rarely used by consumers
- **Primary sources:** Jones AW et al. Br J Nutr 2016;116:1226, PMID 27462401; Systematic review/meta-regression, J Funct Foods 2022 (ScienceDirect S1756464622003863)

### N-acetylcysteine (NAC) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy as a general immune supplement. Its real, drug-grade evidence is as a mucolytic that reduces COPD exacerbations and as the paracetamol-overdose antidote. The single positive flu-symptom trial is old, in elderly/comorbid patients, and unreplicated; it did not prevent infection, only symptomatic expression.

- **Evidence base:** 1 relevant flu-symptom RCT; separate solid COPD RCT base — total n ≈ 262 (De Flora 1997)
- **Largest independent trial:** De Flora S et al. 1997, n=262 (multicenter Italian, independent)
- **Effect size (CI):** De Flora 1997: seroconversion to A/H1N1 unchanged (NAC did not prevent infection), but only 25% of infected NAC subjects became symptomatic vs 79% on placebo; fewer/less severe influenza-like episodes. Single-trial, not replicated for this endpoint.
- **Clears MCID?** Plausibly in the studied elderly/comorbid group; unproven and untransferred to healthy adults
- **Dose — trials:** 600 mg twice daily (1200 mg/day) for 6 months · **retail (active):** 600-1200 mg/day capsules — matches trial
- **Population studied:** Elderly (78% >=65y), 62% with chronic disease (De Flora 1997) — NOT healthy adults · **transfers to healthy adult?** Not demonstrated — the trial population was old and comorbid
- **Funding split:** Not a funding artifact; the issue is single-trial, narrow population · **Outlier leverage:** The immunity claim rests on one trial
- **Regulatory / safety:** FDA (2022) holds NAC is EXCLUDED from the dietary-supplement definition (approved as a drug first); currently sold only under FDA enforcement discretion. Regulatory status, not efficacy, is the flag.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** NAC's robust evidence is as a DRUG: mucolytic reducing COPD exacerbations (BRONCUS, PANTHEON/HIACE) and the standard antidote for acetaminophen overdose — not a general immune booster
- **Conditional on:** Elderly or chronically ill population; general antioxidant/mucolytic use, not cold prophylaxis in the healthy
- **Primary sources:** De Flora S et al. Eur Respir J 1997;10:1535, PMID 9230243; FDA Final Guidance on NAC enforcement discretion, Aug 2022

### Vitamin C (regular prophylaxis and cold treatment) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy to avoid catching colds — regular supplementation does not lower incidence in the general population. Daily supplementation shaves a modest, sub-clinical fraction off cold duration; taking it only after symptoms start does nothing. Genuine benefit exists ONLY for people under extreme acute physical stress.

- **Evidence base:** ~29 incidence + 31 duration comparisons (Cochrane) — total n ≈ >11,000 (incidence); 9,745 cold episodes (duration); 598 in stress subgroup
- **Largest independent trial:** Hemilä Cochrane pooled base (independent); no single sponsor drives it
- **Effect size (CI):** Incidence general pop: RR ~0.97, no effect. Duration: -8% adults (95% CI 3-13%), -14% children (5-22%). Therapeutic dosing at onset: no consistent benefit. Extreme-stress subgroup incidence: RR 0.48 (0.35-0.64).
- **Clears MCID?** No — 8% of a ~7-day cold is roughly half a day, below any meaningful threshold for a healthy adult
- **Dose — trials:** >=200 mg/day regular supplementation (many trials 1 g/day) · **retail (active):** 500-1000 mg/day ascorbic acid — meets or exceeds trial dose
- **Population studied:** General population (incidence/duration) and a distinct subgroup under extreme physical stress (marathon runners, skiers, soldiers) · **transfers to healthy adult?** Duration effect is trivial; the halved-incidence effect does NOT transfer from marathoners to sedentary adults
- **Funding split:** Not reported as a funding split; large independent Cochrane base · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable — effect is small but consistent across many trials, not outlier-driven
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found; GI upset and oxalate kidney-stone risk at gram doses
- **Conditional on:** Incidence benefit applies only to short bouts of extreme physical/cold stress (marathons, sub-arctic exercise)
- **Primary sources:** Hemilä H, Chalker E. Cochrane Database Syst Rev 2013;CD000980, PMID 23440782; Douglas RM et al. earlier Cochrane iterations

### Vitamin D (respiratory infection prevention) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for immunity if you are vitamin D replete. The one honest benefit is correcting genuine deficiency, which is cheap and does not require an 'immune' product. In replete adults the effect is null.

- **Evidence base:** ~40+ RCTs; two large IPD/aggregate meta-analyses — total n ≈ >45,000 across meta-analyses (VITAL alone n=25,871)
- **Largest independent trial:** VITAL (Manson/Martineau, NEJM 2019; respiratory analysis Thorax 2021), n=25,871, 2000 IU/day — no reduction in ARI
- **Effect size (CI):** 2021 aggregate: OR 0.94 (0.88-1.00), p=0.057 — NULL. 2017 IPD overall OR 0.88 (0.81-0.96) but diluted to null once VIDARIS/ViDA/VITAL added. Deficiency subgroup (baseline <25 nmol/L) 2017 OR 0.30 (0.17-0.53). Dose-response trap: on bone, 4000-10000 IU LOWERED BMD vs 400 IU (Burt JAMA 2019) — more is not better.
- **Clears MCID?** No in replete adults (null); yes only when correcting frank deficiency
- **Dose — trials:** Daily 400-4000 IU (10-100 mcg); daily/weekly dosing effective, single large bolus doses NOT effective · **retail (active):** 1000-2000 IU (25-50 mcg) cholecalciferol daily — overlaps trial range
- **Population studied:** Mixed: general adults, children, and deficient subgroups across ~40 RCTs. Benefit concentrated in profoundly deficient participants. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Poor — benefit is a deficiency-repletion effect, does not transfer to a replete healthy adult
- **Funding split:** Largely government/independent-funded (VITAL, ViDA, VIDARIS); not a funding artifact · **Outlier leverage:** The 2017 'positive' pooled effect lost significance once later large independent trials were added — the signal hinged on smaller early trials
- **Regulatory / safety:** No respiratory-specific advisory; hypercalcemia/toxicity at sustained high intake is the safety ceiling
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For replete adults, sunlight/diet; deficiency is corrected with cheap generic D3, not an immune-branded product
- **Conditional on:** Baseline 25(OH)D below ~25-30 nmol/L (frank deficiency), corrected with daily or weekly (not bolus) dosing
- **Primary sources:** Jolliffe DA et al. Lancet Diabetes Endocrinol 2021, PMID 33798465; Martineau AR et al. BMJ 2017;356:i6583, PMID 28202713; VITAL respiratory analysis (Thorax 2021); Burt LA et al. JAMA 2019;322:736 (bone dose-response), PMID 31454046; Jolliffe DA et al. Lancet Diabetes Endocrinol 2024 stratified aggregate

### Echinacea — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. Across 24 blinded trials and 4,600+ participants, echinacea shows no reliable treatment benefit and at best a weak, non-significant, clinically irrelevant prevention trend. Preparations are heterogeneous and non-comparable.

- **Evidence base:** 24 double-blind RCTs, 33 comparisons — total n ≈ 4,631
- **Largest independent trial:** Cochrane pooled base (Karsch-Volk 2014) — no single positive independent trial stands out
- **Effect size (CI):** Prevention: post-hoc pooled RRR ~10-20%, not statistically significant, deemed of questionable clinical relevance. Treatment: only 2 of 6 duration trials significant; no consistent effect.
- **Clears MCID?** No — even the weak prevention trend does not reach clinical relevance
- **Dose — trials:** Highly variable — different species (purpurea/angustifolia/pallida), plant parts, and extraction methods; no standard dose · **retail (active):** Wildly variable and often not matched to any positive trial preparation
- **Population studied:** Adults and children, prevention and treatment of the common cold · **transfers to healthy adult?** No consistent benefit to transfer
- **Funding split:** Not a clean funding artifact; the problem is preparation heterogeneity and weak effects · **Outlier leverage:** Heterogeneity across preparations, not a single outlier
- **Regulatory / safety:** TGA groups echinacea with andrographis for allergic-reaction/anaphylaxis risk; caution in atopic individuals
- **Primary sources:** Karsch-Volk M et al. Cochrane Database Syst Rev 2014;CD000530, PMID 24554461; Shah SA et al. Lancet Infect Dis 2007 meta (DARE)

### Elderberry (Sambucus nigra) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. The favorable meta-analysis is built almost entirely on small, manufacturer-funded trials, and the pooled effect leans on the industry-sponsored air-traveler study. Independent replication is essentially absent, and a subsequent trial in influenza failed to show benefit.

- **Evidence base:** 4 small RCTs (Hawkins 2019 meta) — total n ≈ 180 (Hawkins meta); Tiralongo n=312 enrolled
- **Largest independent trial:** No adequately powered independent trial exists; the largest data are sponsor-linked (Tiralongo n=312)
- **Effect size (CI):** Hawkins 2019 reported a large pooled symptom-reduction SMD, but the estimate is dominated by Tiralongo 2016 (industry-funded) and small n; wide, fragile CIs
- **Clears MCID?** Cannot be trusted — nominal reductions exist but rest on compromised inputs
- **Dose — trials:** Standardized extract (BerryPharma) ~600-1500 mg/day, or ~15 mL syrup up to 4x/day · **retail (active):** Gummies/syrups frequently deliver far less standardized anthocyanin/extract than trials; labels rarely state a comparable standardized dose · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Small trials in air travelers and self-limited colds/flu; tiny samples · **transfers to healthy adult?** Unclear; underpowered and confounded by sponsorship
- **Funding split:** Pooled result positive; independent-only result effectively vanishes — Tiralongo 2016 funded by Iprona AG (BerryPharma brand). No credible independent replication. · **Outlier leverage:** Yes — remove the industry-funded Tiralongo trial and the meta-analysis has little left ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** Raw/unripe elderberry contains cyanogenic glycosides (sambunigrin) — nausea/cyanide risk if inadequately processed; commercial extracts are heat-treated
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: Funding-source subgroup / independent replication (Check 1) — reinforced by outlier leverage (Check 2)). I tried to move the 2 in both directions and it held. It cannot go up: Hawkins 2019 (PMID 30670267, total n=180, "large mean effect size") pools only 4 small trials that are all product-branded or industry-linked (Tiralongo 2016/Iprona-BerryPharma; Zakay-Rones 2004 & 1995/Sambucol; Kong 2009/ViraBLOC), so there is no independent positive trial — funding-split and outlier-leverage checks both fail. The decisive blow is Macknin 2020 (PMID 32929634), the one reasonably independent, investigator-initiated ER influenza RCT, which found no benefit and numerically slightly worse outcomes (5.3 vs 4.9 days, p=0.57), i.e. independent replication failed. Retail gummies/syrups routinely deliver less standardized anthocyanin/extract than the ~600-1500 mg standardized trial doses, so retail_below_trial holds. It also should not drop to 1, because compromised-but-real human RCT data exist — the textbook definition of a 2 (mechanism/positive-but-industry-concentrated outrunning independent outcome). No adverse dose-response or major regulatory efficacy advisory beyond the well-known raw-elderberry cyanogenic-glycoside issue, which is neutralized by heat-treated commercial extracts.
- **Primary sources:** Tiralongo E et al. Nutrients 2016;8(4):182, PMID 27023596 (Iprona-funded); Hawkins J et al. Complement Ther Med 2019;42:361, PMID 30670267

### Ginseng (Panax quinquefolius, COLD-fX/CVT-E002) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. A few trials report modest reductions in cold frequency/duration, but the evidence is concentrated in manufacturer-funded studies of a single proprietary extract, with no convincing independent confirmation and low overall quality.

- **Evidence base:** ~5 RCTs of proprietary American ginseng extract — total n ≈ ~800 across the better trials (e.g., Predy 2005 n=323)
- **Largest independent trial:** Predy 2005 (CMAJ) is sponsor-linked; no strong independent trial
- **Effect size (CI):** Predy 2005: fewer recurrent colds and reduced total symptom-days over 4 months; effect modest, trials small and sponsor-linked, later independent reviews (Seida 2011) judged evidence insufficient/low-quality
- **Clears MCID?** Uncertain — modest and not independently confirmed
- **Dose — trials:** CVT-E002 (COLD-fX) 400 mg/day standardized poly-furanosyl-pyranosyl-saccharides · **retail (active):** Proprietary product at 400 mg/day; generic ginseng products are not equivalent
- **Population studied:** Community adults and older adults, cold prevention over a winter season · **transfers to healthy adult?** Marginal; sponsor-linked and low-certainty
- **Funding split:** Concentrated in Afexa/CV Technologies (COLD-fX manufacturer) trials; independent evidence weak · **Outlier leverage:** Small sponsor-linked trials; not robust to independent scrutiny ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** No major advisory; interactions with anticoagulants/hypoglycemics noted
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: funding-source subgroup). I tried to lift the score by finding an independent positive trial or a robust pooled effect, and could not. Every positive result (Predy 2005: 0.25 fewer colds, CI 0.04-0.45; McElhaney trials) traces to the manufacturer, and the largest, best-powered study (High 2012, n=293) was flatly negative on both prespecified primary endpoints (ARI days 8.5 vs 6.8, numerically worse), while the independent Seida 2011 review of 5 trials (n=747) judged the evidence insufficient for incidence/severity with only a tentative duration signal. I also tried to sink it below 2: the retail product IS the trial product (COLD-fX 400 mg/day = trial dose, so retail_below_trial is false — this check does NOT count against it), and there is no serious regulatory safety advisory (only a Health Canada rejection of an efficacy claim and routine anticoagulant/hypoglycemic interaction notes). But real human outcome data on a prespecified endpoint does exist, so it is not a 1. This lands squarely on the rubric's definition of 2: positive results concentrated in industry-funded/low-quality studies, mechanism outrunning outcome. Effect size is also marginal versus any MCID (~one fewer cold every four years in Predy). Score holds; compromised confirmed.
- **Conditional on:** Specific CVT-E002 extract; not a class effect for ginseng products generally
- **Primary sources:** Predy GN et al. CMAJ 2005;173:1043, PMID 16247099; Seida JK et al. Evid Based Complement Alternat Med 2011 (review)

### Pelargonium sidoides (EPs 7630, Umckaloabo) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for general immunity. Trials suggest faster symptom resolution in acute bronchitis/cold, but nearly all come from a single manufacturer, methodological/quality concerns are noted by Cochrane, and it is a treatment for an active infection, not prevention.

- **Evidence base:** ~8-10 RCTs (Cochrane Timmer 2013) — total n ≈ >2,000 across trials
- **Largest independent trial:** Few/none independent; base is manufacturer-driven
- **Effect size (CI):** Cochrane: may improve symptom resolution in acute bronchitis (reduced symptom scores by day 7), but evidence graded low quality with high risk of bias and reliance on one manufacturer's trials
- **Clears MCID?** Uncertain given quality limitations
- **Dose — trials:** EPs 7630 root extract, ~30 drops 3x/day or 20 mg tablets 3x/day · **retail (active):** Standardized EPs 7630 products at label dose (where available)
- **Population studied:** Adults and children with acute bronchitis, sinusitis, or common cold (treatment, not prevention) · **transfers to healthy adult?** Not a prevention agent; applies to acutely ill
- **Funding split:** Overwhelmingly Schwabe/ISO-Arzneimittel (manufacturer) trials; independent replication sparse · **Outlier leverage:** Single-sponsor evidence base — the classic funding-concentration problem ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** European regulators (incl. UK/Germany) flagged rare hepatotoxicity reports with pelargonium products
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: Check 1 - Funding-source concentration (single-manufacturer, Schwabe-sponsored evidence base with no independent replication)). I tried to break the score in both directions and it held at 2. Verified via PubMed (Cochrane Timmer 2013, PMID 24146345) and the EMA Pelargonii radix assessment: 8-10 investigator-initiated RCTs (n>2,000), nearly all sponsored by Dr. Willmar Schwabe GmbH, same investigators and region, funnel-plot suspicion of publication bias, GRADE quality low, and effect inconsistent by formulation (liquid appeared effective, tablets did not) - the textbook single-sponsor / mechanism-outruns-outcome pattern that defines a rubric 2. It cannot fall to 1 because a real multi-trial human outcome base exists and Cochrane did detect a symptom-resolution signal for the liquid extract; it cannot rise to 2.5+ because that entire signal sits inside compromised, low-quality, industry-funded data with no independent replication. Retail is NOT below trial dose - genuine EPs 7630 products (Umckaloabo/Umcka) are the same standardized extract at label dose - so that check does not help the score. The EMA confirms a real (rare, frequency-unknown) hepatotoxicity signal, and the compound is a treatment for active acute bronchitis/cold, not an immunity/prevention agent, so the 'do not buy for general immunity' verdict is correct.
- **Conditional on:** Treatment of an active acute bronchitis/cold episode, not prophylaxis
- **Primary sources:** Timmer A et al. Cochrane Database Syst Rev 2013;CD006323, PMID 24146345

### Garlic / allicin — **1.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. The entire prevention claim rests on a single low-quality, manufacturer-funded trial that has never been replicated. Cochrane could include only that one study. This is marketing propped up by one weak result.

- **Evidence base:** 1 RCT meeting Cochrane inclusion — total n ≈ 146 (Josling 2001)
- **Largest independent trial:** None exists
- **Effect size (CI):** Josling 2001: 24 colds (garlic) vs 65 (placebo) over 12 weeks; fewer days ill. Single trial, high risk of bias, no CI-robust replication.
- **Clears MCID?** Cannot be relied upon — one biased trial
- **Dose — trials:** Allicin-standardized supplement ~180 mg allicin potential/day · **retail (active):** Aged garlic/garlic powder capsules with highly variable and usually far lower actual allicin yield (allicin is unstable; many products deliver little bioavailable allicin) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults, 12-week winter prevention (single trial) · **transfers to healthy adult?** Unverified; single unreplicated study
- **Funding split:** The one trial is industry-linked (garlic supplement manufacturer); no independent confirmation · **Outlier leverage:** Entire evidence base IS the outlier — remove Josling 2001 and nothing remains ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** None specific; bleeding risk with anticoagulants at high doses
- **Primary sources:** Lissiman E et al. Cochrane Database Syst Rev 2014;CD006206, PMID 25386977; Josling P. Adv Ther 2001;18:189, PMID 11697022

### Quercetin — **1.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. The large, well-powered RCT was flatly null for URTI overall. The only positive signal is a post-hoc subgroup (fit, over-40, at the higher dose). Poor oral bioavailability compounds the weakness. Mechanism-driven marketing.

- **Evidence base:** 1 large RCT (plus small exercise studies) — total n ≈ 1,002 (Nieman/Heinz 2010)
- **Largest independent trial:** Nieman/Heinz 2010, n=1002 — null overall
- **Effect size (CI):** Overall: no significant effect on URTI incidence or severity in any prespecified analysis. Post-hoc subgroup (fit, >=40, 1000 mg): -36% severity, -31% sick days — hypothesis-generating only, wide uncertainty.
- **Clears MCID?** No — overall null; subgroup effect is exploratory
- **Dose — trials:** 1000 mg/day (the dose with the subgroup signal); 500 mg/day also tested · **retail (active):** Typically 500 mg/day — below the 1000 mg dose that produced the subgroup effect · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Community adults 18-85; positive signal only in post-hoc subgroup (age >=40, self-rated fit) · **transfers to healthy adult?** No — the primary population showed nothing
- **Funding split:** Not the core issue; the primary result is null · **Outlier leverage:** Effect exists only in a carved-out subgroup, not the main analysis
- **Conditional on:** Only the exploratory subgroup (physically fit adults >=40 at 1000 mg/day) showed anything
- **Primary sources:** Heinz SA, Henson DA, Nieman DC et al. Pharmacol Res 2010;62:237, PMID 20478383
