## Gut

### Psyllium husk (ispaghula) — **4/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Buy — the strongest evidence in the category. Cheap, independent, guideline-endorsed soluble/gel-forming fiber for IBS global symptoms and for chronic constipation. Titrate to an effective dose and give it 4 weeks.

- **Evidence base:** Soluble-fiber IBS meta-analysis (Moayyedi/Ford 2014, 7 psyllium RCTs); constipation meta-analysis (van der Schoot, AJCN 2024) and elderly meta-analysis (Foods 2025). — total n ≈ ~500 in soluble-fiber IBS pooling; several hundred more across constipation RCTs
- **Largest independent trial:** Bijkerk et al. 2009, BMJ (n=275, primary care): psyllium 10 g/day significantly improved IBS symptom severity vs placebo and bran at 3 months.
- **Effect size (CI):** IBS: symptoms not improving RR 0.83 (95% CI 0.73-0.94), NNT≈7 (Ford/Moayyedi 2014, BMJ). Constipation: significant increase in stool frequency and improved consistency (AJCN 2024 meta-analysis; ~66% responders vs ~41% control).
- **Clears MCID?** Yes — modest but clinically usable; effect largest in IBS-C and constipation.
- **Dose — trials:** ≥10 g/day psyllium for ≥4 weeks (optimal); IBS trials commonly 10-20 g/day · **retail (active):** ~3.4-5 g psyllium per single serving (e.g. Metamucil rounded tsp ~3.4 g) — most users take one serving, under the ≥10 g/day trial target · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Adults with IBS (all subtypes, best in IBS-C) and adults/elderly with chronic idiopathic constipation. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Partial — a healthy adult with adequate fiber gets little symptom benefit; benefit is in constipation/IBS or low-fiber diets.
- **Funding split:** Not a decisive issue; positive results appear in independent/academic pooled analyses (Cochrane-grade fiber reviews). · **Outlier leverage:** No single-study dependence; effect is consistent across the soluble-fiber pool (contrast: insoluble bran shows no benefit and can worsen symptoms).
- **Regulatory / safety:** None. Well-established safety; caution only re esophageal/bowel obstruction if taken with insufficient fluid.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 4; decisive check: Check 4 — dose in trials (10-20 g/day) vs retail single serving (~3.4 g); the only refutation with teeth, but mitigable by titration). I tried to break the 4 on all seven checks and it held. Verified in the primary literature: Ford/Moayyedi 2014 BMJ soluble-fiber RR 0.83 (0.73-0.94), NNT 7, an above-MCID effect from independent/academic pooling (bran correctly shows nothing, RR 0.90), and Bijkerk 2009 (n=275, 10 g/day) beat placebo. No funding contamination, no single-outlier dependence, no adverse dose-response, and no regulator advisory (FDA in fact carries an authorized psyllium soluble-fiber heart-health claim). The only vulnerability that survived scrutiny is the retail-vs-trial dose gap (one ~3.4 g scoop vs the ≥10 g trial target), but unlike collagen/reishi this is cheaply titratable, so it lowers real-world efficacy for under-dosers without sinking the compound. Minor caveat: the specific van der Schoot 2024 constipation cite is weaker than framed (found psyllium inferior to kiwifruit, concluded evidence is scarce), but ACG endorsement and older CIC data still support the constipation benefit. Stays a 4, not a 5, because individual trials are small, the effect is modest, and benefit is condition-specific rather than general.
- **Conditional on:** Take with adequate water; benefit strongest in IBS-C/constipation, weaker in IBS-D pain.
- **Product:** NOW Foods NOW Foods Psyllium Husk Powder — ~6 g psyllium husk per serving (1 rounded tablespoon) active/serving; none — ⚠ per-serving dose BELOW trial dose. Powder is preferred over Metamucil-style single scoops (~3.4 g) because it lets the user titrate up to the >=10 g/day trial dose without excessive capsule counts. A single ~6 g serving is below the >=10 g/day optimal target, so ~2 servings/day are needed; introduce gradually with adequate water to limit bloating. Also sold in caps (would require many caps to hit 10 g). Link is an Amazon search URL.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=NOW+Foods+psyllium+husk+powder
- **Primary sources:** Moayyedi P, Ford AC, et al. Am J Gastroenterol 2014 / BMJ soluble fiber meta-analysis (RR 0.83); Bijkerk CJ, et al. BMJ 2009;339:b3154 (independent primary-care RCT) PMID 19713235; van der Schoot A, et al. Am J Clin Nutr 2024 (fiber for chronic constipation meta-analysis); Foods 2025;14:2315 (fiber for constipation in elderly meta-analysis)

### Saccharomyces boulardii (CNCM I-745) — **3.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Conditional buy — genuinely effective for a specific job (preventing antibiotic-associated diarrhea, shortening acute infectious diarrhea), NOT for routine 'gut health.' Independent evidence, meaningful NNT. Avoid in the critically ill.

- **Evidence base:** AAD: ~21 RCTs (Szajewska/Kołodziej 2015 meta-analysis); pediatric acute diarrhea: 6+ RCTs. — total n ≈ >4000 across AAD RCTs
- **Largest independent trial:** Pooled Szajewska & Kołodziej 2015 (Aliment Pharmacol Ther) across ~21 RCTs; consistent 50%+ relative risk reduction.
- **Effect size (CI):** AAD prevention: RR 0.47 (95% CI 0.35-0.63); absolute risk ~18.7%→8.5%, NNT≈10 (95% CI 9-13). Pediatric acute diarrhea: reduces duration by ~-1 day.
- **Clears MCID?** Yes for AAD and acute pediatric diarrhea (NNT≈10; ~1 day shorter illness).
- **Dose — trials:** 250-500 mg BID (5-10 billion CFU/day) · **retail (active):** 250-500 mg (~5-10 billion CFU) per capsule — matches trial dosing
- **Population studied:** Adults and children on antibiotics (AAD prevention) and children with acute gastroenteritis; NOT healthy adults seeking general gut benefits. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. Benefit is condition-specific (antibiotics, acute infection); no evidence it helps an asymptomatic healthy gut.
- **Funding split:** Some manufacturer involvement (Biocodex), but AAD benefit reproduces in independent meta-analyses and Cochrane-adjacent syntheses. · **Outlier leverage:** No — AAD effect is robust across many RCTs, not one-study-driven.
- **Regulatory / safety:** EMA (2018) restriction/contraindication: do NOT use S. boulardii products in critically ill or immunocompromised patients or those with a central venous catheter — risk of fungemia (Saccharomyces cerevisiae). CDC EID 2021 (vol 27) documents fungemia cases.
- **Conditional on:** Concurrent antibiotic course, or acute infectious diarrhea. No demonstrated role in IBS or routine maintenance.
- **Product:** Florastor (Biocodex) Florastor Daily Probiotic Supplement (250 mg capsules) — 250 mg (~5 billion CFU) per capsule active/serving; none. Florastor is the reference product for CNCM I-745 (S. boulardii lyo), the exact strain used in the trials. One 250 mg cap matches the low end of the per-dose range; take 2/day (or 1 BID) to reach 500 mg / ~10 billion CFU/day. A 'Florastor Max' 500 mg version also exists (1 cap/day = 250 mg base ingredient; check label). Link is an Amazon search URL.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Florastor+daily+probiotic+250mg+Saccharomyces+boulardii
- **Primary sources:** Szajewska H, Kołodziej M. Aliment Pharmacol Ther 2015 (AAD meta-analysis, RR 0.47); McFarland LV. World J Gastroenterol 2010 (S. boulardii meta-analysis); EMA 2018 referral/PRAC restriction on S. boulardii medicinal products (ICU/CVC contraindication); Rannikko J, et al. Emerg Infect Dis 2021;27(8) (fungemia case series)

### L-glutamine (post-infectious IBS-D) — **3/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Conditional and narrow. One striking single-center trial in post-infectious IBS-D with measured increased gut permeability — huge effect, but unreplicated, single research group, and needs 15 g/day (retail sells ~5 g). Not for healthy adults or general 'leaky gut.'

- **Evidence base:** 1 RCT (Zhou 2019, Gut) — the effect rests entirely on this single study. — total n ≈ 115
- **Largest independent trial:** Zhou Q, et al. Gut 2019;68:996-1002 (n=115) — the only trial; not yet independently reproduced.
- **Effect size (CI):** ≥50-point IBS-SS reduction in 79.6% (glutamine) vs 5.8% (placebo) — very large; also reduced daily stool frequency and normalized lactulose/mannitol ratio (Zhou Q, et al. Gut 2019;68:996-1002).
- **Clears MCID?** Yes in the studied population (huge responder gap) — but plausibility strained by effect size and lack of replication.
- **Dose — trials:** 5 g three times daily = 15 g/day for 8 weeks · **retail (active):** ~5 g per serving (typical retail dosing is 5 g/day — one-third of the trial dose) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Adults with post-infectious IBS-D AND confirmed increased intestinal permeability (urinary lactulose/mannitol ratio ≥0.07), documented enteric infection ≥1 year prior. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. Requires post-infectious IBS-D with measured hyperpermeability; no rationale in a healthy adult.
- **Funding split:** Not industry-funded per se, but single-center, single-investigator-group (Verne/Zhou), unreplicated — the grade hinges on one study. · **Outlier leverage:** Yes — the entire evidence base is one trial with an implausibly large effect; awaits independent replication (NCT06291038 ongoing). ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** None specific; glutamine caution in hepatic encephalopathy/high-dose settings.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 3; decisive check: Check 2 (outlier leverage / replication status)). I tried to break the 3 by attacking the grade's core premise that the effect rests on a single unreplicated, compromised trial. Two of those attacks backfired: Zhou 2019 is NIH-funded with no declared COI (not industry-compromised), and an independent group produced a second positive RCT at the same 15 g/day dose (Rastgoo 2021, Frontiers in Nutrition, n=44, ~58% IBS-SS reduction as a low-FODMAP adjunct), so the direction of effect is not single-outlier-dependent. What legitimately weakens it and holds the score at 3 rather than 4: both trials are small and short (8 weeks), Rastgoo is confounded by a FODMAP co-intervention and a broader IBS population, and Zhou's flagship 14-fold effect (79.6% vs a suspiciously low 5.8% placebo response) is implausibly large and unreplicated at that magnitude, with the confirmatory Rouen trial not completing until ~2029. Retail dosing (~5 g/day) is one-third of the 15 g/day used in both trials, and the benefit is confined to post-infectious IBS-D with measured hyperpermeability — no transfer to healthy adults or generic 'leaky gut.' Net: a real, narrowly-scoped signal in small trials needing specific conditions — a textbook 3.
- **Conditional on:** Post-infectious IBS-D with laboratory-confirmed increased intestinal permeability; and dosing at the full 15 g/day.
- **Product:** Thorne Thorne L-Glutamine Powder — ~5 g L-glutamine per serving (1 scoop) active/serving; NSF Certified for Sport — ⚠ per-serving dose BELOW trial dose. Chosen over generic 'third-party tested' brands because Thorne L-Glutamine is NSF Certified for Sport. Per-serving 5 g is only one-third of the 15 g/day trial dose, so dose_adequate=false: the user must take 5 g three times daily. Powder makes the 15 g/day dose practical vs capsules. Link is an Amazon search URL.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Thorne+L-Glutamine+powder+NSF+Certified+for+Sport
- **Primary sources:** Zhou Q, et al. Gut 2019;68:996-1002 PMID 29122852 (single RCT); NCT01414244 (registry); NCT06291038 (ongoing replication in IBS with impaired permeability)

### Partially hydrolyzed guar gum (PHGG) [added, in-category] — **3/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Conditional. A better-tolerated soluble fiber than inulin, with small RCTs suggesting improved IBS symptoms/bowel habit and less bloating than wheat bran, but trials are small and some industry-linked. Reasonable low-gas fiber alternative to psyllium; not first-line.

- **Evidence base:** A handful of small RCTs (e.g. Parisi 2002, n=188 PHGG vs wheat bran) plus small constipation studies. — total n ≈ Low hundreds
- **Largest independent trial:** Parisi GC, et al. Dig Dis Sci 2002 (n=188): PHGG comparable to wheat bran for IBS with better tolerability.
- **Effect size (CI):** Parisi 2002: PHGG improved IBS symptom scores comparably to wheat bran with better tolerability and greater patient preference; effect sizes modest, trials small.
- **Clears MCID?** Borderline — modest symptom/bowel-habit improvement, better tolerability profile than inulin.
- **Dose — trials:** 5-10 g/day · **retail (active):** ~5-6 g per serving (e.g. Sunfiber); often single-serving, at/below the effective range · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Adults with IBS (mixed subtypes) and functional constipation. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Partial — benefit is in IBS/constipation, not healthy adults.
- **Funding split:** Some manufacturer involvement (Sunfiber); independent replication limited. · **Outlier leverage:** Small trial base; not robust.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None; well tolerated.
- **Conditional on:** IBS or constipation where a low-fermentation, low-gas fiber is preferred over psyllium.
- **Product:** Tomorrow's Nutrition (Sunfiber, Taiyo) Tomorrow's Nutrition Sunfiber Prebiotic Fiber Powder — ~6 g PHGG per serving (1 scoop; ~5-6 g fiber) active/serving; none. Uses Sunfiber, the branded PHGG form used in clinical studies (preferred per the branded-ingredient rule). One ~6 g serving falls within the 5-10 g/day effective range, so dose_adequate=true; a second serving reaches the top of the range. Low-viscosity, well tolerated, gluten-free. Anthony's Organic Sunfiber is a comparable alternative. Link is an Amazon search URL.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Tomorrow%27s+Nutrition+Sunfiber+PHGG+prebiotic+fiber+powder
- **Primary sources:** Parisi GC, et al. Dig Dis Sci 2002;47:1697-1704 (PHGG vs bran in IBS); Yasukawa Z, et al. Nutrients 2019 (PHGG bowel habit)

### Peppermint oil (enteric/gastro-resistant, IBS) — **3/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Conditional buy for IBS abdominal pain/global symptoms — the best OTC option in this category, but the effect is fragile: it holds in pooled older trials yet the largest independent trial missed its primary endpoint. Reasonable to trial for 4 weeks; stop if no benefit.

- **Evidence base:** 10 RCTs in the 2022 pooled meta-analysis (Black/Ford), n=1030; plus the independent Weerts 2020 RCT (n=190). — total n ≈ ~1220 across positive pooled trials + largest independent trial
- **Largest independent trial:** Weerts et al. 2020, Gastroenterology (n=190/analyzed ~178): small-intestinal- and ileocolonic-release peppermint oil vs placebo, NO significant benefit on co-primary abdominal pain response or global relief at 8 weeks; only secondary pain-severity outcomes improved.
- **Effect size (CI):** Global symptoms not improving RR 0.65 (95% CI 0.43-0.98), NNT=4; abdominal pain not improving RR 0.76 (95% CI 0.62-0.93), NNT=7 (Black 2022 meta-analysis, Aliment Pharmacol Ther).
- **Clears MCID?** Yes on pooled dichotomous responder outcomes (NNT 4-7 is clinically meaningful) — but the largest trial failed the FDA-standard primary endpoint.
- **Dose — trials:** 180-225 mg enteric/gastro-resistant peppermint oil (~0.2 mL) 2-3x/day = 360-540 mg/day · **retail (active):** ~180-225 mg peppermint oil per enteric-coated capsule, 1-2 caps 2-3x/day (matches trial range)
- **Population studied:** Adults with IBS (Rome II-IV), mixed subtypes; most trials 2-4 weeks, few high-quality. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. Benefit is in symptomatic IBS; no rationale in a healthy adult.
- **Funding split:** Not cleanly split in the meta-analysis, but many older positive trials were small/industry-linked; the independent, government/academic-funded Weerts 2020 trial (4 Dutch hospitals) was null on both co-primary endpoints (abdominal pain response and global relief at 8 weeks). · **Outlier leverage:** Yes — pooled positivity leans on small, short, older trials with likely unblinding (menthol taste/smell); the single best-powered independent trial (Weerts 2020, Gastroenterology 158:123-136) removed the primary-endpoint effect. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** None material for efficacy; label caution re GERD/heartburn aggravation with non-enteric formulations. No TGA/EMA/FDA safety advisory found.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **does NOT survive** — score cut (revised 3; decisive check: Outlier/quality leverage (check 2)). I verified Weerts 2020 (PMID 31470006): n=189, 182 mg, 8 weeks, independent Dutch academic funding, and both co-primary endpoints null (pain response P=.170/.385; overall relief P=.317/.351) - only secondary continuous outcomes moved. I verified Alammar 2019 (PMID 30654773): pooled RR 2.39 global / 1.78 pain, NNT 3-4, I2=0%, but 6/12 high attrition-bias and 10/12 not reporting randomization, with menthol taste undermining blinding. The pooled positivity is genuine but rests entirely on small, short, old, likely-unblinded trials, and evaporated in the only rigorous, best-powered, independent trial. Retail dose does match trial dose (~182 mg enteric), it is safe, and there is no regulator advisory - so it is not marketing (not a 2). But 3.5 overweights the pooled NNT relative to the collapse under FDA-standard endpoints; a 3.0 ('real signal, needs specific conditions to appear') is the honest grade. It remains a reasonable conditional trial for symptomatic IBS, just with a more fragile evidence base than 3.5 implies.
- **Conditional on:** IBS with abdominal pain/spasm; use enteric or ileocolonic-release formulation to limit reflux/heartburn.
- **Product:** Nature's Way Nature's Way Pepogest, Peppermint Oil (Enteric-Coated Softgels) — ~180 mg peppermint oil (0.2 mL) per enteric-coated softgel active/serving; none. Classic enteric-coated peppermint oil matching the trial's 0.2 mL/~180 mg gastro-resistant capsule. Take 1 softgel 2-3x/day (up to 2 per dose) to sit inside the 360-540 mg/day trial window. Enteric coating is essential (avoids esophageal reflux/heartburn and delivers oil to the intestine). Link is an Amazon search URL; verify it is the enteric-coated Pepogest softgel, not a plain peppermint oil.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Nature%27s+Way+Pepogest+peppermint+oil+enteric+coated
- **Primary sources:** Black CJ, et al. Aliment Pharmacol Ther 2022 (10-RCT meta-analysis, NNT 4/7); Weerts ZZRM, et al. Gastroenterology 2020;158:123-136 (independent RCT, null primary endpoint) PMID 32289372; Alammar N, et al. BMC Complement Altern Med 2019;19:21 (pooled RR 2.39 global improvement) PMID 30654773; Ford AC, et al. ACG monograph / IBS guideline conditional recommendation

### Lactobacillus rhamnosus GG (LGG) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Weak buy at best. Its flagship claim (acute pediatric diarrhea) was overturned by two large independent trials; residual benefit for antibiotic-associated diarrhea is modest. Do not buy for adult IBS or general gut health.

- **Evidence base:** Acute diarrhea: many small older RCTs + 2 large 2018 independent nulls; AAD: pooled with other Lactobacillus. — total n ≈ ~1800 in the two large independent gastroenteritis trials alone
- **Largest independent trial:** Schnadower D, et al. NEJM 2018;379:2002-2014 (n=943, 10 US pediatric EDs, NIH-funded): LGG 10^10 CFU BID = NO benefit over placebo for acute gastroenteritis.
- **Effect size (CI):** Schnadower 2018 NEJM (n=943): NO benefit — moderate/severe disease in 11.8% LGG vs 12.6% placebo (RR 0.96, 95% CI 0.68-1.35). Freedman 2018 (PROBIOTIC, n=886) also null. Older meta-analyses claimed ~-1 day duration but on low-quality evidence.
- **Clears MCID?** No — the high-quality independent trials show no clinically meaningful effect on acute gastroenteritis.
- **Dose — trials:** 1×10^10 CFU BID (2×10^10 CFU/day) · **retail (active):** 10-20 billion CFU/day (matches trial dose)
- **Population studied:** Children with acute gastroenteritis (trials); some AAD-prevention data in children and adults. Adult IBS/general use largely untested. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. No credible healthy-adult gut-health outcome data.
- **Funding split:** Positive pooled estimates lean on small, older, often industry-linked/low-quality trials; the two definitive nulls were NIH/independently funded. · **Outlier leverage:** Yes — meta-analytic positivity depended on numerous small low-quality studies; adding the large rigorous trials collapses the effect. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** General probiotic-in-critically-ill caution (bacteremia case reports); no specific efficacy advisory. Avoid in immunocompromised/central-line patients.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2.5; decisive check: Outlier leverage / funding-quality collapse (checks 1-2), reinforced by population transfer (check 5)). I tried to break 2.5 in both directions and it held. Primary sources confirm the flagship claim is dead: Schnadower 2018 NEJM (n=943, NIH-funded) is a clean LGG-specific null (RR 0.96, 0.68-1.35), and Cochrane 2020 (Collinson) collapsed the old ~25h-duration benefit to RR 1.00 (0.91-1.09) for diarrhea >=48h in low-bias trials, with publication bias explicitly demonstrated — textbook outlier-leverage/funding collapse, so is_compromised is true. Pushing to 2.0 fails because a real residual signal survives: pediatric antibiotic-associated diarrhea shows high-dose probiotics (incl. LGG) RR 0.37, NNTB 6 at moderate GRADE certainty — that is better than industry-only/low-quality data. Pushing to 3.0 fails because that benefit is pooled (not LGG-specific; Freedman 2018's null even used a different strain), confined to children on antibiotics, and does not transfer to the healthy-adult/general-gut/IBS uses LGG is actually marketed for, where there is no credible outcome data. Retail dose (10-20B CFU/day) matches trial dose, so retail_below_trial is false and dose is not a rescue or a further downgrade. 2.5 is well-calibrated on the 2-vs-3 boundary.
- **Conditional on:** Any residual value is limited to AAD prevention in children/adults on antibiotics; not for acute viral gastroenteritis or IBS.
- **Primary sources:** Schnadower D, et al. NEJM 2018;379:2002-14 PMID 30462939 (large independent null); Freedman SB, et al. NEJM 2018;379:2015-26 (PROBIOTIC trial, null); Szajewska H, et al. Aliment Pharmacol Ther 2019 (WJG meta-analysis; low-quality evidence, effect attenuated with 2018 trials); Szajewska H, Kołodziej M. Aliment Pharmacol Ther 2015 (LGG for AAD)

### Multi-strain probiotics (IBS / general gut) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Do not buy as a class for IBS or 'gut health.' Meta-analyses show a signal but at low/very-low GRADE certainty with heavy heterogeneity and industry funding; ACG and AGA recommend against routine use outside a trial. Any benefit is strain-and-product-specific, not a class effect.

- **Evidence base:** ~50+ RCTs across meta-analyses (e.g. Goodoory/Ford 2023 Gastroenterology; network meta-analyses 2023-2024). — total n ≈ >3000 in pooled IBS analyses
- **Largest independent trial:** No single large independent trial establishes a class effect; guideline bodies (ACG 2020, AGA 2020) explicitly decline to recommend for global IBS.
- **Effect size (CI):** Pooled global symptom benefit exists (e.g. RR of symptoms persisting ~0.7-0.8) but GRADE certainty low/very low; effect not reproducible across strains. No robust, MCID-clearing, replicated class effect.
- **Clears MCID?** Uncertain — statistical signal, but low certainty and heterogeneity make clinical significance unreliable.
- **Dose — trials:** Highly variable (1×10^9 to 5×10^10+ CFU/day, single- to multi-strain) · **retail (active):** 1-50 billion CFU/day, product-dependent; often does not match any trialed strain/dose
- **Population studied:** Adults with IBS (Rome II-IV), highly heterogeneous strains/doses/durations. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. No credible outcome benefit in healthy adults; 'microbiome support' marketing outruns data.
- **Funding split:** Substantial manufacturer funding across the positive literature; independent replication is inconsistent. · **Outlier leverage:** Yes — pooled effects are driven by specific products/strains and are heterogeneous; not a robust general effect. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** Probiotic bacteremia/fungemia risk in critically ill/immunocompromised (general caution). No efficacy endorsement from ACG/AGA for IBS.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2.5; decisive check: Check 2 (outlier leverage / heterogeneity): the "class effect" collapses because Goodoory/Ford 2023 (Gastroenterology; 82 RCTs, n=10,332) rates combination/multi-strain probiotics as VERY LOW GRADE certainty across every endpoint (global symptoms, abdominal pain, bloating). Only single strains (Escherichia = moderate, Lactobacillus = low) rate higher, so benefit is strain-and-product-specific, not a property of the multi-strain class.). I tried to break the score in both directions and it held at 2.5. Attempt to raise it failed: although the evidence base is large (82 RCTs, >10,000 patients) with a pooled symptom-persistence RR ~0.7-0.8, the definitive independent meta-analysis (Goodoory/Ford 2023) grades multi-strain COMBINATION products at very-low certainty on every endpoint, only 24 of 82 trials were low risk of bias, funding is heavily manufacturer-linked, and both ACG 2021 and AGA 2020 explicitly decline to recommend probiotics for IBS outside a clinical trial. Attempt to lower it to 2.0 was tempting (multi-strain is the weakest GRADE tier + industry-funded + guidelines against) but a genuine academic pooled signal and a benign safety profile keep it above pure marketing. The dose check does not rescue it: retail CFU counts typically meet or exceed trial doses (so retail_below_trial is false), but products rarely match the specific trialed strain, and all benefit is confined to diagnosed IBS with zero outcome data in healthy adults for 'gut health.'
- **Conditional on:** If tried, use a specific strain/product with its own positive RCT (e.g. Bifidobacterium infantis 35624) rather than a generic 'multi-strain' blend.
- **Primary sources:** Goodoory VC, Ford AC, et al. Gastroenterology 2023 (probiotics in IBS meta-analysis); AGA Clinical Practice Guideline on Probiotics, Gastroenterology 2020 (recommend against outside trials); ACG Monograph / IBS Guideline 2020 (Ford et al.); Network meta-analysis, Nutrients 2024;16:2114 (strain-specific, prebiotics/synbiotics not significant)

### Zinc-carnosine (polaprezinc) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Weak/conditional. Real but small-trial evidence for gastric mucosal protection and ulcer healing (mostly Japanese, adjunct to PPI) and one tiny crossover showing it blunts NSAID-induced gut permeability. Not established for 'leaky gut' or IBS. Mechanism ahead of outcomes.

- **Evidence base:** Several small-to-moderate Japanese RCTs (ulcer healing) + tiny permeability crossover + small UC enema pilot. — total n ≈ ~250 in the largest ulcer RCT; n=10 in the key permeability study
- **Largest independent trial:** Mahmood A, et al. Gut 2007 (n=10 crossover): zinc-carnosine 37.5 mg BID prevented indomethacin-induced increase in small-intestinal permeability and stimulated repair. Ulcer efficacy from larger Japanese RCTs.
- **Effect size (CI):** Gastric ulcer: endoscopic improvement ~60% and higher symptom response vs comparator in an ~258-patient RCT. Permeability: prevented the ~3-fold indomethacin-induced rise in gut permeability in healthy volunteers (Mahmood 2007, n=10 crossover).
- **Clears MCID?** Plausibly for ulcer healing as adjunct; unproven for functional gut symptoms/IBS.
- **Dose — trials:** 75 mg/day polaprezinc (37.5 mg BID; delivers ~16 mg elemental zinc) for gut integrity; up to 150 mg/day in ulcer trials · **retail (active):** ~75 mg zinc-carnosine chelate (~16 mg elemental zinc) per typical supplement dose — matches the permeability-trial dose
- **Population studied:** Gastric ulcer patients (adjunct to standard therapy); healthy volunteers given indomethacin; small UC enema pilots — largely Japanese cohorts. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Marginal — the permeability data are in an experimental NSAID-injury model, not everyday healthy-gut benefit.
- **Funding split:** Older Japanese trials, some manufacturer involvement; limited independent Western replication. · **Outlier leverage:** The permeability claim rests on one n=10 study — thin.
- **Regulatory / safety:** Approved as a gastric drug (polaprezinc) in Japan; not an approved therapeutic elsewhere. No major safety advisory; watch cumulative zinc intake (copper depletion) with chronic use.
- **Conditional on:** Gastric ulcer (adjunct to PPI/eradication) or NSAID-associated mucosal injury; not validated for IBS or general 'gut lining' claims.
- **Primary sources:** Mahmood A, et al. Gut 2007;56:168-175 PMID 16777920 (permeability crossover); Polaprezinc gastric ulcer multicenter RCT (2022, positive-controlled); Polaprezinc enema in ulcerative colitis, pilot PMID 24286534

### Butyrate / tributyrin (oral) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. Compelling colonocyte biology, but oral butyrate is largely absorbed before reaching the colon and human outcome trials are small, inconsistent, and include a clear null in pediatric IBD. Mechanism outruns outcome.

- **Evidence base:** Several small RCTs (UC, secondary markers) + a multicenter pediatric IBD null + emerging tributyrin/pediatric-IBS trials. — total n ≈ Tens per trial (e.g. ~36-72)
- **Largest independent trial:** Pieczarkowski/pediatric IBD multicenter RCT (PMC9414716): 12-week oral sodium butyrate add-on did NOT improve remission over placebo.
- **Effect size (CI):** Pediatric IBD: 12-week sodium butyrate add-on = NO difference in remission vs placebo (multicenter RCT). Some small adult UC trials show improvement in secondary markers (ESR, Mayo subscores, mood) but not robust clinical remission endpoints.
- **Clears MCID?** No — no consistent clinically meaningful outcome; the cleanest add-on remission trial was null.
- **Dose — trials:** Oral sodium butyrate 300-1800 mg/day (some trials use unusual 600 mg/kg dosing); calcium butyrate 500 mg/day (pediatric IBS) · **retail (active):** Sodium/calcium butyrate ~500-600 mg/day — but colonic delivery is the real limiter, not the label dose
- **Population studied:** Ulcerative colitis and IBD patients; pediatric IBD and pediatric IBS — small heterogeneous trials. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. No healthy-adult outcome data; 'gut lining/SCFA' marketing is mechanistic.
- **Funding split:** Small trials, mixed sponsorship; no independent large positive trial. · **Outlier leverage:** Positive signals come from tiny trials on secondary endpoints; not robust.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None material; a food-grade supplement, no efficacy endorsement.
- **Conditional on:** Any plausible role is adjunctive in IBD under specialist care, not self-directed gut-health use; tributyrin (prodrug) is proposed to improve colonic delivery but lacks outcome trials.
- **Primary sources:** Pediatric IBD sodium butyrate multicenter RCT, PMC9414716 (null on remission); UC butyrate RCT, PMC11930386 (secondary-marker improvement); Calcium butyrate pediatric IBS RCT, PMC12408980

### Digestive enzymes (lactase, alpha-galactosidase, blends) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy generic 'digestive enzyme' blends for IBS/gut health. The only justified uses are enzyme REPLACEMENT for a specific deficiency — lactase for lactose intolerance, and prescription PERT for pancreatic insufficiency, which is not an OTC supplement.

- **Evidence base:** Small targeted RCTs (alpha-galactosidase for GOS/beans: Di Stefano 2007, Hillilä 2016); lactase established for lactose maldigestion; no good RCT for generic multi-enzyme blends in IBS. — total n ≈ Small (tens to low hundreds per targeted trial)
- **Largest independent trial:** Di Stefano M, et al. 2007 and Hillilä M, et al. 2016 — small alpha-galactosidase RCTs showing reduced gas symptoms in GOS-sensitive subjects; no large trial for generic blends.
- **Effect size (CI):** Alpha-galactosidase: significant reduction in flatulence/bloating after high-GOS meals in sensitive subjects (small crossover RCTs). Generic blends: no credible IBS outcome data.
- **Clears MCID?** Only for the matched deficiency (lactose intolerance, GOS sensitivity); not demonstrated for undifferentiated IBS.
- **Dose — trials:** Enzyme-specific (e.g. alpha-galactosidase ~300-1200 GalU with the offending meal; lactase ~3000-9000 FCC units with dairy) · **retail (active):** Highly variable proprietary blends; units often unstated, frequently under-dosed vs the specific-enzyme trials
- **Population studied:** Lactose-intolerant individuals (lactase); GOS-sensitive individuals (alpha-galactosidase); EPI patients (PERT — prescription). · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. A healthy adult with normal enzyme production gains nothing.
- **Funding split:** Not the key issue; the problem is absence of outcome data for blends. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable (no pooled effect to lean on).
- **Regulatory / safety:** PERT (Creon etc.) is FDA-regulated prescription therapy for EPI; OTC 'digestive enzyme' blends are unregulated supplements with unverified enzyme content.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For exocrine pancreatic insufficiency the real intervention is prescription Pancreatic Enzyme Replacement Therapy (PERT); for lactose intolerance the fix is lactase dosing with dairy or dairy avoidance.
- **Conditional on:** Confirmed lactose intolerance (lactase) or symptomatic GOS/bean sensitivity (alpha-galactosidase). EPI requires diagnosis and prescription PERT.
- **Primary sources:** Di Stefano M, et al. Dig Liver Dis 2007 (alpha-galactosidase, gas); Hillilä M, et al. 2016 (alpha-galactosidase RCT); AGA/ACG guidance on PERT for exocrine pancreatic insufficiency

### Prebiotics (inulin, GOS/FOS, inulin-type fructans) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for IBS symptoms. The best meta-analysis shows no effect on abdominal pain or bloating overall, and inulin-type fructans can WORSEN flatulence. Rising bifidobacteria is a biomarker, not a symptom outcome. Wrong dose-response direction.

- **Evidence base:** Wilson/So et al. 2022 AJCN meta-analysis of RCTs across prebiotic types. — total n ≈ Several hundred across pooled RCTs
- **Largest independent trial:** So D, Gibson PR, et al. Am J Clin Nutr 2022 meta-analysis (prebiotics in IBS/functional bowel disorders): no symptom benefit; inulin-type worsens flatulence.
- **Effect size (CI):** No significant difference vs placebo in abdominal pain, bloating, or flatulence overall; flatulence improved only at ≤6 g/day non-inulin types, while inulin-type fructans WORSENED flatulence (So D, et al. AJCN 2022).
- **Clears MCID?** No — null for the symptoms patients care about.
- **Dose — trials:** Varies; ≤6 g/day better tolerated, >6 g inulin-type worsens symptoms · **retail (active):** Inulin/FOS ~3-5 g per serving (users often escalate, worsening gas)
- **Population studied:** Adults with IBS and functional bowel disorders. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Increases bifidobacteria in healthy adults (biomarker), but no demonstrated symptom/health outcome; can cause gas.
- **Funding split:** Not the deciding issue — the pooled null is from an independent academic meta-analysis. · **Outlier leverage:** No — the null and the dose-response harm signal are consistent.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None; tolerability (gas/bloating) is the main issue.
- **Conditional on:** Only as a bifidogenic biomarker shift, not for symptom relief; keep dose ≤6 g/day if used at all.
- **Primary sources:** So D, et al. Am J Clin Nutr 2022 (prebiotics in IBS meta-analysis); Wilson B, et al. Am J Gastroenterol 2019 (prebiotic systematic review); Huaman JW, et al. Gastroenterology 2018 (low-FODMAP vs prebiotic)

### Synbiotics (probiotic + prebiotic combinations) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. Combining a probiotic with a prebiotic does not rescue either: network meta-analyses find no significant benefit for IBS symptoms or chronic constipation, with high heterogeneity, few RCTs, and substantial bias.

- **Evidence base:** ~4-7 synbiotic RCTs in the IBS/constipation meta-analyses (vs 30-54 for probiotics). — total n ≈ ~1000 across pooled synbiotic RCTs
- **Largest independent trial:** No large independent trial; network meta-analysis (Nutrients 2024) finds prebiotics/synbiotics non-significant while some single-strain probiotics show a signal.
- **Effect size (CI):** No significant improvement in IBS symptom scores or stool output vs control (2024 network meta-analysis, Nutrients 2024;16:2114; and 2022 chronic-constipation meta-analysis PMID 36372047 — insufficient evidence, high heterogeneity).
- **Clears MCID?** No — pooled effect not significant.
- **Dose — trials:** Product-specific probiotic CFU + prebiotic gram doses; highly variable · **retail (active):** Variable proprietary blends; CFU and prebiotic grams often below any trialed formulation
- **Population studied:** Adults with IBS and chronic constipation; a few RCTs, heterogeneous formulations. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No. No credible healthy-adult outcome benefit.
- **Funding split:** Small, often manufacturer-linked trials; independent pooling is null. · **Outlier leverage:** Too few trials to be robust; any signal is heterogeneous and bias-prone.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None specific; standard probiotic caution in the critically ill/immunocompromised.
- **Conditional on:** None supported; if a specific branded synbiotic has its own positive RCT, judge that product on its own data, not the class.
- **Primary sources:** Nutrients 2024;16:2114 (network meta-analysis; synbiotics not significant); van der Schoot A, et al. 2022, PMID 36372047 (probiotics/synbiotics in chronic constipation; insufficient evidence); Ford AC, et al. Am J Gastroenterol 2014 (prebiotics/probiotics/synbiotics in IBS/CIC)

### Slippery elm / marshmallow root / demulcent 'gut-soothing' herbs [added, in-category] — **1/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. Traditional demulcents with essentially no credible human outcome trials for IBS, reflux, or 'gut lining.' Pure marketing in this context.

- **Evidence base:** No credible standalone RCTs. — total n ≈ Negligible
- **Largest independent trial:** None.
- **Effect size (CI):** No controlled human outcome data isolating slippery elm/marshmallow.
- **Clears MCID?** No — no outcome data to assess.
- **Dose — trials:** Not established · **retail (active):** Variable powders/capsules; no defined active dose
- **Population studied:** None of adequate quality; occasional multi-herb combination products, not the single agent. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No.
- **Funding split:** Not applicable — no evidence base. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None specific; unstandardized botanical.
- **Primary sources:** No credible primary RCTs identified for isolated slippery elm/marshmallow in gut conditions


---

## Muscle / strength

### Creatine monohydrate — **5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Buy. The single best-evidenced sports supplement. Large, mostly independent RCT base shows a real, clinically meaningful add-on to resistance training. Cheap, off-patent, well-tolerated.

- **Evidence base:** >100 RCTs; multiple meta-analyses (Branch 2003; Chilibeck 2017 older adults; Delpino 2022; Wu 2022). — total n ≈ Thousands across meta-analyses (e.g. Chilibeck older-adult meta n>350; Delpino 2022 pooled n>1500).
- **Largest independent trial:** Numerous independent university RCTs (e.g. Candow/Chilibeck aging trials, Volek 1999). No single trial carries the grade.
- **Effect size (CI):** Added to RT vs RT+placebo: lean mass +1.1 kg; upper-body strength ~+4.4 kg, lower-body ~+11 kg (1RM). Branch 2003 pooled ES ~0.24 (95% CI 0.16-0.31) for strength/performance.
- **Clears MCID?** Yes - a ~1 kg LBM and multi-kg 1RM increment over placebo across a training block is a meaningful add-on.
- **Dose — trials:** 3-5 g/day maintenance (optional 20 g/day x5-7 d loading). Most trials 5 g/day. · **retail (active):** 5 g monohydrate per serving - matches trial dose exactly.
- **Population studied:** Healthy young and older adults doing resistance training; both sexes (larger LBM effect in males). Also studied in sarcopenia and clinical wasting. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Yes. Effect demonstrated directly in healthy trained and untrained adults; transfer is not an inference.
- **Funding split:** Not a decisive issue - creatine is generic and much of the base is university/independent; effect persists in non-industry trials. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable - effect is distributed across a large, consistent literature, not driven by one study.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found. No credible renal/hepatic harm at recommended doses in healthy adults; longstanding safety record.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 5; decisive check: Check 3 (effect size vs MCID) — the only real point of attack — held; the confirming pillars were Check 4 (dose match) and Check 5 (population transfer)). I attacked this as the trap-prone checks (2,3,4,5,7) but none broke it. The strongest available attack is Check 3: the pooled effect is genuinely modest (Branch 2003 ES ~0.24; some of the +1.1 kg "lean mass" is intracellular water rather than contractile tissue). But this does not sink the grade — Delpino 2022 LBM MD +1.10 kg (95% CI 0.56-1.65, verified) excludes zero, strength gains are real, and the effect is distributed across >100 RCTs, so there is no outlier leverage (Check 2 fails to break it). Unlike the rubric's cautionary examples, every disqualifying condition is absent: retail 5 g/day matches trial maintenance dose exactly (Check 4, ISSN-confirmed), so retail is not below trial; the benefit was demonstrated directly in healthy young and older adults doing resistance training, not inferred from a deficiency/MCI/disease population (Check 5); creatine is generic and off-patent with a heavily university-funded base, so industry funding is not load-bearing (Check 1, not compromised); dose-response is not adverse — up to 30 g/day for 5 years is well tolerated (Check 6); and there is no TGA/FDA/EMA/MHRA advisory, with EFSA in fact granting a positive muscle-function claim (Check 7). The grade survives at 5; a defensible 4.5 floor exists purely on the modest per-person effect size, but the evidence quality is the best of any sports supplement.
- **Product:** Thorne Thorne Creatine — 5 g creatine monohydrate per scoop (fully active; no elemental discount) active/serving; NSF Certified for Sport. Best-in-class pick: uses Creapure (the branded form used in most cognition/creatine trials) and is NSF Certified for Sport. 5 g/scoop meets the standard chronic cognition dose. Unflavored powder. ASIN varies by listing; search URL is reliable.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Thorne+Creatine+NSF+Certified+for+Sport
- **Product:** Thorne Thorne Creatine (Creapure) — 5 g creatine monohydrate per scoop active/serving; NSF Certified for Sport. Best-in-class pick: branded Creapure creatine monohydrate AND NSF Certified for Sport (strongest anti-adulteration mark). 5 g/scoop fully covers the 3-5 g maintenance/muscle-energy dose and the low end of cognition dosing; sits at the LOW end of the 5-20 g cognition range and below the ~14-18 g single-dose sleep-deprivation protocol (would need 3-4 scoops). Alternative also NSF: Klean Athlete Klean Creatine (also Creapure).  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Thorne+Creatine+Creapure+NSF+Certified+for+Sport
- **Primary sources:** Kreider 2017 ISSN Position Stand, J Int Soc Sports Nutr 14:18 (PMID 28615996); Branch 2003 meta-analysis, Int J Sport Nutr Exerc Metab 13:198 (PMID 12945830); Chilibeck 2017 older-adults meta, Open Access J Sports Med 8:213 (PMID 29138605); Delpino 2022 Clin Nutr 41:551 (PMID 35081505)

### Whey / protein powder — **4.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Buy only if you are not already hitting your protein target from food. It is a convenient food, not a magic compound - the benefit vanishes once total intake reaches ~1.6 g/kg/day.

- **Evidence base:** 49 RCTs (Morton 2018 meta). — total n ≈ 1863 (Morton 2018).
- **Largest independent trial:** Morton 2018 pools many independent university RCTs; no single-study dependence.
- **Effect size (CI):** Morton 2018: +2.49 kg 1RM strength (95% CI 0.64-4.33), +0.30 kg fat-free mass (95% CI 0.09-0.51), +310 um2 fiber CSA. Benefit plateaus above 1.62 g/kg/day.
- **Clears MCID?** Strength effect yes; the +0.30 kg FFM increment is small and only meaningful because it compounds over months.
- **Dose — trials:** Supplemental protein such that total intake reaches ~1.6 g/kg/day (Morton 2018 breakpoint). · **retail (active):** 20-40 g protein per scoop - adequate; not the limiting factor.
- **Population studied:** Healthy adults on prolonged resistance training; effect attenuates with age, larger in already-trained lifters. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Yes, but conditionally - the gain only appears when baseline dietary protein is below the ~1.6 g/kg/day threshold.
- **Funding split:** Not reported as decisive; whey is generic and the effect is modest and consistent regardless of sponsor. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable - large 49-study base.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found (whole-protein foods).
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Adequate total dietary protein (~1.6-2.2 g/kg/day) from ordinary food; the powder is just a cheap delivery vehicle.
- **Conditional on:** Total dietary protein currently below ~1.6 g/kg/day; no incremental FFM benefit above that.
- **Product:** Klean Athlete Klean Athlete Klean Isolate Whey Protein Isolate — 20 g protein per scoop active/serving; NSF Certified for Sport. Protein per scoop is not the limiting factor; total daily intake to ~1.6 g/kg is the target, met by food + as many scoops as needed. NSF Certified for Sport (preferred over generic 'third-party tested'). Same-cert alternatives: Momentous Essential Grass-Fed Whey (20 g/serving), Thorne Whey Protein Isolate (~21 g). Search URL used because ASINs change; confirm the NSF batch on the listing.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Klean+Athlete+Klean+Isolate+whey+protein
- **Primary sources:** Morton 2018 meta-analysis, Br J Sports Med 52:376 (PMID 28698222)

### Caffeine — **4/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Buy as an acute ergogenic. One of the few genuinely proven performance aids - small but real, independently replicated strength/power effect. Cheap, generic, well-characterized.

- **Evidence base:** ~20 RCTs in the primary meta; corroborated by an umbrella meta-of-metas (2024). — total n ≈ Hundreds pooled across strength and power outcomes.
- **Largest independent trial:** Many independent university crossover RCTs; effect is robust to any single study's removal.
- **Effect size (CI):** Grgic 2018 meta: strength Hedges g 0.20, power g 0.17 (both significant). Upper-body strength significant; lower-body less clear. Small effect, large evidence base.
- **Clears MCID?** Modestly - a small acute uplift that is genuine and repeatable, meaningful for competition/hard sessions.
- **Dose — trials:** 3-6 mg/kg ~60 min pre-exercise (jump benefit seen from ~1-2 mg/kg). · **retail (active):** Pre-workouts/tablets 150-300 mg (~2-4 mg/kg for an 80 kg adult) - within the effective range.
- **Population studied:** Trained and untrained adults, acute pre-exercise dosing; strength and power tasks. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Yes - demonstrated directly in healthy adults; effect is acute and reproducible.
- **Funding split:** Not decisive - caffeine is generic and the literature is heavily independent/academic. · **Outlier leverage:** No - consistent small effect across many studies.
- **Regulatory / safety:** General: keep total intake below ~400 mg/day (FDA/EFSA); WADA monitors but does not ban. No muscle-specific advisory.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 4; decisive check: Check 3 (effect size vs MCID) — the small g~0.20 is the only real weakness, and it is precisely why this is a 4 not a 5; combined with Check 4 (retail dose is adequate, not below trial) it survives.). I confirmed the exact effect sizes from the primary source (Grgic 2018, PMID 29527137): strength SMD 0.20 (0.03-0.36), power 0.17 (0.00-0.34), upper-body significant, lower-body not. The effect is genuinely small and some CIs hug zero, but there is no valid outlier or funding attack (caffeine is generic, hundreds of independent academic crossover RCTs, robust to single-study removal), the population is healthy adults studied directly, and — critically — the retail dose (~2-4 mg/kg) sits inside the effective trial range (3-6 mg/kg), so retail is not below trial. The only regulatory signals are a general 400 mg/day cap and pure-powder warnings, with no muscle-specific advisory and no adverse dose-response for the strength endpoint. The score holds: a large, independent, replicable evidence base for a small-but-real acute effect is a textbook 4.
- **Product:** Nutricost Nutricost Caffeine Pills 200 mg — 200 mg per tablet active/serving; Not NSF/Informed Sport certified (GMP/third-party tested per label). 200 mg is within the effective pre-exercise range (dose_adequate=true); a heavier athlete targeting 3-6 mg/kg could take ~1.5-2 tablets. No widely available NSF/Informed Sport standalone caffeine tablet exists - for a certified option use an NSF Certified for Sport pre-workout. Search URL used because ASINs change.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Nutricost+Caffeine+200mg+tablets
- **Product:** Nutricost Nutricost Caffeine + L-Theanine Capsules — ~200 mg L-theanine + ~100 mg caffeine per capsule active/serving; none (label claims GMP / third-party tested). 2:1 theanine:caffeine ratio matching the classic focus-stack literature. Caffeine 100 mg is squarely in the trial range but below the 160 mg high-dose acute arm. No sport certification available for this SKU.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Nutricost+Caffeine+L-Theanine
- **Primary sources:** Grgic 2018 meta, J Int Soc Sports Nutr 15:11 (PMID 29527137); Grgic 2020 meta-of-metas, Sports Med (PMID 32488520)

### Beta-alanine — **3.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Buy only for its real domain: high-intensity efforts lasting ~1-4 minutes (repeated sprints, rowing, CrossFit-style work). It is NOT a maximal-strength or hypertrophy aid - marketing overreaches on that.

- **Evidence base:** ~40 RCTs (Saunders 2017 meta). — total n ≈ >1400 pooled (Saunders 2017).
- **Largest independent trial:** Multiple independent carnosine-loading trials (Hill 2007, Hoffman); no single-study dependence.
- **Effect size (CI):** Saunders 2017 (Br J Sports Med, 40 studies): median ~2.85% performance improvement; pooled effect concentrated in 60-240 s efforts (ES ~0.18, 95% CI ~0.08-0.31). Minimal/absent effect on maximal strength.
- **Clears MCID?** Yes for high-intensity endurance/repeated-effort tasks; no for maximal strength.
- **Dose — trials:** 4-6 g/day for 2-4+ weeks (cumulative loading ~65-180 g needed to raise muscle carnosine). · **retail (active):** 3.2-6.4 g/day standalone is adequate, but in pre-workout blends servings are often ~1.6-3.2 g and split, below the effective daily load. · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Trained and untrained adults in high-intensity/anaerobic tasks; buffering-limited efforts. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Yes for the 1-4 min high-intensity domain; does NOT meaningfully transfer to pure 1RM strength or muscle growth.
- **Funding split:** Not decisive; mechanism (carnosine buffering) is well established across independent labs. · **Outlier leverage:** No - distributed effect across many studies.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found beyond benign paraesthesia (harmless tingling) at bolus doses.
- **Conditional on:** Task is high-intensity and buffering-limited (~1-4 min); irrelevant for powerlifting-style maximal strength.
- **Product:** NOW Sports (NOW Foods) NOW Sports Beta-Alanine Pure Powder (CarnoSyn) — 2 g per serving (1/4 level teaspoon) active/serving; Not NSF/Informed Sport certified (NOW GMP/self-tested); uses clinically-studied CarnoSyn form — ⚠ per-serving dose BELOW trial dose. Uses branded clinically-studied CarnoSyn beta-alanine (the trial form). One 2 g serving is below the 4-6 g/day effective load, so dose_adequate=false: use 2-3 servings/day (splitting reduces paresthesia). No widely available NSF/Informed Sport standalone beta-alanine exists, so the certification preference could not be met. Search URL used because ASINs change.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=NOW+Sports+Beta-Alanine+CarnoSyn+powder
- **Primary sources:** Saunders 2017 meta, Br J Sports Med 51:658 (PMID 27797728); Hill 2007 Amino Acids 32:225 (PMID 16868650)

### Essential amino acids (EAA) — **3.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Conditional. Genuinely stimulates muscle protein synthesis, but offers no proven hypertrophy advantage over cheaper whole protein for a healthy adult eating enough. Useful mainly in older adults or when protein intake is low.

- **Evidence base:** Many acute mechanistic trials; few chronic hypertrophy RCTs. — total n ≈ Small per chronic trial (typically n<40).
- **Largest independent trial:** Acute crossover MPS trials (e.g. leucine-enriched EAA, n~20-30); no large chronic independent hypertrophy RCT.
- **Effect size (CI):** Robust acute MPS elevation; chronic hypertrophy RCTs are few and largely redundant with adequate protein. No pooled long-term effect size that beats whole protein.
- **Clears MCID?** na - acute signalling endpoint, not a clinical outcome; long-term outcome data insufficient to judge MCID.
- **Dose — trials:** ~10-15 g EAA with ~2-3 g leucine per dose. · **retail (active):** ~5-10 g EAA per serving - often at or slightly below the leucine-threshold dose.
- **Population studied:** Acute MPS studies in young and older adults; chronic hypertrophy data thinner and often in older/low-protein populations. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Partially - acute MPS response is real in healthy adults, but chronic muscle-mass advantage over whole protein is not established.
- **Funding split:** Not formally split; mechanistic literature is largely academic. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Adequate whole protein achieves the same MPS at lower cost.
- **Conditional on:** Low baseline protein intake or older/anabolic-resistant adults.
- **Product:** Thorne Thorne Amino Complex — ~7.8 g amino acid blend per scoop; leucine only ~1-2 g per scoop active/serving; NSF Certified for Sport — ⚠ per-serving dose BELOW trial dose. Chosen because NSF Certified for Sport (preferred over 'third-party tested'). One scoop sits at/below the ~2-3 g leucine threshold and below the 10-15 g EAA target, so dose_adequate=false: take ~2 scoops per dose. Exact leucine per scoop not clearly labeled - verify on product page. Search URL used because ASINs change.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Thorne+Amino+Complex
- **Primary sources:** Wolfe 2017 review, J Int Soc Sports Nutr 14:30 (PMID 28852372); Jackman 2017 Front Physiol 8:390 (PMID 28638350)

### Vitamin D (strength in deficiency) — **3/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Conditional buy - correct a measured deficiency, then stop. Real strength/fall benefit only in deficient people; null in the already-replete, and high doses actively harm. This is a repletion story, not an ergogenic one.

- **Evidence base:** Dozens of RCTs; multiple metas. — total n ≈ Thousands across strength/falls metas.
- **Largest independent trial:** Burt 2019 (Calgary, JAMA 322:736, n=311) - independent: 4000 and 10000 IU/day LOWERED radial/tibial bone density vs 400 IU, demonstrating the wrong-direction dose-response and repletion harm.
- **Effect size (CI):** Deficient (<25 nmol/L): large effect on hip/proximal strength; athletes <75 nmol/L: small strength effect (2023 Frontiers meta, 11 RCTs, n=436). Replete: no grip-strength effect. Falls reduced by active analogues in elderly.
- **Clears MCID?** Yes in deficiency (proximal weakness reversal, fall reduction); no in repletion.
- **Dose — trials:** Repletion dosing ~1000-2000 IU/day (or equivalent) to restore normal 25(OH)D. · **retail (active):** 1000-2000 IU adequate; 5000-10000 IU products are unnecessary and enter the harm range.
- **Population studied:** Deficient/older adults (25(OH)D <25-50 nmol/L) and athletes with low baseline; distinct from replete healthy adults. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No - benefit does NOT transfer to a vitamin-D-replete healthy adult; grip strength unaffected when 25(OH)D >25 nmol/L.
- **Funding split:** Not a decisive industry issue - largely publicly funded. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable to the deficiency-repletion signal.
- **Regulatory / safety:** High-dose harm signal: Burt 2019 JAMA bone-density decline; hypercalcaemia/toxicity above ~4000 IU/day chronic. Supports capping, not maximizing, the dose.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** For a replete adult: nothing needed - sensible sun exposure/diet maintains status; extra vitamin D adds no strength.
- **Conditional on:** Measured 25(OH)D below ~50 nmol/L (deficient/insufficient). Test before supplementing; do not exceed ~2000-4000 IU/day.
- **Product:** Klean Athlete Klean Athlete Klean D3 — 2000 IU (50 mcg) per softgel active/serving; NSF Certified for Sport. NSF Certified for Sport (preferred). 2000 IU is within repletion range and avoids the unnecessary/harm-range 5000-10000 IU products. Same-cert lower-dose alternative: Thorne Vitamin D-1000 (1000 IU). Search URL used because ASINs change; confirm the 2000 IU SKU.  
  https://www.amazon.com/s?k=Klean+Athlete+Klean+D3+2000
- **Primary sources:** Burt 2019 JAMA 322:736 (PMID 31454046); 2023 athletes meta, Front Nutr 10:1163313 (PMID 37841405); Beaudart 2014 muscle-strength meta, J Clin Endocrinol Metab (PMID 25162434)

### Betaine anhydrous — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *low*

**Verdict.** Skip pending better data. A borderline-significant strength signal exists but rests on small trials with a confidence interval nearly touching zero, and there is no body-composition benefit.

- **Evidence base:** ~6-10 small RCTs. — total n ≈ Low hundreds pooled; individual trials n~20-30.
- **Largest independent trial:** No decisive large independent RCT; base is small early-phase studies.
- **Effect size (CI):** Pooled maximal strength SMD 0.47 (95% CI 0.04-0.89); lower-body SMD 0.49 (95% CI 0.01-0.98) - both lower bounds essentially at zero. No effect on upper-body strength, power, or endurance. Ashtary-Larky 2022: no body-composition effect.
- **Clears MCID?** Borderline - point estimate is moderate but the near-zero CI bound means the true effect may be trivial.
- **Dose — trials:** 2.5 g/day for 1+ weeks. · **retail (active):** ~2.5 g/day - matches trial dose.
- **Population studied:** Young resistance-trained men, short (1-6 week) trials. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Uncertain - the studied population is healthy trainees, but the effect is too fragile to bank on.
- **Funding split:** Not formally split, but betaine is manufactured by ingredient suppliers (e.g. DuPont/Danisco) and several trials are industry-adjacent - contributes to the low confidence. · **Outlier leverage:** Yes - with CI lower bounds at 0.01-0.04, removing one small positive trial would likely null the result. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found for muscle dosing.
- **Primary sources:** Betaine strength meta-analysis (Gao/Cholewa lineage); Ashtary-Larky 2022 body-composition meta, Br J Nutr (PMID via Cambridge Core, failed body-comp)

### Citrulline malate — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Borderline / skip. The vasodilator pump is real, but the performance benefit is small, inconsistent, and the meta-analysis authors themselves declined to recommend it. Effect sits near the null.

- **Evidence base:** 8-12 small RCTs. — total n ≈ ~137 (Varvik 2021).
- **Largest independent trial:** Varvik 2021 pooled analysis is the best synthesis; no single large independent confirmatory trial exists.
- **Effect size (CI):** Vialpando/Varvik 2021 meta (8 studies, n=137): +6.4% reps to failure, SMD 0.196 (small); subgroups non-significant and authors concluded evidence does not support a recommendation. Trujillo-Colmena/three-level metas confirm SMD ~0.2 (95% CI ~0.01-0.39), fragile.
- **Clears MCID?** No - a fraction of a rep advantage that does not reliably exceed noise.
- **Dose — trials:** 6-8 g citrulline malate (~3.5-4.5 g L-citrulline), 40-60 min pre-exercise. · **retail (active):** 6-8 g standalone is on-dose, but many pre-workout blends contain only 1-3 g - below trial dose. · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Mostly young resistance-trained men, acute pre-exercise dosing. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Weakly - even in the studied healthy trained population the effect is small and unreliable.
- **Funding split:** Several trials industry-adjacent; base too small to split cleanly. · **Outlier leverage:** Yes - pooled significance is fragile and hinges on a few small positive studies; CI lower bound near zero.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found.
- **Primary sources:** Varvik 2021 meta, Int J Sport Nutr Exerc Metab 31:350 (PMID 34010809); Trujillo-Colmena 2025 three-level meta, Nutrients 18:1881

### Ashwagandha (Withania somnifera) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for muscle/strength. The headline strength gains come from extract-manufacturer-funded trials with implausibly large effects and no independent replication - and multiple regulators have now flagged liver, thyroid and reproductive safety.

- **Evidence base:** A few small single-site RCTs, predominantly manufacturer-funded. — total n ≈ Low (Wankhede n=57; others similar).
- **Largest independent trial:** No adequately powered independent strength RCT exists; the evidence base is sponsor-dependent.
- **Effect size (CI):** Wankhede 2015: bench-press 1RM +46 kg vs +26 kg placebo and large testosterone/muscle-size gains - effect sizes implausibly large for 8 weeks and unreplicated independently.
- **Clears MCID?** Nominally yes in the sponsor trial, but the result is not trustworthy enough to credit.
- **Dose — trials:** 300 mg root extract twice daily (KSM-66), or ~600 mg/day standardized extract. · **retail (active):** 600 mg KSM-66 - matches trial dose (dose is not the issue; credibility and safety are).
- **Population studied:** Young men with little resistance-training experience (Wankhede 2015, n=57); small single-site trials. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Not credibly - the untrained-novice gains are confounded by learning effects and sponsor bias; no robust healthy-adult replication.
- **Funding split:** Decisive. Positive strength trials trace to KSM-66 maker Ixoreal Biomed / extract suppliers; independent replication absent. This is why the grade is capped at 2. · **Outlier leverage:** Yes - the category's strength claim rests almost entirely on the Wankhede 2015 outlier. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** Substantial. Denmark banned ashwagandha in food/supplements (2023, liver/thyroid/reproductive concerns); Iceland issued a liver-injury warning (2023); EU Heads of Food Safety Agencies prioritized it for an Article 8 procedure (June 2024); Bjornsson 2020 Iceland/US DILIN case series and NIH LiverTox document hepatotoxicity with 2-12 week latency.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: Check 1 — funding-source independence: every strength trial traces to the KSM-66 maker (Ixoreal) or industry-tied authors; no adequately powered independent replication exists.). Verified against primary sources: Wankhede 2015 (n=57, untrained men, extract supplied by Ixoreal BioMed) shows large bench-press and testosterone gains that clear MCID, so the effect size is not the weakness — credibility is. A real pooled signal does exist beyond a single outlier (Bonilla 2021 meta-analysis, strength subgroup dunb 0.68, 95% CI 0.40-0.95 across 5 trials), which is why the grade cannot drop to 1; but that meta-analysis runs no funding subgroup, its authors carry industry COI, and its constituent trials are almost all Ixoreal/extract-supplier linked with zero independent replication, which is why it cannot rise to 3. LiverTox (Likelihood B, ~23 cases, 2-12 week latency, thyrotoxicosis case) plus the Denmark ban, Iceland warning and EU Article 8 procedure confirm a genuine multi-regulator safety overlay. The retail dose (600 mg KSM-66) matches the trial dose, so dose is not the issue; the industry-concentration and safety flags are, and the score of 2 holds.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Progressive resistance training plus adequate protein and sleep - the actual drivers of the outcomes attributed to ashwagandha.
- **Primary sources:** Wankhede 2015, J Int Soc Sports Nutr 12:43 (PMID 26609282) - industry-funded; Bjornsson 2020 liver-injury case series, Liver Int (PMID 31879990); NIH LiverTox NBK548536; EU HoA Article 8 prioritization 2024; Danish/Icelandic advisories 2023

### HMB (beta-hydroxy-beta-methylbutyrate) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for hypertrophy/strength in healthy trained adults. The large effects come from manufacturer-linked studies; independent replications in young trainees are null. A modest signal survives only in older/sarcopenic and clinical-wasting populations.

- **Evidence base:** Multiple RCTs and metas, but split sharply by sponsor and population. — total n ≈ Hundreds pooled; individual young-adult trials small.
- **Largest independent trial:** Jakubowski 2019 (J Appl Physiol / MSSE-adjacent, resistance-trained men, no benefit over placebo) and Teixeira/Sanchez-Martinez independent work - null.
- **Effect size (CI):** Sanchez-Martinez 2018 and Jakubowski 2019: no significant body-composition or strength effect in young/trained. The oversized Wilson/Lowery 2014 HMB-FA result (large FFM/strength gains) is industry-linked and widely regarded as implausible. Older-adult metas show small, heterogeneous effects.
- **Clears MCID?** No in healthy young adults; marginal at best in sarcopenia.
- **Dose — trials:** 3 g/day (as Ca-HMB or HMB-free acid). · **retail (active):** 3 g/day - matches trial dose; underdosing is not the excuse.
- **Population studied:** Young trained/untrained (null in independent work) vs older adults and sarcopenia/clinical wasting (small positive signal). · **transfers to healthy adult?** No - benefit does not transfer to healthy trained adults; the meaningful signal is confined to catabolic/elderly populations.
- **Funding split:** Decisive. Early positive trials trace to Metabolic Technologies / patent holders (Nissen et al.); independent replications are null. This split is why the grade is 2, not higher. · **Outlier leverage:** Yes - historical pooled optimism leaned on the Wilson/Lowery 2014 outlier and Nissen-group studies. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2; decisive check: Population transfer (with funding split a close second)). I tried to break the 2 in both directions and it held. Downward to 1: rejected, because a genuine independent-leaning signal survives in sarcopenic/elderly and clinical-wasting populations (Oktaviana 2019: ~0.35 kg lean mass, 95% CI 0.11-0.59 over ~6 mo), so it is not pure marketing. Upward to 2.5+: rejected, because the marketed population (healthy young/trained) shows a robust NULL in independent work - Jakubowski 2020 (PMID 32456217) found HMB does not improve FFM, strength, or fat mass despite a trivial total-body-mass blip, and the 2026 Age & Ageing meta calls even the older-adult adjunctive benefit "unclear." The decisive lever is population transfer: benefit does not carry from catabolic/elderly to healthy trained adults, and the historical optimism that inflated early scores traces to the Metabolic Technologies/Nissen patent-holder group and the implausible Wilson/Lowery 2014 HMB-FA outlier (is_compromised = true). Retail 3 g/day matches trial dose, so underdosing is no excuse (retail_below_trial = false); the elderly effect also sits below a meaningful MCID for lean mass. No regulatory or adverse dose-response signal exists. Score 2 is correct.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Adequate protein + resistance training deliver the leucine-metabolite effect without the manufacturer-funded premium.
- **Conditional on:** Any residual benefit limited to sarcopenic/elderly or clinical catabolic states, not healthy lifters.
- **Primary sources:** Sanchez-Martinez 2018 meta, J Sci Med Sport 21:727 (PMID 29249685); Jakubowski 2019 (independent RCT, resistance-trained); Wilson/Lowery 2014 Eur J Appl Physiol 114:1217 (industry-linked outlier, PMID 24599749)

### BCAAs (branched-chain amino acids) — **1.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. Mechanism outruns outcome, and it is actively inferior to whole protein or EAA. Isolated BCAAs cannot sustain muscle protein synthesis because the other essential amino acids are missing.

- **Evidence base:** A handful of acute mechanistic trials; no credible long-term hypertrophy superiority RCT. — total n ≈ Small (acute crossover n~8-15).
- **Largest independent trial:** Jackman 2017 (n=10 crossover) - the definitive mechanistic study, independent, showing sub-maximal response.
- **Effect size (CI):** Jackman 2017: BCAA-alone raised post-exercise MPS ~22% but that is ~50% lower than whole-protein/EAA. IV BCAA trials showed DECREASED muscle protein turnover. No hypertrophy outcome advantage.
- **Clears MCID?** No - fails to produce a sustained anabolic outcome; the acute bump is a null for real hypertrophy endpoints.
- **Dose — trials:** ~5-10 g BCAA in acute trials. · **retail (active):** 5-7 g per serving (2:1:1) - dose is not the problem; the missing EAAs are.
- **Population studied:** Acute MPS studies in young men; a few soreness/recovery trials. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No net benefit that transfers - the anabolic response is transient and sub-maximal versus complete protein.
- **Funding split:** Not the issue - the mechanistic verdict is negative even in positive-leaning trials. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable.
- **Regulatory / safety:** None found.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Whole protein or complete EAA - both dominate BCAAs for the same purpose.
- **Primary sources:** Wolfe 2017, J Int Soc Sports Nutr 14:30 (PMID 28852372); Jackman 2017, Front Physiol 8:390 (PMID 28638350)

### Testosterone boosters (Tribulus, D-aspartic acid, fenugreek blends) — **1/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. Marketing. No credible human data showing raised testosterone or strength/hypertrophy in eugonadal men; the best-controlled trials are null. Included because it dominates this category's shelf space.

- **Evidence base:** A handful of small RCTs, predominantly null. — total n ≈ Small (typically n<30 per trial).
- **Largest independent trial:** Melville 2015 (independent, D-aspartic acid, n=24) - no benefit, higher dose counterproductive.
- **Effect size (CI):** D-aspartic acid: Melville 2015 RCT (n=24) - no rise in testosterone, 6 g/day trended DOWN. Tribulus: Rogerson 2007 and others - no testosterone or performance effect. Pooled: null.
- **Clears MCID?** No - no effect to clear it.
- **Dose — trials:** Varies (e.g. D-aspartic acid 3-6 g/day; Tribulus ~750 mg/day). · **retail (active):** Proprietary blends, frequently underdosed and undisclosed.
- **Population studied:** Mostly young men; a few small RCTs, largely null. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No benefit to transfer - null in the healthy men studied.
- **Funding split:** Positive noise is industry/anecdotal; controlled independent trials are null. · **Outlier leverage:** Not applicable - no robust positive pool to leverage.
- **Regulatory / safety:** Sporadic contamination/adulteration warnings for proprietary 'test booster' blends (undeclared steroids/DMAA-type stimulants) from FDA.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Resistance training, sleep, adequate energy/protein, and body-fat management - the only levers that move testosterone and strength in healthy men.
- **Primary sources:** Melville 2015, J Int Soc Sports Nutr 12:15 (PMID 25844073); Rogerson 2007 Tribulus RCT, J Strength Cond Res 21:348 (PMID 17530942)
