## Longevity (NAD & senolytics)

### GlyNAC (glycine + N-acetylcysteine) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Conditional, do not buy off-the-shelf. Real, sizeable improvements in glutathione, oxidative stress, strength and cognition - but from small (n=24) trials by a single conflicted group (Sekhar/Baylor), never independently replicated, in a glutathione-deficient older population. Promising within its niche, not proven for healthy adults.

- **Evidence base:** 1 pilot + 1 small RCT (single research group) — total n ≈ 24 older adults randomised (+12 young reference) in the 2022 RCT; pilot ~24
- **Largest independent trial:** None independent - all from Sekhar's Baylor group, which holds related IP/conflicts.
- **Effect size (CI):** Kumar et al. 2022, J Gerontol A / Kumar 2021 pilot: GlyNAC corrected glutathione deficiency, lowered oxidative stress, improved mitochondrial function, gait speed, muscle strength, cognition and insulin resistance vs alanine placebo over 16 wks. Effect sizes reported as large, but n is very small and CIs wide.
- **Clears MCID?** plausibly yes for glutathione/oxidative markers; functional gains promising but underpowered and unreplicated.
- **Dose — trials:** Glycine + NAC dosed to body weight (glycine ~100 mg/kg/day; NAC ~100 mg/kg/day) x16 weeks - large gram-level daily doses · **retail (active):** Retail GlyNAC blends commonly deliver well below the weight-based grams used by Sekhar (typical products underdose) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Older adults (61-80y) with glutathione deficiency/oxidative stress; a HIV-cohort variant. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Uncertain - benefit is demonstrated in glutathione-DEFICIENT elders; repletion logic means healthy, non-deficient adults may gain little.
- **Funding split:** Single-lab, conflict-of-interest concern; no independent replication - the favourable grade depends on this unreplicated body of work. · **Outlier leverage:** With n=24, results are fragile; a few participants drive the effect. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** NAC itself had an FDA supplement-status dispute (FDA later exercised enforcement discretion, 2022). No efficacy advisory. High-dose NAC generally well tolerated.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2.5; decisive check: Population transfer (check 5), reinforced by check 1: the independent industry-funded Nestle RCT (Lizzo et al. 2022, n=114 healthy older adults, 14 days) was NULL on its primary glutathione endpoint in the overall healthy cohort, with benefit confined to a ~27.5% subset having high oxidative stress AND low baseline glutathione. The mechanism does not fire in non-deficient adults.). I tried to break 2.5 in both directions and it held. The grade's claim of "no independent replication" is actually wrong: an independent Nestle RCT (n=114) exists and FAILED its primary endpoint in healthy older adults, which strengthens rather than weakens the skeptical read (population non-transfer to the retail buyer). Every functional/longevity outcome (gait +20% p=0.032, grip p=0.004, HOMA-IR -64% p<0.001) comes from a single Sekhar/Baylor lab at n=11-12 per arm, unreplicated and fragile; these clear MCID if real, so MCID is not the weak point, but the concentration in one group plus the independent null keeps it below 3. It stays above 2 only because the glutathione-repletion biomarker signal is real and independently reproduced in the deficient subgroup, and trial safety is good. Retail products (~1-2 g) are far below the ~14 g/day trial dose (100 mg/kg each), and no regulator advisory or adverse dose-response signal alters the picture.
- **Conditional on:** Documented glutathione deficiency / elevated oxidative stress in older adults.
- **Primary sources:** Kumar et al. 2022, J Gerontol A Biol Sci Med Sci, PMID 35975308 (RCT, n=24 older adults); Kumar et al. 2021 pilot, PMID 33783984; FDA final guidance on NAC enforcement discretion (2022)

### NMN (nicotinamide mononucleotide) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for healthspan/longevity. NMN reliably raises blood NAD+, but that is a biomarker, not an outcome. The largest, most rigorous syntheses show physical-function and metabolic benefits are non-significant; the scattered positive trials are small, short, and largely industry- or single-region (China/Japan) sourced.

- **Evidence base:** ~10-12 RCTs (n~437-513 pooled) — total n ≈ ~500 across RCTs
- **Largest independent trial:** Igarashi et al. 2024 (250 mg, older adults, ~n80) among larger; independent adequately powered outcome trials remain scarce.
- **Effect size (CI):** NAD+ rises robustly (SMD large, p<0.001). Physical performance: pooled effect non-significant. Igarashi 2024 (250 mg, older adults) shorter 4 m gait; Yi 2022 improved 6-min walk. Glucose/lipid meta-analysis (12 RCTs, n=513): NAD up, but HbA1c, fasting glucose, lipids not significantly changed vs placebo.
- **Clears MCID?** no - performance changes are small and inconsistent; pooled physical-performance effect does not reach significance, let alone MCID.
- **Dose — trials:** 150-1200 mg/day (most 250-900 mg) · **retail (active):** 250-1000 mg/day (retail matches trial range)
- **Population studied:** Healthy middle-aged (avg ~58y) and older adults; several trials in amateur athletes and Asian cohorts. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Weak. Signals appear in older/at-risk or athlete cohorts; no durable functional benefit demonstrated in healthy adults.
- **Funding split:** No formal funding-subgroup meta-analysis exists; the more positive trials cluster among manufacturer-linked / single-region groups. Independent large-outcome data are thin. · **Outlier leverage:** Positive pooled signals lean on a few small trials; removing them collapses the effect toward null.
- **Regulatory / safety:** FDA drug-preclusion saga: FDA told marketers in 2022 NMN is excluded from the dietary-supplement definition (prior drug-investigation clause); after NPA litigation FDA reversed and declared NMN lawful in supplements (2025). Legal status, not a safety advisory. No TGA/EMA safety signal.
- **Conditional on:** Any plausible benefit is limited to older adults with declining function, not healthy midlife users.
- **Primary sources:** PMID 36482258 (efficacy/safety RCT, healthy middle-aged, Sci Rep 2023); Cureus 2024 systematic review PMC11365583 (10 RCTs, physical performance non-significant); Crit Rev Food Sci Nutr 2024, PMID 39116016 (glucose/lipid meta-analysis, 12 RCTs n=513); PMC11557618 (glucose/lipid meta-analysis); Yi et al. 2022 (6-min walk, healthy adults)

### NR (nicotinamide riboside) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for longevity. Best-characterised NAD precursor with genuine independent RCTs and a clean safety record - but the flagship blood-pressure/vascular endpoints were non-significant in the primary analyses. NAD+ goes up; hard outcomes do not.

- **Evidence base:** Multiple RCTs incl. independent (Martens 2018; NCT03821623; NR-SAFE 2023) — total n ≈ Dozens per trial (Martens n=24 crossover; NCT03821623 larger)
- **Largest independent trial:** Martens et al. 2018, Nat Commun (n=24 crossover, healthy 55-79y): NAD+ up, BP/stiffness endpoints exploratory and non-significant.
- **Effect size (CI):** Martens 2018 (Nat Commun, independent, U. Colorado): 1000 mg/d x6 wk raised NAD+ ~60%; systolic BP fell ~9 mmHg in the elevated-BP subgroup but NOT statistically significant in the full cohort. Follow-up NCT03821623 (Frontiers Cardiovasc Med 2022) found NR did not significantly reduce systolic BP or aortic stiffness (primary endpoints null).
- **Clears MCID?** no - the ~9 mmHg BP signal would be clinically meaningful IF real, but it did not reach significance; confirmatory trial was null.
- **Dose — trials:** 500-1000 mg/day (safety up to 3000 mg/day) · **retail (active):** 250-500 mg/day (typically below the 1000 mg trial dose) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Healthy midlife/older adults (Martens, Boulder); Parkinson's (NR-SAFE); overweight adults; hypertensive older adults. · **transfers to healthy adult?** NAD+ elevation transfers to healthy adults; functional/vascular benefit does not.
- **Funding split:** Much NR work is ChromaDex (Niagen)-funded; the key BP trials (Seals/Martens lab) are comparatively independent and were essentially null - so the null is not a funding artifact. · **Outlier leverage:** The apparent BP benefit hinges on one subgroup in one small trial; not replicated.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No regulator advisory. Preclinical caution: a 2023 mouse study reported NR promoted metastasis of aggressive tumours in immunocompromised mice (triple-negative breast); species/immune-status dependent, not established in humans - relevant caution for active-cancer patients.
- **Conditional on:** Any BP interest is confined to people with above-normal systolic BP, not normotensive healthy adults.
- **Primary sources:** Martens et al. 2018, Nat Commun (independent, NAD+ up, BP exploratory); Frontiers Cardiovasc Med 2022, PMID 35620522 / NCT03821623 (BP & stiffness null); NR-SAFE, Nat Commun 2023 (3000 mg safety in Parkinson's); Conze/ChromaDex NIAGEN safety RCT PMC6611812

### Rapamycin / sirolimus (drug - noted) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Not a supplement. The strongest PRECLINICAL geroprotector (reproducible mouse lifespan extension), but the first dedicated healthy-human trial (PEARL) was largely null on primary endpoints, with only modest subgroup improvements and low bioavailability of the compounded drug. Mechanistically the best bet in this list, but human outcome evidence is still thin.

- **Evidence base:** 1 dedicated healthy-aging RCT (PEARL) + small pilots; no hard-outcome longevity RCT — total n ≈ 114 (PEARL)
- **Largest independent trial:** PEARL (AgelessRx/Lifespan.io/academic) - decentralised, largely null primary; independent of a pharma sponsor.
- **Effect size (CI):** PEARL (Zalzala et al. 2024, medRxiv, n=114, 48 wk): safety comparable to placebo; primary healthspan endpoints largely null; women on 10 mg showed improved lean tissue mass and self-reported pain; 5 mg group improved self-reported wellbeing/general health. Visceral adiposity unchanged. Mouse ITP: intermittent/late-life rapamycin reproducibly extends median lifespan ~10-25%.
- **Clears MCID?** no in humans yet - subgroup, self-reported gains; no hard clinical endpoint moved.
- **Dose — trials:** 5 or 10 mg compounded weekly (intermittent); effective ~1.7-3.3 mg/wk after ~66% lower bioavailability · **retail (active):** n/a (prescription drug; 'longevity' dosing 5-6 mg/wk is off-label and below immunosuppressive daily dosing) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Mice (ITP lifespan); healthy adults 50-85 (PEARL); small immune/skin-aging pilots. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Preclinical benefit is robust; human healthy-aging outcome benefit not yet demonstrated.
- **Funding split:** na - not a supplement-industry funding question; the honest read is 'thin human outcomes'. · **Outlier leverage:** Human positives confined to small sex-specific subgroups in one trial.
- **Regulatory / safety:** Approved immunosuppressant (transplant); boxed risks include immunosuppression, impaired wound healing, dyslipidaemia, mouth ulcers. Longevity use is off-label. No regulator endorses anti-aging use.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Rapamycin is a prescription immunosuppressant used off-label; it is not a supplement and should not be self-administered.
- **Conditional on:** Requires physician supervision; dose/schedule (intermittent low-dose) and immune/metabolic monitoring matter.
- **Primary sources:** Zalzala et al. 2024, PEARL Trial, medRxiv 2024.08.21.24312372 (n=114, largely null primary); Harrison et al. 2009, Nature (ITP mouse lifespan extension); Mannick et al. 2014/2018 (rapalog RTB101, immune function in elderly)

### Urolithin A (Mitopure) — **2.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Conditional / mostly do not buy. The only category member where dosed retail matches trial dose, but every RCT is Amazentis-sponsored, and in the population that matters most (older adults 65-90) the headline 6-minute-walk and muscle-ATP endpoints were NOT significant. The '12% strength' result comes from a middle-aged industry trial.

- **Evidence base:** ~2-3 RCTs, all industry-sponsored — total n ≈ ~66 (older-adult trial) to ~88 (middle-aged)
- **Largest independent trial:** None fully independent - Amazentis sponsored/employed authors on all pivotal trials.
- **Effect size (CI):** Middle-aged (Singh/Andreux 2022, Cell Rep Med): muscle strength ~+12% and improved 6-min walk vs placebo. Older adults (Liu et al. 2022, JAMA Netw Open, 65-90y): 6-min walk distance and hand-muscle max ATP were NOT significantly different vs placebo; only muscle endurance and plasma biomarkers (acylcarnitines, CRP) improved.
- **Clears MCID?** borderline - the middle-aged strength/6MWT gains approach clinical relevance, but the older-adult functional primaries missed significance.
- **Dose — trials:** 500-1000 mg/day x4 months · **retail (active):** 500-1000 mg/day (Mitopure matches trial dose exactly)
- **Population studied:** Overweight middle-aged adults (Cell Rep Med 2022); older adults 65-90 (JAMA Netw Open 2022). · **transfers to healthy adult?** Signal is in overweight middle-aged and older cohorts; the clearest functional benefit did NOT replicate in the older-adult trial's primary endpoints.
- **Funding split:** No independent replication exists; every positive result is manufacturer-run. The grade's upside depends entirely on industry-funded data - hence compromised. · **Outlier leverage:** The favourable functional read-out rests on the single middle-aged trial; the older-adult trial's primaries were null. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** US FDA GRAS notice (GRN 791) accepted for Urolithin A; no adverse safety advisory. Well tolerated.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 2.5; decisive check: Effect size vs MCID / outcome quality (Check 3), reinforced by outlier leverage (Check 2): across every RCT the hard functional/strength primaries miss, and only surrogate endurance + biomarker endpoints move.). I tried to break 2.5 in both directions and it held. Downward pressure: the grade's headline "+12% strength / improved 6MWT" comes from SECONDARY endpoints of Singh/Andreux 2022 (Cell Rep Med, n=88) whose actual PRIMARY endpoint, peak power output, was non-significant (~4%); the older-adult trial (Liu 2022, n=66) missed its 6MWT and muscle-ATP primaries; and a newly surfaced trial the grade omitted, Whitfield 2025 (Sports Medicine, n=42, Amazentis/Nestle-funded), also missed its running-performance primary and its VO2max between-group comparison. So functional/strength primaries fail in three separate trials while only endurance/fatigue-resistance and surrogate biomarkers (CRP, acylcarnitines) consistently move — a textbook case of mechanism outrunning outcome, which is exactly what a 2.5 encodes. Upward pressure: I found an apparently independent trial the grade said didn't exist (Zhao 2024, J Int Soc Sports Nutr, n=20, "no conflict of interest reported"), which replicated the endurance signal (MVIC p=0.048, reps-to-failure p=0.011, lower CRP); combined with a perfect retail-to-trial dose match (500-1000 mg, no dose gap), a clean FDA GRAS status with no regulator advisory, and no adverse dose-response direction, this keeps it from dropping to 2.0. These roughly cancel. is_compromised stays true because all pivotal efficacy data are Amazentis-run and the only independent trial is n=20 on a surrogate; retail_below_trial is false (the one genuine point in its favor).
- **Conditional on:** Best (still modest) case is overweight/older adults with low baseline mitochondrial function, not lean healthy adults.
- **Primary sources:** Singh/Andreux et al. 2022, Cell Reports Medicine, PMID 35584623 (middle-aged, +12% strength); Liu et al. 2022, JAMA Netw Open, PMID 35050355 (older adults; 6MWT & ATP non-significant); Andreux et al. 2019, Nat Metab (first-in-human safety/mitophagy biomarkers)

### Fisetin (senolytic) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy yet. Compelling mouse healthspan/lifespan data, but human senolytic outcome data are still pending - the pivotal Mayo AFFIRM frailty RCT has not reported positive outcomes, and no completed human trial shows a clinical benefit. Mechanism far outruns outcome.

- **Evidence base:** Several ongoing/pilot human trials; ~2 senolytic trials with controls completed overall — total n ≈ AFFIRM ~50+ enrolled (not reported); pilots small
- **Largest independent trial:** AFFIRM (Mayo Clinic, FDA-approved, frail elderly women, 20 mg/kg pulsed) - enrolled >50, results not yet reported positive.
- **Effect size (CI):** No completed human RCT with a positive clinical endpoint. Mouse: Yousefzadeh et al. 2018 (EBioMedicine) intermittent fisetin extended median/max lifespan and reduced senescence. 2025 preclinical (PMC12341784) improved physical function and reduced skeletal-muscle senescence in aged mice.
- **Clears MCID?** na - no human outcome effect size to evaluate.
- **Dose — trials:** 20 mg/kg/day x2 days, intermittent pulsed (~1200-1600 mg/dose) · **retail (active):** 100-500 mg/day taken continuously (different regimen and lower per-dose) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Mice (lifespan); humans: frail elderly women (AFFIRM), childhood-cancer survivors, COVID nursing-home residents - mostly ongoing/pilot. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Unknown in humans; even mouse benefit is a senescent-cell-burden model, not established in healthy human aging.
- **Funding split:** Academic (Mayo/Scripps) led; not primarily industry - but simply no outcome data yet. · **Outlier leverage:** na
- **Regulatory / safety:** No regulator advisory; pilots reported no severe adverse events. Long-term safety of chronic dosing unestablished.
- **Conditional on:** Any future indication is likely frailty/senescence-associated disease, not general healthy use.
- **Primary sources:** Yousefzadeh et al. 2018, EBioMedicine (mouse lifespan, senotherapeutic); AFFIRM protocol, Mayo Clinic (frailty RCT, pulsed 20 mg/kg); PMC12341784 2025 (intermittent fisetin, muscle senescence, preclinical); COVID-FIS pilots NCT04537299 / NCT04771611

### Metformin (drug - noted) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Not a supplement, and not evidence-backed for slowing aging in healthy people. The pivotal TAME trial remains unlaunched/unfunded, the longevity case rests on observational diabetic data, and RCTs show metformin BLUNTS exercise-induced fitness and muscle gains in older adults - a real downside for a healthy user.

- **Evidence base:** TAME not launched; MILES and exercise-interaction RCTs small — total n ≈ Konopka 2019 n=53; MASTERS n~94; TAME target 3000 (not enrolled)
- **Largest independent trial:** TAME (Barzilai/AFAR) - designed but stalled/unfunded through 2026; no results.
- **Effect size (CI):** Konopka et al. 2019, Aging Cell (n=53, 62y, 12 wk aerobic training): metformin abrogated exercise-induced gains in mitochondrial respiration and cardiorespiratory fitness/insulin sensitivity. MASTERS 2019 (resistance training): metformin blunted muscle hypertrophy vs placebo. Observational Bannister 2014: diabetics on metformin had lower mortality than matched non-diabetics - hypothesis-generating, confounded.
- **Clears MCID?** no benefit demonstrated in healthy aging; the measurable effect is a negative interaction with exercise.
- **Dose — trials:** 1500-2000 mg/day · **retail (active):** n/a (prescription drug)
- **Population studied:** Type-2 diabetics (observational); older non-diabetic exercisers (mechanistic RCTs). No completed healthy-aging outcome RCT. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No - benefit is inferred from diabetics; in healthy older exercisers the net effect on fitness/hypertrophy is negative.
- **Funding split:** na - no pivotal outcome trial completed. · **Outlier leverage:** Longevity case leans heavily on the Bannister 2014 observational analysis, weakened by later reanalyses.
- **Regulatory / safety:** Approved anti-diabetic; risks include B12 deficiency and (rare) lactic acidosis. FDA recalled specific extended-release lots for NDMA (2020). Prescription only.
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** Metformin is a prescription drug, not a supplement. For a healthy adult the evidence-backed 'anti-aging' intervention is structured aerobic + resistance exercise - which metformin has been shown to blunt.
- **Conditional on:** Established indication is type-2 diabetes / prediabetes, not healthy longevity.
- **Primary sources:** Konopka et al. 2019, Aging Cell 18(1):e12880 (blunts exercise mitochondrial adaptation); MASTERS trial 2019, PMC6826125 (blunts hypertrophy); Bannister et al. 2014, Diabetes Obes Metab (observational mortality); TAME rationale, AFAR / Barzilai (unlaunched as of 2026)

### Resveratrol — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. Two decades and 200+ human studies later, the picture is dominated by poor oral bioavailability and inconsistent, often null clinical outcomes. Modest lipid/inflammatory shifts in type-2 diabetics do not generalise, and resveratrol has been shown to blunt exercise-training adaptations.

- **Evidence base:** Dozens of RCTs; multiple meta-analyses — total n ≈ Thousands pooled across heterogeneous endpoints
- **Largest independent trial:** Multiple; e.g., lipid dose-response meta-analysis PMC9506025 - mixed/modest. No landmark independent trial shows a meaningful healthspan benefit.
- **Effect size (CI):** Lipids: meta-analyses show small reductions in total-C/TG/LDL in some pools, null in others; HDL unchanged. Vascular/endothelial outcomes inconsistent and dose-unrelated. Anthropometric/metabolic outcomes in obesity: no significant clinical improvement. CRP/TNF-alpha modestly reduced in CVD patients; IL-6 unchanged.
- **Clears MCID?** no - where significant, effect sizes are small and clinically marginal; heterogeneity high.
- **Dose — trials:** 150 mg - 1000+ mg/day (some to 5 g) · **retail (active):** 250-500 mg/day (trans-resveratrol)
- **Population studied:** Broad: T2D, obesity/overweight, CVD, cognition, healthy adults. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Poor. Signals concentrate in diseased/dysmetabolic cohorts; may be counterproductive in healthy exercisers (Gliemann 2013: resveratrol blunted training-induced cardiovascular benefit in older men).
- **Funding split:** Not consistently split; heterogeneity and bioavailability are the dominant problems, not a single funding artifact. · **Outlier leverage:** High heterogeneity; several pooled signals are fragile to study exclusion.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No major efficacy advisory; GI intolerance at high doses; potential CYP interaction. Note the exercise-blunting signal as a functional downside.
- **Conditional on:** Any metabolic benefit is limited to type-2 diabetes / dysmetabolic populations.
- **Primary sources:** MDPI IJMS 2024, 25(2):747 (systematic review of resveratrol trials, gaps); Lipid dose-response meta-analysis PMC9506025; Obesity metabolic meta-analysis, ScienceDirect S2667368124000433 (no significant clinical improvement); Gliemann et al. 2013 J Physiol (blunts exercise adaptation); Resveratrol bioavailability meta-analysis (rapid metabolism, low bioavailability)

### Spermidine — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for cognition/longevity. The best RCT (SmartAge) was flatly null on its primary memory and biomarker endpoints. The pro-longevity case rests on observational dietary-intake epidemiology, which cannot establish causation, and trial doses (~0.9 mg/d) may be far below any active dose.

- **Evidence base:** 1 pivotal 12-month RCT (n=100) + smaller pilots — total n ≈ 100 (SmartAge); 89 completed
- **Largest independent trial:** SmartAge, Schwarz et al. 2022 (academic, Germany) - null primary.
- **Effect size (CI):** SmartAge (Schwarz et al. 2022, JAMA Netw Open, n=100, 12 mo): no significant effect on memory (primary) or neuroimaging/inflammatory biomarkers vs placebo. Exploratory hints on verbal memory/inflammation only.
- **Clears MCID?** no - primary endpoint null.
- **Dose — trials:** 0.9 mg spermidine/day (wheat-germ extract) · **retail (active):** 1-10 mg/day (retail often higher than the SmartAge trial dose)
- **Population studied:** Older adults (60-90y) with subjective cognitive decline (SmartAge). · **transfers to healthy adult?** No demonstrated benefit even in the at-risk (SCD) cohort; healthy-adult benefit unsupported.
- **Funding split:** Academic-funded and null, so not a funding artifact. · **Outlier leverage:** na - null result.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No advisory. Generally recognised as safe from dietary sources.
- **Conditional on:** Higher-dose trials are needed before any claim; current retail doses untested for outcomes.
- **Primary sources:** Schwarz et al. 2022, JAMA Netw Open, PMID 35616942 (SmartAge RCT, null primary); SmartAge protocol, Alzheimers Res Ther 2019 PMC6492385

### Sulforaphane (broccoli sprout extract) — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for longevity. An NRF2 activator with interesting but inconsistent autism and chemoprevention biomarker data - and zero human healthspan/longevity outcome trials. Early positive autism results have not held up in larger trials.

- **Evidence base:** Several small RCTs; no longevity RCT — total n ≈ Autism RCTs n~29-44 each; chemoprevention studies small
- **Largest independent trial:** Academic autism RCTs; results mixed and not longevity-relevant.
- **Effect size (CI):** Singh et al. 2014, PNAS (autism, n=44): ABC improved ~34%, SRS ~17% vs minimal placebo change over 18 wks - but subsequent/larger trials were less impressive or failed. Chemoprevention: modulates carcinogen-detoxification biomarkers; no hard clinical-outcome endpoints.
- **Clears MCID?** uncertain - autism signal not robustly replicated; no aging outcome to assess.
- **Dose — trials:** ~50-150 umol sulforaphane/day (broccoli sprout extract) · **retail (active):** Variable and often poorly standardised (glucoraphanin without active myrosinase = low sulforaphane yield)
- **Population studied:** Autism spectrum disorder (young males); cancer-chemoprevention biomarker cohorts; small metabolic studies. No aging cohort. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No longevity/healthspan data in healthy adults; benefit is biomarker-level.
- **Funding split:** Not the key issue; replication and standardisation are. · **Outlier leverage:** Early PNAS autism effect is the outlier positive; larger trials regressed toward null.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No advisory; generally safe as a food-derived compound.
- **Conditional on:** Bioavailable sulforaphane (myrosinase-active preparation) required; most products underdeliver.
- **Primary sources:** Singh et al. 2014, PNAS 111(43):15550 (autism RCT, n=44); NCT02909959 / NCT01474993 (autism RCTs, mixed results)

### Taurine — **2/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy for longevity. The 2023 Science paper is animal lifespan data plus human correlations - no human RCT shows taurine extends healthspan or lifespan. A 2025 analysis directly disputes that taurine even reliably declines with human age. Wait for the interventional trials.

- **Evidence base:** 0 human longevity/healthspan RCTs; small metabolic/BP RCTs exist for other endpoints — total n ≈ Human aging: observational cohorts; no outcome RCT
- **Largest independent trial:** No human longevity RCT exists; the field is awaiting one.
- **Effect size (CI):** Singh et al. 2023, Science: taurine supplementation increased median lifespan ~10-12% in mice and improved healthspan markers in monkeys; human data limited to cross-sectional correlations of lower taurine with age-related disease. Cui et al. 2025 (PMC12507425) present experimental evidence AGAINST a consistent age-related taurine decline in humans.
- **Clears MCID?** na - no human healthspan outcome measured.
- **Dose — trials:** Mouse: ~1 g/kg/day; human aging trials pending (typical human dosing 1.5-6 g/day) · **retail (active):** 500-2000 mg/day (below the allometric mouse-equivalent used to claim benefit) · **retail < trial dose**
- **Population studied:** Mice, monkeys (interventional); humans (observational correlations only for aging). · **transfers to healthy adult?** Not established; the human evidence is correlational and now contested.
- **Funding split:** na - no outcome RCTs to split. · **Outlier leverage:** The human 'decline with age' claim hinges on cohorts that a 2025 reanalysis could not reproduce.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No adverse advisory; taurine is widely consumed and well tolerated. EFSA has reviewed taurine (energy-drink context) without a longevity claim.
- **Conditional on:** A benefit, if any, would require demonstrating true deficiency and repletion effect in humans.
- **Primary sources:** Singh et al. 2023, Science 380(6649), doi:10.1126/science.abn9257 (animal lifespan, human correlations); Editorial, Science 2024, doi:10.1126/science.adl2116 (is taurine a biomarker?); PMC12507425 2025 (evidence against taurine deficiency driving human aging)

### Calcium alpha-ketoglutarate (CaAKG / Rejuvant) — **1.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. The sole human 'longevity' result is an uncontrolled, retrospective, manufacturer study using an unvalidated saliva epigenetic clock, reporting a biologically implausible '8-year' age reversal with no placebo group. This is a marketing artifact, not evidence.

- **Evidence base:** 0 controlled human RCTs (1 uncontrolled retrospective report) — total n ≈ 42
- **Largest independent trial:** None. The only human study is by the product's affiliated group.
- **Effect size (CI):** Demidenko et al. 2021, Aging (US): mean biological-age reduction 8.44 y (men) / 6.98 y (women) on TruAge/TruMe saliva methylation - but no placebo arm, retrospective, self-selected, and the clock is not a validated mortality-predictive clock. Effect is uninterpretable.
- **Clears MCID?** na - no controlled outcome; the metric itself is not clinically validated.
- **Dose — trials:** 2 g/day CaAKG (2x1 g timed-release) + vitamin A (men)/D (women) · **retail (active):** 1-2 g/day CaAKG (matches the reported dose)
- **Population studied:** 42 self-selected Rejuvant users (28 M, 14 F), retrospective, no control group. · **transfers to healthy adult?** Unsupported - no controlled human data of any kind.
- **Funding split:** Entirely manufacturer-affiliated, uncontrolled; the reported 'signal' is a compromised artifact. · **Outlier leverage:** Uncontrolled design means regression-to-baseline and selection effects fully confound the result. ⚠ **grade depends on a funding/outlier artifact**
- **Regulatory / safety:** No advisory; CaAKG is a food-grade ingredient. Mouse healthspan data (Shahmirzadi et al. 2020, Cell Metab) exist but do not transfer.
- **Adversarial re-check:** **survives** scrutiny (revised 1.5; decisive check: Check 1 (funding-source/independence) combined with Check 5 (population transfer): the only human signal is entirely manufacturer-affiliated, uncontrolled, and non-transferable — there is no independent controlled human outcome of any kind.). I tried to break the 1.5 upward by hunting for a real RCT and found the ABLE trial (Geroscience 2023 protocol; Exp Gerontol 2025 recruitment), a legitimate independent double-blind placebo-controlled RCT of 1g/day CaAKG — but it has published no efficacy results yet, so the efficacy base remains zero controlled human outcomes. The sole human study (Demidenko 2021, Aging-US) is confirmed uncontrolled, retrospective, self-selected, manufacturer-affiliated (Ponce de Leon Health / Kennedy), and uses an unvalidated saliva clock, so the '8-year reversal' is an uninterpretable artifact of regression-to-baseline and selection — checks 1, 2, 3, and 5 all fail. The only check that does not hurt it is dosing: retail 1–2g matches/exceeds trial dose, so retail_below_trial is false, and there is no regulatory safety signal. The score does not drop to 1.0 only because a strong mouse lifespan/healthspan paper (Shahmirzadi 2020, Cell Metab) plus adequate dosing lift it a half-point above pure marketing; 1.5 is well calibrated and survives, though a positive ABLE readout could raise it in future.
- **Conditional on:** A registered placebo-controlled RCT (e.g., NCT07114536) is needed before any claim.
- **Primary sources:** Demidenko et al. 2021, Aging (US), PMC8660611 (retrospective, uncontrolled, TruAge); Asadi Shahmirzadi et al. 2020, Cell Metabolism (mouse healthspan); NCT07114536 (registered CaAKG RCT, pending)

### Pterostilbene — **1.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *medium*

**Verdict.** Do not buy. A better-absorbed resveratrol analog with almost no outcome data and a genuine dose-response warning sign: the one substantive RCT showed pterostilbene RAISED LDL cholesterol. No longevity evidence.

- **Evidence base:** ~1-2 substantive RCTs; sparse — total n ≈ 80 (Riche 2014)
- **Largest independent trial:** Riche et al. 2014 (academic, U. Mississippi) - the LDL-raising finding is independent.
- **Effect size (CI):** Riche et al. 2014 (n=80, hypercholesterolaemic): 250 mg/day reduced SBP ~7.8 mmHg and DBP ~7.3 mmHg vs placebo - BUT also raised LDL cholesterol ~+24.9 mg/dL (attenuated by statin or added grape extract). Small BMI reduction in non-medicated subgroup. Later NR+pterostilbene RCTs (n=120, 60-80y) showed increased total cholesterol, more pronounced at higher BMI.
- **Clears MCID?** BP change would be meaningful if isolated, but the concurrent LDL rise is a clinically adverse trade-off; net benefit unproven.
- **Dose — trials:** 125-250 mg/day (monotherapy trial); ~100-250 mg/day in NR combos · **retail (active):** 50-250 mg/day (often paired with NMN/NR)
- **Population studied:** Adults with hypercholesterolaemia (Riche 2014); healthy older adults in NR+pterostilbene combos. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No healthy-aging outcome data; LDL-raising signal is a caution for general use.
- **Funding split:** Not the deciding issue; data are simply sparse. · **Outlier leverage:** Single trial dominates the human evidence base.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No formal advisory; the LDL-raising dose-response is the key safety signal from the trial literature.
- **Conditional on:** Any BP interest would require monitoring LDL; not for people with dyslipidaemia.
- **Primary sources:** Riche et al. 2014, PMID 25057276 (metabolic RCT, n=80; BP down, LDL up); Riche et al. safety analysis PMC3575612; NR+pterostilbene RCT (n=120, cholesterol increase)

### Quercetin (senolytic) — **1.5/5**  ·  confidence in score: *high*

**Verdict.** Do not buy as a senolytic. Every human senolytic result attributed to quercetin used it WITH the prescription leukaemia drug dasatinib (D+Q). Quercetin monotherapy has never been shown to clear senescent cells in humans. As a standalone longevity supplement it is marketing.

- **Evidence base:** 0 quercetin-monotherapy senolytic outcome trials; ~10 BP RCTs for the flavonoid generally — total n ≈ D+Q pilots n=9-14; BP meta-analysis n=841
- **Largest independent trial:** Hickson et al. 2019 (Mayo, D+Q, DKD) - academic, but combination therapy.
- **Effect size (CI):** D+Q (Hickson 2019, EBioMedicine, DKD, n=9): reduced adipose p16INK4a+/p21+ cells and circulating SASP (IL-1a, IL-6, MMP-9/-12) at day 11 - but this is the COMBINATION. BP meta-analysis (10 trials, n=841): significant SBP reduction in mixed/normotensive subgroups, DBP in prehypertensives - modest.
- **Clears MCID?** no for senescence (no monotherapy data); BP effect small and subgroup-dependent.
- **Dose — trials:** Senolytic: 1000 mg quercetin x3 days WITH dasatinib 100 mg. BP: 500-1000 mg/day. · **retail (active):** 500-1000 mg/day quercetin (matches BP-trial dose; but senolytic effect needs dasatinib)
- **Population studied:** Diabetic kidney disease (n=9, open-label D+Q); IPF (D+Q). Never quercetin-alone senolytic trials. · **transfers to healthy adult?** No senolytic transfer as monotherapy. BP effect modest, mainly in (pre)hypertensives.
- **Funding split:** Academic; the issue is confounding by dasatinib, not funding. · **Outlier leverage:** Senolytic signal entirely attributable to co-administered dasatinib.
- **Regulatory / safety:** No advisory for quercetin; high-dose quercetin can inhibit CYP3A4 (drug-interaction risk).
- **The real intervention is not a supplement:** The evidence-backed senolytic is the prescription drug dasatinib PLUS quercetin (D+Q), used under supervision - not quercetin bought off the shelf.
- **Conditional on:** Any senolytic claim requires co-administration with dasatinib under medical supervision.
- **Primary sources:** Hickson et al. 2019, EBioMedicine PMC6796530 (D+Q, DKD, senescent-cell reduction); Justice et al. 2019, EBioMedicine (D+Q, IPF, physical function); Quercetin BP meta-analysis, ScienceDirect S014628062200247X (10 trials, n=841)
